 BluePaper
China's Emerging Frontiers
China's Next Industrial Revolution 
China is entering a new phase of industrial development, moving beyond its historical 
advantages of low-cost and manufacturing scale towards ecosystem and innovation 
leadership. We see profound implications for China's industrial supercycle, manufacturing 
profit migration, and its position in global manufacturing value chains.

 BluePaper
4
China's Next Industrial Revolution 
Industry View
China Industrials | 
Asia Pacific
In-Line
China’s manufacturing story is often 
reduced to overcapacity, low prices, 
margin pressure and 'China+1'. We believe 
the more important story is what comes 
next. China is moving from being the world’s 
factory towards becoming the world’s industrial operating system – 
combining scale, automation, AI, supply-chain depth and global 
deployment. We see this transition as China's Industry 5.0, following 
digitalization and connectivity in Industry 4.0. 
China's Industry 5.0 starts from a powerful position, with ~28% of 
global manufacturing value-added, coverage of all 666 subcatego-
ries as defined by the UN, and a no.1 ranking in over half of global 
export products.  More than 30K smart factories and 100mn con-
nected industrial devices provide the physical and data infrastruc-
ture for the next phase. The key question is  whether China can make 
its industrial system adaptive, sovereign and globally embedded.
#1 Industry 5.0: Built on the pillars of Industrial Intelligence, 
Resilience and Leadership (IRL)
China’s proven strength is industrial deployment; Industry 5.0 aims 
to extend this model upstream. In the next phase, we see five key 
breakthroughs. On intelligence: 1) AI-native adaptive factories, and 2) 
embodied AI that turns physical labor into software-upgradable cap-
ital. On resilience: 3) sovereign and evolvable industrial stacks, and 
4) export of industrial ecosystems, not just products. And on leader-
ship: 5) technological leadership in selective areas – the larger oppor-
tunity is to lead the upstream foundational layers that improve many 
downstream industries at once. 
#2 This is a physical investment supercycle
China's Industry 5.0 will require a major rebuild of the production 
system. We estimate approximately US$12tn, or Rmb80tn, of incre-
mental industrial capex (out of a total cumulative US$50tn of indus-
trial capex) for industrial IRL during 2026-35 in China. Spending will 
flow into: AI, power and digital infrastructure; factory automation, 
robotics, sensors, controls and software; and strategic capacity in 
semiconductors, advanced materials, energy, mobility and other 
frontier industries. We estimate investment will accelerate to a 6-7% 
CAGR over 2028-35, after a slower start of 4-5% over 2025-27 due 
to ongoing anti-involution efforts. 
#3 The expected payoff will be productivity, margins and global 
share
By 2035, we believe China's Industry 5.0 should have delivered three 
major benefits, likely in a J-curve pattern: 
• Industrial margins: Profit margins rise from approximately 5% 
to 8%, as value shifts towards software, equipment, mate-
rials, services and platforms.
• Potential GDP: China’s potential GDP level rises by approxi-
mately 3.5%, with productivity benefits becoming more vis-
ible from the late 2020s.
• Manufacturing share: China’s share of global manufacturing 
value-add increases from ~28% to 30%.
#4 Multipolar global system: 'Made in China' becomes 'Made by 
China'
China+1 and reshoring is the current trend, but we think it will 
struggle to replicate China’s full upstream ecosystem quickly. The 
next phase will increasingly be 'Made by China' in that China supplies 
the components, machinery, production systems, technology, and 
service networks for manufacturing overseas, while embedding  stan-
dards in global industrial ecosystems, as the global system becomes 
more multipolar.


[Page 5]
M
 BluePaper
Morgan Stanley Research
5
#5 China's Industry 5.0 won't be linear
China's path to Industry 5.0 will likely hinge on policy, technology and 
geopolitics: stronger policy support for household demand, faster 
industrial-AI breakthroughs, and stable overseas market access 
could accelerate monetization, productivity and deployment. The 
core risk remains economically inefficient success: premature fiscal 
tightening and supply-centric investment could create 'smart overca-
pacity', deflation and weak returns, while technology bottlenecks, 
prolonged outbound investment controls and trade friction could 
delay adoption and overseas expansion. 
#6 Equity implications: Structural opportunities
Industry 5.0 should create an efficiency and ROE cycle. We expect 
key beneficiary groups will be: 1) Industrial intelligence enablers, 
such as software, automation, controls, sensors and robotics; 2) 
Sovereign bottlenecks: wafer fab equipment (WFE), electronic 
design automation, computer numerical control (CNC) machine 
tools, metrology and advanced materials; 3) Frontier platforms: 
humanoids, autonomous systems, electric vertical take-off and 
landing aircraft (eVTOL), and aerospace; 4) Ecosystem exporters: 
EV-battery-charging, solar-storage-grid, smart factories; 5) Global 
leaders:  autos, electronics, semiconductors, and machinery. 
We identify 45 stocks that are best positioned. What’s priced in: 
Near-term capex broadening out, localization theme. Not priced in: 
Long-term potential of China's transition, ROE recovery from pro-
ductivity, upstream software/equipment profit migration, and global 
TAM potential.

M
 BluePaper
10
Over the past two centuries, every major industrial revolution has 
reshaped the global economic order. Britain led mechanization 
through steam power in the 19th century. The United States domi-
nated mass production and electrification in the 20th century. 
Germany and Japan became synonymous with industrial precision 
and engineering excellence. The digital revolution of the past three 
decades has elevated the US into the world’s dominant software and 
internet power.
Now, with the rise of AI, we believe the next industrial revolution is 
around the corner, and will become the most consequential indus-
trial transition since the rise of electrification and the internet. Unlike 
previous industrial revolutions centered primarily on technological 
progress, China's Industry 5.0 is simultaneously a productivity revo-
lution, a geopolitical response, a manufacturing reorganization, and 
a strategic capital supercycle. 
What is China's Industry 5.0?
Industry 5.0 represents the move into Physical AI following 
Mechanization (1.0), Electrification  (2.0), Automation (3.0) and 
Digitalization (4.0). At its core, we believe China's Industry 5.0 is built 
on three mutually reinforcing  pillars:
Executive Summary 
1. Industrial Intelligence: This is represented by AI-enabled manufac-
turing, robotics, autonomous systems, industrial software and 
embodied intelligence. Industry 4.0 connected factories and made 
production digitally visible; Industry 5.0 seeks to make them adap-
tive. 
2. Industrial Resilience: This is demonstrated by localized, sovereign 
and controllable industrial systems spanning semiconductors, 
energy, aerospace, materials and advanced manufacturing. The 
objective is not produce 100% domestic content. It is to eliminate 
irreplaceable single points of failure, control critical interfaces and 
preserve the ability to redesign around external restrictions. 
3. Industrial Leadership: This means building global ecosystems 
through scale, standards setting, industrial platforms and next-gen-
eration manufacturing capabilities, as well as leading technology 
innovation in selected industries.
Together, the IRL (Intelligent + Resilient + Leading) pillars will be crit-
ical to defining China’s pathway towards next-generation industrial-
ization. These pillars form a flywheel, where Intelligence raises 
productivity and generates data; Resilience secures and improves the 
critical stack; and Leadership expands the installed base overseas, 
creating additional data, profits and commercial feedback for the 
next innovation cycle.
Exhibit 4:
In the past few decades, China has been catching up the world's centuries of economic growth; now  China 
is poised to rise again with Industry 5.0

How will Industry 5.0 transform the 
industrial landscape?
In the next decade, we expect China’s manufacturing leadership 
will evolve from scale and cost towards ecosystem and industrial 
innovation leadership. We identify five transformational trends 
below. 
1. AI at the heart of manufacturing operating systems; automated 
becomes adaptive 
The largest productivity gains from steam and electricity came only 
after factories reorganized around them. We believe AI will follow 
the same pattern, meaning factory and operational platforms will be 
built with artificial intelligence as their core architecture, as AI 
actively coordinates workflows, predicts problems, and makes deci-
sions rather than just acting as an added software tool.  There is much 
early evidence of productivity gains through shorter lead times, 
lower defect rates, higher labor efficiency, and less energy consump-
tion in factories, such as those of Valeo, Midea and CATL. China has 
the world’s largest physical manufacturing base; more than 100mn 
industrial devices are already connected. The strategic opportunity 
is to turn those connected assets into a common sensing, reasoning 
and execution system.
2. Embodied AI: physical labor becomes software-upgradable cap-
ital
Manufacturing will be increasingly conducted by groups of embodied 
AI, including embodied-AI machines and various robots, e.g., 
humanoid, industrial, and collaborative robots, AMR/AGVs, autono-
mous vehicles, and VTols. Physical labor will become software-up-
gradable capital equipment, extending automation from highly 
standardized production into flexible high-mix manufacturing, and 
converting their deployment into data generation. This is not only 
about productivity improvements, but also about offsetting the 
demographic and skills constraints that are gradually becoming 
severe in China. China has a leading edge in manufacturing high 
quality robots at lower cost, an important factor to support deploy-
ment. Based on our forecasts, robotics sales across the above catego-
ries in the Chinese market will rise from approximately 8mn in 2025 
to 29mn in 2030 and 76mn in 2035. 
3. Sovereign industrial stack development: localization gives 
architectural autonomy
China’s largest sovereignty gaps are concentrated upstream, in the 
industrial 'brain, nervous system and precision layer', i.e., advanced 
semiconductors and related equipment/materials, industrial soft-
ware and control systems, high-end CNC, qualified advanced mate-
rials, and foundational processes. These technologies will determine 
whether China can independently design, operate and continually 
upgrade complex industrial systems. We expect these gaps to 
narrow materially over the next decade, because China’s large down-
stream market provides lead customers, qualification opportunities 
and operating feedback, and it is also second globally in R&D 
spending (Rmb3.93tn; absolute amount), although progress will 
likely be uneven. With the breakthrough of foundational layers, we 
also expect the creation of interoperable and evolvable domestic 
technology stacks, i.e., autonomy of the value chain. 
4. Exporting ecosystems: from general product shipments to 
architecture and industry standards
China already exports more manufactured products than any other 
economy. The trend is towards gradually exporting the full system 
around them, such as an EV ecosystem that includes batteries, 
charging or swapping, intelligent-driving software, energy manage-
ment, financing and recycling. A smart-factory ecosystem combines 
robots, controls, production lines, software, commissioning and 
maintenance, areas in which China as a major provider will be able to 
take share. Standards influence will also follow commercial adop-
tion, not precede it, in our view. Global standards require installed 
scale, interoperability, certification, safety acceptance, service sup-
port and an attractive economic proposition for third-party partici-
pants.
5. AI-accelerated industrial discoveries: increasing the lab-to-fab 
pathway
This is the least mature of the five breakthroughs we expect, but 
potentially the most transformative. The breakthrough is a shift from 
sequential, human-led R&D towards a closed-loop, discovery-to-
manufacturing system. We expect China to lead industrial tech-
nology innovation in some areas, especially in foundational layers, 
such as advanced materials, precision equipment, sensors, semicon-
ductor processes and biotechnology, where it has made significant 
investments. This would greatly lift multiple downstream industries 
simultaneously rather than benefit only one product category.


[Page 12]

 BluePaper
12
Why is China uniquely positioned to lead?
Industrial 5.0 is being built on the world's largest and most diver-
sified manufacturing base, which is both a solid industrial founda-
tion and self-reinforcing industrial ecosystem
China is not building Industry 5.0 from scratch: it is based on an indus-
trial system already operating at unmatched scale, with ~28% of 
global manufacturing value-add, and full product category coverage. 
Government policy establishes long-term direction and lowers early 
adoption barriers, and often acts as both a growth accelerator and a 
risk mitigator. Large downstream customers provide qualification 
and operating data; local industry clusters connect equipment, com-
ponents, materials and software; engineers and competing firms 
refine products rapidly; China’s financial system reinforces this pro-
cess through bank credit, policy lending, industrial funds, and local-
government support, which provides relatively stable, long-tenor 
funding for strategic sectors, and allows companies to absorb 
lengthy qualification cycles before commercial returns emerge. Well 
developed and highly efficient infrastructure enables low cost, fast 
product iteration, and accelerates deployment. 
China’s NEV rise provides the clearest evidence. China reached 
approximately 51% of global NEV sales in 2025 after combining cen-
tral policy direction, infrastructure support, local-government com-
petition, complete industrial clusters and intense private-sector 
iteration. China’s battery, motor, power-electronics and charging 
ecosystems then created a reinforcing scale advantage: CATL alone 
accounted for approximately 39% of global EV-battery installations 
in 2025. The Yangtze River Delta’s “four-hour industrial circle", 
existing capabilities in electronics, machinery and power equipment, 
and abundant engineering talent and capital turned an initially poli-
cy-supported market into a commercially competitive global eco-
system. We believe the same execution model, now amplified by AI, 
can be redeployed across adaptive factories, embodied intelligence, 
advanced equipment, industrial software, materials and other fron-
tier industries.
What Will Drive the Change to Industry 5.0? 
A Decade Long  Capex Supercycle – US$12trn of Incremental 
Opportunities
We expect ~Rmb340trn  (or US$50trn) cumulative industrial 
investment in 2026-35, with Industry 5.0 creating ~Rmb80trn (or 
US$12trn) of incremental capex demand: Industrial revolutions 
have always been capex intensive. As AI diffusion raises demand for 
both intangible and physical investment, the global capex supercycle 
should support external demand and facilitate overcapacity absorp-
tion. We thus expect the industrial capex/GDP ratio to rise  from 17.4% 
in 2025 to  ~19.5% by 2035 (slightly higher than its previous peak of 
19.2% in 2011), providing a counterweight to  structurally weaker 
property and traditional infrastructure capex due to aging demo-
graphics and  local-government debt problems. This would bring 
~Rmb80trn (or US$12trn) additional industrial capex over the next 
decade vs. a non-Industry 5.0 trajectory, where the share of industrial 
capex in GDP could slip to ~14.0% given overcapacity, loss of manu-
facturing competitiveness amid an aging population, and weak sup-
port from external demand.

Specifically,  we expect Industry 5.0 to unlock three reinforcing layers 
of investment at once:
• 1. Infrastructure (including AI infrastructure) – US$0.5 tril-
lion: the enabling backbone of Industry 5.0.  
• 2. Factory upgrades – US$5.5 trillion: the core deployment 
layer, and likely the largest bucket.  
• 3. New capacity – US$6 trillion: the strategic overlay that 
extends the cycle (global market-share gains and new fron-
tier industries).
The cycle will likely start slowly, and accelerate as capacity and 
technology constraints ease: Despite the rising global  capex super-
cycle and increasing policy support, constraints from lingering excess 
capacity, curbs on duplicative buildout and advanced-chip accessi-
bility will likely keep near-term capex spending slow, at 4-5%p.a. in 
2026-27 (vs. 5.7%p.a. in 2024-25). That said, with gradual over-ca-
pacity digestion, easing tech bottlenecks with rising semi-localiza-
tion, and a shift of AI deployment towards broader factory adoption 
and then to large-scale commercialization, we estimate industrial 
capex growth will accelerate to 6-7%p.a. from 2028.

How to finance the US$12trn? 
China has a comprehensive financial system for industrial growth. 
Based on our China Financials team's estimates, external funding 
already accounted for roughly 60–70% of annual industrial capex 
during 2020–25. Going forward, the team expects:
• Bank loans to remain the anchor at approximately 47% of 
funding over the next decade, reflecting banks’ broad indus-
trial coverage, supply-chain penetration and capacity to pro-
vide long-tenor financing. This implies around 6.3% annual 
growth in industrial medium- and long-term loans. 
• Corporate retained earnings and own capital should pro-
vide roughly one-quarter to one-third of funds. 
• Fiscal funds (cash subsidies, land support and govern-
ment-led investment) and capital markets (China and Hong 
Kong, split broadly between private-market funding and pub-
lic-market financing through IPOs and follow-on issuance) 
will provide the strategic and risk-bearing layers. Capital mar-
kets will be an increasingly important source.
This financing structure is well suited to a long-duration transforma-
tion: bank credit funds mature equipment and infrastructure, fiscal 
capital helps de-risk strategic bottlenecks, and equity absorbs the 
higher uncertainty of frontier technologies.

What will be the Main Economic Benefits to 
China?
#1. Industrial margin and ROE recovery to higher 
quality profit pools
We expect China’s industrial-enterprise profit margin to recover 
from ~5% in 2025 to ~8% by 2035, on four forces: 1) a rising mix of 
higher-margin technology-intensive manufacturing; 2) anti-involu-
tion policies and industry consolidation that improve utilization and 
pricing discipline; 3) overseas expansion into markets with stronger 
pricing and lifecycle-service economics; and 4) selective participation 
in new 0–1 industries (i.e., innovation from scratch instead of 
adapting existing ideas), where Chinese companies capture innova-
tion economics. 
 But this is unlikely to be uniform margin expansion across manufac-
turing; rather, Industry 5.0 should drive profit-pool migration from 
downstream capacity and assembly towards upstream foundational 
technologies, software, precision equipment, qualified components, 
services and ecosystem platforms. 
ROE to improve 2-3% by 2028: MSCI China ROE has already edged 
up from a trough of roughly 9–10% in mid-2023 to around 11% as of 
May 2026, an early indication that the decade-long erosion may be 
stabilizing. Our China Strategist expects a broader improvement in 
listed-sector ROE. Looking forward, unlike the prior two cycles, we 
believe this round is more about an efficiency-driven revolution, 
which could help lift both major AI-related sectors and overall index 
ROE. We expect  Industrials ROE to improve  2-3% by 2028.

ecosystem buildout globally, more '0-1' innovation,  frontier industries ramping up, and anti-involution 
Source: NBS, Morgan Stanley Research estimates
#2. A boost to potential GDP to reach Beijing's long-
term growth target 
AI and Industry 5.0 should lift China's potential GDP by ~3.5% by 
2035, partially cushioning the demographic drag. We raise poten-
tial growth forecasts by 30bp to 4.3% for 2028-30 and 50bp to 3.9% 
for 2031-35 – enough to meet Beijing's 2020-35 GDP-doubling 
target. The uplift reflects a capex-heavy industrial transformation 
and a 3pp cumulative Total Factor Productivity (TFP) gain from econ-
omy-wide AI diffusion. Near-term impact should stay broadly neu-
tral, as excess capacity and J-curve labor adjustments offset early 
gains, before turning more positive from the late 2020s as deploy-
ment broadens. 
Policy rebalancing to stay calibrated, keeping China in lowflation 
over the medium term: A supply-centric policy framework and cali-
brated rebalancing make decisive demand-led reflation unlikely. We 
expect the GDP deflator to remain weak through 2026-27, then grind 
toward 0.5-1.0% from 2028 as housing stabilizes, investment 
becomes more disciplined, external demand absorbs capacity and 
margin gains gradually spread to wages.

#3 Higher global manufacturing share
At first glance, our call is counter-intuitive: tariffs, China+1 diversifica-
tion and AI-enabled reshoring should weaken China’s manufacturing 
position, while aging and higher labor costs erode its traditional cost 
advantage. Yet we expect China’s share of global manufacturing val-
ue-add to rise from ~28% today to ~30% by 2035. Our logic is three-
fold: Industry 5.0 increases the value of ecosystem scale and 
production data; trade diversification is shifting final assembly faster 
than upstream capacity; and China is moving into the complex, capi-
tal-intensive sectors driving the next global trade cycle:
Manufacturing. Industry 5.0 should compound the advantages of 
China’s installed manufacturing base. Other economies may lead in 
selected technologies, but many must rebuild capital stock and 
supply chains while adopting them; China is upgrading a system 
already operating at scale. 
Upstream supplier. Final assembly can shift relatively quickly; sup-
plier depth, process know-how and capital-equipment capacity 
cannot. Many remain reliant on Chinese intermediate inputs, compo-
nents, machinery and production systems, therefore China can retain 
substantial value-add upstream and become the system-level sup-
plier behind manufacturing growth elsewhere.
Complex production. Future gains should come from complex pro-
duction at scale, not renewed low-cost assembly, in sectors where 
China is adding productivity-enhancing capital, localizing bottle-
necks and moving towards upstream technologies, industrial soft-
ware and ecosystem control. This underpins our 2ppt share-gain call.

Implications for a Multipolar World 
China’s role in global trade is changing form, rather than dimin-
ishing. Tariffs, technology controls and China+1 strategies have 
reduced direct US–China trade, but the evidence points more to 
trade rerouting and selective final-assembly relocation than whole-
sale migration of productive capacity. We estimate that only around 
40% of China-to-US exports can be readily substituted: supplier 
depth, process know-how and capital-equipment ecosystems are 
considerably harder to replicate than assembly lines. Indeed, while 
China’s share of US imports has fallen from 21.6% in 2017 to around 
8%, its export share in the rest of the world –which accounts for 
roughly 90% of Chinese exports – has increased from 12% to 16%. 
The next global trade cycle should reinforce China’s upstream role. 
Demand is shifting toward complex capital goods and production 
systems, where scale, supplier coordination and cumulative engi-
neering experience matter more than low-cost assembly. Reshoring 
also raises import demand in its early phase, as countries must 
acquire the industrial inputs needed to build domestic capacity 
before local supply networks can mature. This points to a reconfig-
ured form of globalization: final production becomes more geo-
graphically dispersed, while Chinese capabilities remain embedded in 
industrial expansion overseas. In other words, it marks a shift from 
“made in China” toward a broader “made by China” model. 
AI and physical-AI diffusion should create a multipolar industrial-
capex cycle, benefiting global capital goods demand, with dif-
ferent regions capturing different layers of value. The result is a 
multipolar industrial system: the US leads in frontier technology, 
capital and demand; Europe in selected software, automation and 
complex equipment; Japan in precision and production efficiency; and 
China in scaled physical deployment and integrated industrial eco-
systems.
• The US has the strongest demand, technology and capital-
market position, but must rebuild industrial depth. It is best 
positioned to benefit from AI infrastructure, reshoring and 
electrification.  However, high production costs, permitting 
constraints and shallow domestic supplier networks mean 
that near-term reshoring will remain import-intensive and 
dependent on overseas machinery, components and mate-
rials. 
• Europe faces a more challenging structural pressure, but its 
differentiated industrial champions remain well positioned, 
benefiting from both China’s industrial upgrading and US 
reshoring.  Less differentiated and energy-intensive industries 
therefore face continued competitiveness pressure. 
• Japan should remain a critical supplier of precision and effi-
ciency technologies across this multipolar buildout. 
• Korea and Taiwan are likely to remain central to the technol-
ogy-intensive upstream layers of the AI cycle.

M
 BluePaper

Equity implications – Structural 
Opportunities
Industry 5.0 should be an efficiency- and ROE-driven equity cycle, 
rather than another indiscriminate capacity trade. Morgan Stanley 
China Strategist Laura Wang estimates potential ROE improvement 
by 2028 of approximately 6–7% for Energy, 2–3% for Materials, 
2–3% for Industrials and 8–9% for Information Technology, together 
contributing around 2% to MSCI China’s overall index ROE. 
We favor a barbell of structural technology and industrial-growth 
opportunities alongside quality dividend plays. Near-term earn-
ings visibility is strongest among the upstream enablers receiving the 
first wave of capex – semiconductors, optical components, industrial 
automation, advanced equipment, power systems and selected 
materials. This opportunity is disproportionately represented in 
A-shares: high-end upstream manufacturing and hard-core tech-
nology companies account for approximately 44.8% of the CSI 300, 
versus 14.7% of MSCI China and 8.3% of the Hang Seng Index. The 
value pool should migrate over time from companies building the 
chips, equipment, automation and infrastructure towards adopters 
that use AI to improve products, workflows and unit economics. 
Autos and machinery illustrate the shift from downstream 
volume toward higher-quality control points. China’s EV eco-
system remains a major industrial leadership platform, but the sector 
analysis favors differentiated auto-component suppliers over undif-
ferentiated OEM capacity. Selected parts leaders generate gross 
margins around 4–6ppt above vehicle manufacturers, such as ADAS 
chips and controllers, intelligent chassis, smart glass, steering and 
safety electronics. Construction-machinery  leaders are using electri-
fication and service capabilities to differentiate overseas. Home 
appliances provide more mature proof of the ecosystem-export 
model by moving from product exports toward localized R&D, pro-
duction, distribution and services globally.
Healthcare, materials, semiconductors and power represent the 
higher-value foundational layers of the theme. In healthcare, China 
is progressing from low-cost manufacturing toward high-end med-
ical imaging, IVD, surgical robotics and innovative-drug manufac-
turing, with overseas revenue to exceed 50% for many leading 
medtech companies within 3-5 years, while more than 30% of US 
FDA-approved on-patent novel drugs could be manufactured in 
China by 2030, and China already supplies around 30% of global API 
volume. Materials provide the physical foundation: copper and alu-
minum support grids, data centers and storage; lithium and battery 
materials enable electrification; rare-earth magnets support EVs, 
wind and humanoids; and glass and carbon fiber serve AI hardware, 
aerospace and low-altitude transportation. We favor high-end, quali-
fication-intensive or supply-constrained materials over generic com-
modity capacity.
Semiconductors, automation, robotics, power equipment are 
among the clearest cross-pillar beneficiaries. Smart factories 
require a full 'sense–think–act–connect' semiconductor stack: 
analog, power, MCU and mixed-signal devices provide high-volume 
distributed intelligence, while domestic CPU, GPU and AI accelera-
tors capture disproportionate value in industrial inference, machine 
vision and factory-wide optimization, through automation and 
robots. The opportunity combines factory-intelligence capex, local-
ization and globalization. China automation localization could 
exceed 70% by 2030, while machinery exports grew at a 12% CAGR 
during 2020–25 and accelerated 18% YoY in 4M26.  Power is the 
other indispensable layer: we forecast approximately Rmb4.1tn of 
China grid investment during 2026–30, 49% above the prior five-
year period.

Industry 5.0 Will Not Be Linear – Potential 
Bottlenecks and Risks 
Industrial revolutions have not always progressed smoothly. 
Railroads, telecoms, semiconductors, solar, and EVs all experienced 
prolonged periods of capital destruction before consolidation and 
ecosystem dominance emerged. Similarly, China's Industry 5.0 may 
initially suppress returns before creating long-term winners. The 
largest risk  is not technological failure, but economically inefficient 
success. China excels at scaling capacity, cutting costs and diffusing 
technology fast – but the same strengths could breed overcapacity, 
deflation and margin compression when demand cannot keep up the 
pace with supply. The road from investment to productivity, profits 
and income is thus likely to be bumpy, and policy or external shifts 
could quickly alter its pace and quality.
Along these lines, we present two alternative scenarios to our base 
case, with policy, technology and geopolitics being the key swing fac-
tors.
Bull case: Accelerated economic rebalancing, healthy reflation. By 
pairing its supply-side push with faster pension, healthcare and 
'hukou' reforms, alongside stronger housing stabilization, the gov-
ernment reduces precautionary savings and negative wealth effects, 
and unlocks stronger household consumption. Firmer domestic 
demand absorbs rising industrial output, curbs price-destructive 
competition and improves corporate earnings, cash flow and wages, 
creating a virtuous cycle of consumption, investment and innovation. 
This lifts potential GDP growth to 4.7% in 2026–27, 4.5% in 2028–30 
and 4.2% in 2031–35, supported by stronger capital deepening and 
total factor productivity (TFP) gains, while bringing the GDP deflator 
toward 1.5–2% over the medium term. Lower reliance on external 
demand and faster industrial-AI breakthroughs also ease trade fric-
tion and lift China’s share of global manufacturing value-add toward 
33% by 2035.
Bear case: More entrenched deflation amid premature policy 
tightening: Aggressive pro-cyclical fiscal consolidation amid con-
tinued household deleveraging and property weakness further 
depresses domestic demand, while low-return investment in AI infra-
structure, robotics and smart factories creates 'smart overcapacity' 
and renewed margin pressure. At the same time, restrictions on man-
ufacturing outward foreign direct investment (OFDI) slow overseas 
localization, provoking broader protectionism from more non-US 
trade partners. In this scenario,  potential GDP growth falls to 4.4% in 
2026–27, 4.0% in 2028–30 and 3.5% in 2031–35, with the cumula-
tive GDP uplift from AI and Industrial 5.0 limited to ~2% by 2035, 
which is largely offset by an AI-induced labor replacement effect. The 
GDP deflator remains at -0.5% to -1.0% over the medium term, rein-
forcing deleveraging and constraining innovation, while China’s share 
of global manufacturing value-add is capped at ~27% through 2035.


[Page 21]
M
 BluePaper
Morgan Stanley Research
21
Elevated global manufacturing growth during 
Industry 3.0 and 4.0
Industry 3.0: Automation – the automation era (from the mid-20th 
century to the 2000s), was defined by computer automation, pro-
grammable logic controllers (PLC), and industrial robots, together 
pushing toward higher throughput and lower unit cost. Industry 3.0 
raised productivity by automating repetitive and standardized tasks, 
resulting in lower costs with higher output volumes. The increased 
use of robots contributed 0.36ppt to annual labor-productivity 
growth in 17 developed countries, over 1993-2007, according to 
Graetz & Michaels "Robots at Work". 
Steps to China Industry 5.0: Proven Scale and Cost 
Leadership in Manufacturing  
 Since China began to open up in the late 1970s, its industrial base has undergone a continual upgrade, where it now has ~28% of 
global manufacturing value-add. Manufacturing in China has evolved into a large, cost competitive and efficient base, rapidly 
catching up to global developed markets (DM). We believe its scale and global leading industries, such as solar, batteries, EVs, and 
drones, will extend to more frontier industries, such as embodied AI and upstream localization under Industry 5.0. 
Industry 4.0: Digitalization – starting in the 2010s, the digitalization 
era made manufacturing digitally visible, connected, and increasingly 
automated. The core idea was to connect cyber, physical, and enter-
prise systems, optimizing the production process. Industrial 4.0 con-
nected production devices for better optimization. In China, among 
230 excellence-level smart factories reported, on average, 22.3% 
higher production efficiency, 50.2% lower defect rates and 28.4% 
shorter product-development cycles, according to MIIT. China's labor 
productivity recorded a 9% CAGR over 2010-25, although this 
reflected industrial upgrades, deepening capital, urbanization and 
automation, as well as digitalization.

China became the world’s largest manufacturing 
base
China has upgraded its industrial base quickly to catch up to DMs: 
• Declining proportion of high energy-consuming indus-
tries, driven by continued supply-side reform and de-capacity 
in these sectors since 2015, and accelerated capacity 
upgrades after 2020 when the carbon neutrality target was 
launched. In 2023, China's energy consumption intensity 
(standard coal in tons per value-added in US$mn) of the man-
ufacturing sector was 699t/US$mn, declining by 6% p.a. from 
2,182t/US$mn in 2004, according to NBS. 
• Significant increase of high-value added manufacturing 
industries, such as electrical and computing machinery and 
medicine related products. The percentage mix of manufac-
turing value-add for these China technology-intensive indus-
tries doubled to 34% in 2025 from 18% in 1993, according to 
NBS. 
• Significant decline of labor-intensive industries, particu-
larly light industries such as textiles, apparel and leather 
goods. The percentage mix of manufacturing value-add for 
these China labor-intensive industries declined to 15% in 
2025 from 28% in 1993, according to NBS. 
According to the World Bank, China accounted for 27% of global 
manufacturing value add in 2025. Large and integrated supply chains 
have driven efficiency gains and cost reductions. While labor has 
become more expensive over the years, increasing penetration of 
automation and digital technology will continue to facilitate cost 
reduction and process refinement in China, in our view. Moreover, 
overall manufacturing remains cost-competitive thanks to synergies 
and complementarity between China's different industries, helped 
by the country's robust infrastructure.
In addition to China's edge in production, its large domestic market 
has provided a testing field before going global. Despite criticism of 
intense competition and low margins, the large domestic market has 
allowed companies to test products rapidly, collect customer feed-
back, scale successful products, customize production for diverse 
demand, and strengthening product competitiveness for global 
market competition.

23
The industrial sector in China is the most compre-
hensive globally
Not only scaled up, but scaled out: During China's industrialization, 
it also expanded its capabilities across products. According to the 
MIIT, China – with 41 headline industrial categories, 207 industrial 
categories, and 666 industrial sub-categories – is the only country 
that covers all categories listed in the United Nations Industrial 
Classification. Furthermore, China’s Economic Complexity Index 
(ECI, which focuses on ubiquity and diversity of export capabilities) 
continues to trend higher, reflecting China's expansion into a broader 
mix of advanced manufactured goods. 
Decentralized supply chains with strong efficiency: Many of 
China's industrial firms, both big and small, were created in the pur-
suit of greater efficiency. They have developed into decentralized 
industrial supply chains, dotted across China. With such a large 
number of companies, the pressure to outcompete on either superior 
products or lower cost is extremely high. This level of intense compe-
tition has resulted in strong cost competitiveness and ensured speed 
to market for many industrial products, in a global context.

24
Continual industrial upgrades – further localization 
and frontier industries expansion to continue
China's move up the 'smile curve' remains intact… After decades of 
industrial upgrades, China has improved its localization rate among 
different up/mid/downstream industries. We expect this trend to 
continue, given it's a natural path for China to move up the value chain 
to capture the higher upstream value-add. China's advantages in 
machinery, strong downstream foundation, huge domestic demand 
and dense supplier networks support the gradual localization pro-
cess. We note that localization in the auto/3C supply chain, and auto-
mation equipment more generally (including robots, FA 
components, and machine tools), has accelerated sharply in the past 
few years, riding the tailwinds of China's strong manufacturing cycle 
in 2020-22. While localization in semis, healthcare and aerospace in 
general is still low, it has already improved greatly. 
…as well as leading in frontier industries. Past examples such as 
solar and new-energy vehicles demonstrate how China can convert 
a large domestic market, supportive industrial policy, intense compe-
tition, rapid capacity expansion, and cost advantages into global lead-
ership in emerging sectors. We believe China's technology base and 
manufacturing know-how from mature industries (such as autos and 
precision components) have formed a solid foundation and can sup-
port the development of emerging industries, such humanoids and 
electric vertical take-off and landing aircraft (eVTOL). 
Exhibit 21: Supply chain – Moving up the value chain 'smile curve' is key to upgrading – i.e., upstream has a relatively lower localization 
rate but higher margin
Source: MIR, FactSet, Wind, Company Data, Morgan Stanley Research. Note: the darker color in the pie charts shows the localization rate. Blue pie is downstream localization rate, yellow pie is midstream equipment local-
ization rate, and green pie is upstream localization rate.

Morgan Stanley Research
25
 China's goals under Industry 5.0 are to achieve Industrial Intelligence, Resilience and Leadership. We believe China will move from 
cost and scale leadership towards ecosystem and industrial innovation leadership in this phase, achieving a new level of 
breakthroughs and increasing China's industrial base. 
Over the next decade, we believe China will lead the way in building AI-native manufacturing operating systems and embodied-AI 
labor platforms. The resulting step-change in productivity should power two strategic outcomes: 1) the export of integrated 
industrial ecosystems and rising influence over global standards in industries where China already leads; and 2) the creation of 
sovereign, evolvable industrial technology stacks, together with selective leadership in industrial innovation, reinforced by AI-
accelerated discovery. 
Industry 5.0 –  Striving For Ecosystem and Industrial 
Innovation Leadership
What is Industry 5.0?
We  are at an AI inflection point  
Industries are now gradually shifting to generative and agentic AI, 
moving from single-equipment applications to system-level applica-
tions. Industry 5.0 factories combine the digital foundation of 4.0 
with advances in AI and robotics to create self-optimizing systems. 
Beyond existing factory automation, software can coordinate various 
applications and processes within factories to dynamically optimize 
scheduling, quality control, material flow, maintenance timing, and 
even engineering change responses in real time.
China's unique industrial revolution
China starts from a position where it has 1)  state-guided agenda for 
frontier industries (not just AI, but various industries, such as 
embodied intelligence and biotech), 2) unrivalled manufacturing 
scale, 3) comprehensive supply chains, 4) continuous technology 
iteration, 5) a unique capital system, 6) a massive supply of engi-
neering talent, and 7) ample physical infrastructure. No other 
country has all of these attributes in such depth, and therefore we 
believe China's Industry 5.0 approach will be unique, leveraging its 
strong industrial base, empowered through industrial intelligence, 
industrial resilience and, as time progresses, increasing industrial 
leadership. 


[Page 26]
M
 BluePaper
26
Three Pillars of Industry 5.0 – IRL: Intelligence, 
Resilience, and Leadership
Exhibit 22: China's Industry 5.0 –  Three Pillars Reinforce Each 
Other
Source: Morgan Stanley Research 
1. Industrial Intelligence –  Fusion of AI, Automation and Embodied 
Intelligence.  The future of manufacturing is in closed-loop, adaptive 
and replicable systems that improve with every production cycle. 
Industrial AI becomes more valuable as a reusable platform, 
improving efficiency throughout the equipment and production life-
cycle. The self-learning ability of AI will be key. Every production 
defect, maintenance event, or operator intervention will generate 
incremental proprietary industrial data, creating a compounding fly-
wheel of better models, higher yields, lower costs, and faster 
learning.
2. Industrial Resilience –  Controllable Supply Chains In a 
Multipolar World. We believe gaining control over key bottlenecks 
is a key target for China, including industrial software, advanced 
equipment, critical materials, energy security and supply-chain 
chokepoints. In a multipolar world, localized and resilient manufac-
turers are critical.
3. Industrial Leadership – Achieving Ecosystem Dominance. China 
has been a follower since the original Industrial Revolution, but with 
new frontier industries (humanoids, low-altitude economy, etc) 
emerging and China's industrial upgrades, we believe China's target 
is to become a leader in more manufacturing areas along the value 
chain, from downstream to upstream, and setting standards across 
industries such as EVs, batteries, automation, humanoids, and smart 
infrastructure, similar to how the US shaped the global digital 
economy through tech hardware, internet and software platforms. 
Together, the three pillars form a self-reinforcing flywheel. 
Industrial Intelligence makes factories and supply chains smarter 
with every production cycle, generating better data and stronger 
models. Industrial Resilience protects that system by securing the 
critical layers and supply-chain chokepoints, operating through dis-
ruptions. Industrial Leadership then converts this accumulated capa-
bility into ecosystem control, letting suppliers, customers and 
partners build around. In our view, the three pillars form an inte-
grated industrial architecture.
Next Decade: Striving for Ecosystem and 
Industrial Innovation Leadership
 In the last two decades, the dominant pattern was deployment-led 
catch-up: China first created a very large domestic market, then used 
that scale to shorten engineering cycles, localize tools and compo-
nents, improve process know-how, and eventually export either fin-
ished products or manufacturing systems themselves. This pattern is 
visible in solar PV, EVs, batteries, high-speed rail, home appliances, 
electronics assembly, and, increasingly, automation equipment. That 
pattern was supported by a very large industrial base, dense cluster 
formation, and rising innovation. 
We believe the next stage will be different, as China's manufac-
turing advantage will be systematic. We expect China to move 
from large-scale application to foundational industrial innova-
tion, converting its scale and cost advantage into ecosystem and 
industrial technology leadership. 
We expect five breakthroughs to occur in the intelligence, resil-
ience and leadership layers in the next decade:
1. AI-native manufacturing operating systems – automated 
factories become adaptive factories.
2. Embodied AI as a labor platform – physical labor becomes 
software-upgradable capital.
3. Sovereign and evolvable industrial stack – localization 
advances from component substitution to architecture con-
trol.
4. Ecosystem exports and industrial standard-setting – 
China moves from product exporter to industrial-system 
orchestrator.
5. Industrial technology Innovation leadership – China 
evolves from fast industrializer towards industrial discovery 
engine in selective industries


[Page 27]
M
 BluePaper
Morgan Stanley Research
27
The first two sit primarily within Industrial Intelligence; the third defines Industrial Resilience; the fourth & fifth express Industrial Leadership. 
Many industries will see huge growth opportunities, either as Industry 5.0 enablers or beneficiaries. 
Exhibit 23: The key beneficiaries lie in five categories, and will create a new generation of winners across automation, robotics, semicon-
ductors, materials, etc, for foundational layers, and Industry 5.0 productivity adopters to achieve global leadership 
Source: Morgan Stanley Research
1. AI-native manufacturing operating systems –  from 
automated factories to adaptive factories
Industry 4.0 connected machines and digitized workflows. An 
AI-native manufacturing operating system would coordinate product 
design, process engineering, production scheduling, quality inspec-
tion, maintenance, energy use, logistics, procurement, and supplier 
response. A factory can sense, decide, act and learn across the entire 
production system, to continuously adjust to changes in demand, 
materials, equipment conditions and product mix. 
The historical lesson: general-purpose technologies require orga-
nizational redesign. Steam power and electricity did not transform 
manufacturing only because they supplied a new source of energy. 
The largest gains emerged when factories changed their scale, 
layout, division of labor and production organization. The implication 
for AI is likewise straightforward – putting AI on top of a legacy work-
flow is unlikely to create a manufacturing revolution. Reorganizing 
decision-making, data flows, machinery and supply chains around AI 
could.
The adaptive factory would improve productivity in several ways, 
which are critical for the manufacturing competitiveness. 
• Higher labor productivity: AI agents can perform production 
planning, troubleshooting, documentation, quality analysis 
and maintenance diagnosis. Human workers move from 
repetitive monitoring and manual coordination towards 
exception handling, engineering and system supervision.
• Higher equipment productivity: through predictive mainte-
nance and closed-loop process control, lifting overall equip-
ment effectiveness and reducing the amount of capex 
required for each unit of output.
• Lower quality and material losses: AI-based process controls 
can detect defects earlier and identify causal relationships 
across equipment conditions, raw materials and process 
parameters. This reduces scrap, rework, warranty costs, and 
customer complaints.
• 4. Economical mass customization could be realized in adap-
tive factories for the first time. 


[Page 28]
M
 BluePaper
28
Exhibit 24: Industry 4.0 connected the factory whereas China's Industry 5.0 makes it adaptive
Source: Morgan Stanley Research
Why we think China can lead 
The largest addressable manufacturing base. China accounts for 
roughly 30% of global manufacturing value-added and is the only 
economy covering all traditional and emerging categories in the UN 
industrial classification, with 41 headline industrial categories, 207 
categories and 666 subcategories. This gives industrial AI an unusu-
ally broad set of real-world applications and cross-industry learning 
opportunities.
The deployment base is also becoming more intelligent. China 
already has the world’s largest industrial-robot installed base and 
has rapidly increased automation and digital adoption. The founda-
tion is therefore sufficiently developed for AI to move from isolated 
pilots toward system-level optimization. By early 2025, China had 
built more than 30,000 basic-level smart factories, more than 1,200 
advanced-level smart factories and more than 230 excellence-level 
smart factories. By end-2025, China also had more than 4.83mn 5G 
base stations and 2.9bn mobile-IoT terminal users, giving it one of the 
world’s deepest physical-digital industrial infrastructures.
Policy is shifting from “digital factory” to “industrial agent”. 
China’s 2026 “AI+ Manufacturing” program targets is to achieve more 
AI adoption in manufacturing by 2027, including: three to five gener-
al-purpose models deeply deployed in manufacturing, 1,000 high-
level industrial agents, 100 high-quality industrial datasets, 500 
representative application scenarios, and two to three globally influ-
ential ecosystem-leading companies. The significance is not merely 
the number of pilot projects. The policy focus has moved toward the 
data, model, agent and ecosystem layers required for an industrial 
operating system.
We have already seen very active AI adoption in manufacturing facto-
ries, for example, Valeo’s Shenzhen facility deployed AI-enabled man-
ufacturing and advanced algorithms across 42 use cases. The site 
reported a 60.2% increase in productivity, a 45.9% reduction in fin-
ished-goods defects, a 34.5% reduction in lead time and a 27.1% 
reduction in unit energy use. Midea’s Wuhu operation implemented 
113 digital use cases, 35% AI-enabled, including real-time order man-
agement, advanced planning and a supply-chain control tower. 
End-to-end delivery lead time fell 39%, inventory days fell 30% and 
market defects declined 86%.

An early Industry 5.0 case study: CATL's manufacturing –  Complexity and Intelligence 
CATL's manufacturing demonstrates exceptional complexity and intelligence, characterized by self-developed production lines, 
extensive real-time quality control, and massive data processing capabilities that create significant competitive advantages and 
high barriers to entry. Our battery analyst Jack Lu visited the CATL factory last year; below are some key numbers to consider:
• Self-designed production lines with over 25,000 units of components;
• Daily output of 2.2mn+ cells;
• 6,800+ real-time quality control points that secure high yield rates and quality premiums; and 
• 340k data transactions per second.
How does CATL achieve these?
Platformization –  CATL's replicable intelligence + Data Loop Compounding for 20 years: CATL operates five data platforms 
spanning R&D, testing, manufacturing, operation and after-sales, backed by more than 100 billion data assets. It also says 95% of 
production equipment is connected, more than 7,000 quality-control points are monitored in real time, and product-quality data 
are traceable for more than 20 years. 
• In 2021, its Ningde plant was first recognized as a Lighthouse factory. It leverage AI, advanced analytics and edge/cloud 
computing to achieve 75% improvement in labor productivity and 10% reduction in energy consumption per year. 
• In 2022, its Yibin plant was also recognized as a Lighthouse. CATL further layered AI, IoT and flexible automation on top 
of its Ningde model, with 40,000 sensors via NB-IoT and 5G. It achieved 17% higher line speed, 14% lower yield loss and 
zero carbon emissions.
• In 2023, its Liyang plant was also recognized as a Lighthouse. It combined big-data simulation for quality testing, additive 
manufacturing for faster changeovers, computer vision for micron-level inspection. WEF’s reported a 320% output 
increase, a 33% reduction in manufacturing costs, a 47.4% reduction in normalized emissions and a 99% reduction in 
quality defects.
End-to-end adaptivity –  from manual design to forward design and prediction: In January 2026 CATL was recognized by WEF 
for its AI-driven next-generation battery-design platform that shifts development from manual trial-and-error towards forward 
design and prediction before production.
Exhibit 29:
CATL's Intelligent Cell Design
Source: CATL


[Page 31]

Morgan Stanley Research
31
2. Embodied AI as a labor platform –  from task-spe-
cific equipment to software-upgradable physical cap-
ital
A traditional robot’s function is largely fixed at installation. An 
embodied-AI machine could acquire additional capabilities through 
new models, fleet learning and over-the-air updates without a com-
plete mechanical redesign, i.e. the same physical machine can poten-
tially learn additional tasks. Physical labor becomes software-
upgradable capital equipment. Over its life, its mechanical 
hardware still depreciates, but its task library, perception, reasoning 
and coordination capabilities can improve. Therefore, embodied AI 
could extend automation from highly standardized production into 
flexible high-mix manufacturing, intralogistics, inspection, mainte-
nance, rework and other activities that remain difficult to automate 
economically.
In addition, the embodied AI platform converts deployment into 
data generation. Each deployed machine can generate data, and the 
data flows back into model training, the installed base becomes a 
learning network. More deployment improves capability; greater 
capability unlocks additional use cases; additional use cases drive 
more deployment. This is potentially a more durable advantage than 
one-time hardware cost leadership.
Beyond humanoids, embodied AI is much broader, including auton-
omous commercial vehicles, industrial robots, mobile manipulator, 
collaborative robots, AMRs/AGVs, drones/VToLs, etc. 
• Humanoids are particularly relevant in brownfield environ-
ments designed for human bodies: standard doors, stairs, 
aisles, workbenches, tools, and shelf heights. Their potential 
advantage is reduced infrastructure modification and a 
broader task envelope. 
• Dedicated machines should remain superior where volume is 
high and the task is stable. They can be faster, cheaper, more 
energy-efficient and easier to certify. 
Based on Morgan Stanley Research forecasts, unit sales across 
these categories will rise from 8mn units in 2025 to 29mn in 2030 
and 76mn in 2035. Embodied AI changes manufacturing economics, 
potentially extends automation to autonomous manufacturing, 
addressing the large portion of industrial labor that conventional 
fixed automation has struggled to automate economically. 
• For a copy of the Morgan Stanley Global Robot model, please 
reach out to your Morgan Stanley representative.

What can we expect in terms of manufacturing productivity 
improvement? There are three key aspects, in our view. 
• Direct operating productivity, from labor hour substitution, 
longer operating windows, greater consistency and better 
safety; 
• Capital productivity and manufacturing flexibility, which is 
more important, on lower stranded-asset risk, faster new 
product ramp and elastic surge capacity; 
• Learning productivity and network effects. 
Demographic and skills constraints would be offset by the 
embodied AI working platform as well, while the pre-condition is 
that there are sufficient manufacturing know-how/data to perfect 
the AI manufacturing operation system. The world aging problem 
and labor shortage are expected to worsen in the coming decades. 
United Nations projects the world’s population aged 65+ will reach 
16% in 2050, from 10% in 2025. The situation in China is more con-
cerning. China population YoY % change has turned negative since 
2022. China’s population aged 65+ reached 16% in 2025, and this will 
accelerate when the over 300mn people in the 1970-80s reach 
retirement age in the next 5-10 years. Humanoid robots will likely 
drive labor force restructuring in the mid/long-term. Based on our 
survey data, an average of 11% of jobs will potentially be replaced by 
robots in five years, increasing to 28% in 10 years.

Why we think China can lead
China has a broad physical-AI ecosystem that spans all of the 
embodied AI items mentioned above (autonomous vehicles, 
robots, drones, etc), putting it ahead in the adoption of physical AI in 
the manufacturing loop. For example, even though it's still early in 
the adoption of humanoid robots globally, Chinese enterprises have 
a higher adoption willingness for humanoids. In addition, fast adop-
tion in real factories could provide useful feedback, which should 
support faster product and model iteration. Our AlphaWise survey of 
Chinese businesses indicates 62% of respondents are likely to adopt 
in humanoids over 2025-27, indicating significant potential. This year 
we have seen initial adoption across multiple industries, notable 
examples being logistics sorting (SF Express), industrial material 
handling (Sany RE), battery pack production (CATL), F&B reception 
(Haidilao). Adoption in real working scenarios is strongly encouraged 
and supported by government, vs. trials and lab testing. The recent 
MIIT and SASAC initiative is use-case-driven, targeting 10k level 
deployment capacity and >100 high value applications by end 2026. 
Local governments and SOEs must identify operational sites (≥20/
province, ≥10/central SOE) for humanoid training. State Grid has 
placed a Rmb6.8bn (US$940mn) order to procure 500 humanoid 
robots, 3k dual-arm robots, and 5k quadrupeds.

Existing supply chains materially lower the cost hurdle. A realistic 
key decider of embodied AI adoption is the new system ROI. As we 
analyzed earlier, scale and cost are the key advantages of China's 
manufacturers, and based on our comparison of key robotics compo-
nents, China's supply chain cost is normally 20-50% of global brands' 
costs. We expect that Chinese-made components will close the gap 
with global brands in terms of precision, product life and stability, by 
solving any issues through materials, process refinement, design and 
AI compensation.
3. Sovereign Industrial Technology –  from compo-
nent localization to architecture autonomy
The key difference between localization and autonomy is that  local-
ization lets China keep producing, while an evolvable sovereign 
stack lets China keep improving. China has spent the past decade 
localizing 
components 
across 
semiconductors, 
automation, 
machinery, power electronics, and advanced materials. The higher-
level industrial breakthrough would be the construction of inte-
grated technology stacks that can operate and continue to improve 
under external constraints. We also emphasize that architecture 
autonomy does not require 100% domestic content. Full autarky 
would often be economically inefficient. The practical test is whether 
China retains the ability to operate, repair, redesign and continue 
improving critical systems without a single external point of failure.
A resilient industrial base has three characteristics, in our view, which 
are: 1) no irreplaceable single-point dependencies, 2) sufficient con-
trol of critical interfaces to redesign around disruption, and 3) the 
ability to improve performance independently over time. 
Achieving industrial architecture autonomy is critical. It not only 
ensures production continuity, but faster domestic innovation 
loops, higher domestic value-added and profit-pool migration (to 
software, chips, high-end equipment and materials, services, etc), 
and we also expect these foundational abilities to enable frontier 
industries and ecosystem exports (discussion in below break-
through layer #4). 
Exhibit 38: Sovereign industrial stack roadmap: the breakthrough is architectural autonomy, not domestic content alone
Source: Morgan Stanley Research


[Page 35]
M
 BluePaper
Morgan Stanley Research
35
 Where are China’s most significant sovereignty gaps, and hence 
the opportunities?
We use 6 factor screening framework as listed below
• Criticality: Could the absence of the technology stop produc-
tion or block an entire downstream industry?
• Current gap: Is China still materially dependent on a small 
number of overseas suppliers?
• Downstream anchor: Does China have sufficient domestic 
demand to support qualification and scale?
• Technical traceability: Can domestic suppliers reach commer-
cially acceptable performance by 2035?
• Stack leverage: Does progress in this layer unlock several 
downstream industries?
• Profit-pool quality: Are barriers based on process knowledge, 
software, qualification and service—or only on temporary 
scarcity?
China’s most significant sovereignty gaps are concentrated not in 
downstream manufacturing scale, where it is already globally com-
petitive, but in the upstream “brain, nervous system and precision 
layer” of industry—semiconductors and compute, industrial soft-
ware and control, high-end machine tools and metrology, and 
selected advanced materials. These technologies determine whether 
China can independently design, operate and continuously upgrade 
complex industrial systems. The gap is deepest in frontier semicon-
ductor equipment and compute, but the most attractive opportuni-
ties are often one layer adjacent: WFE, EDA, advanced packaging, 
industrial control software, high-end CNC, precision motion, sensors 
and qualified specialty materials, where China has large domestic 
demand, a viable customer-qualification path and significant room to 
move up the value chain.
The opportunity is therefore less about blanket import substitution 
and more about building interoperable, commercially reliable tech-
nology stacks. We see the strongest medium-term potential in areas 
with broad downstream pull across multiple industries – especially 
industrial software, precision equipment and advanced materials – 
because progress there can unlock semiconductors, robotics, aero-
space, medical equipment and AI-native manufacturing simultane-
ously. Aerospace and scientific instruments also represent important 
strategic gaps, but their commercialization is likely to be more selec-
tive and slower due to long certification cycles, smaller production 
volumes and greater dependence on accumulated scientific and sys-
tems-engineering capabilities.
Exhibit 39: Technology sovereignty gap and 2035 opportunities 
Source: Morgan Stanley Research


[Page 36]
M
 BluePaper
36
How can China advance from localization to industry technology 
sovereignty?
China’s ability to move from localization to technology sovereignty 
rests on a structural advantage that few other economies possess –  
a very large downstream manufacturing system capable of 
financing, testing and refining upstream technologies at commer-
cial scale. As analyzed above, sovereignty requires domestic soft-
ware, equipment, components and materials working together with 
acceptable yields, reliability and lifetime costs; the system must then 
be capable of redesigning itself and funding its next generation. 
China’s approximately 30% share of global manufacturing value-
added, complete industrial coverage and dense clusters in elec-
tronics, machinery, EVs, batteries and emerging physical-AI 
industries provide unusually powerful lead-customer and qualifica-
tion mechanisms. We are confident of this localization to sovereignty 
transition for several reasons given below. 
Exhibit 40: The involutional loop towards Industrial Technology Sovereignty
Source: NBS, MIIT, Morgan Stanley Research
1. China’s innovation system is becoming larger and more enter-
prise-led. National R&D spending reached Rmb3.93tn in 2025, or 
2.80% of GDP, up from Rmb2.44tn and 2.36% in 2020. China also had 
the equivalent of 7.95mn full-time researchers in 2025. Enterprises 
now account for more than 77% of R&D spending, while the country 
has 1,921 nationally recognized enterprise technology centers and 
more than 17,600 “little giant” specialized SMEs. This matters 
because industrial sovereignty is built through repeated improve-
ments in yield, uptime, precision and process control, rather than a 
single laboratory breakthrough. An enterprise-led R&D system is 
more likely to connect research directly to market demand and fac-
tory operating data.
2. China is building the commercialization infrastructure between 
the laboratory and mass production. By August 2025, MIIT had 
included more than 2,400 manufacturing pilot platforms in its 
reserve pool and selected 241 for the first key cultivation cohort. 


[Page 37]
M
 BluePaper
Morgan Stanley Research
37
These platforms assess whether a prototype's technology pathway, 
product consistency, production process, and economics are mature 
enough for mass production. At the other end of the commercializa-
tion chain, China had built more than 30,000 basic-level, 1,200 
advanced-level, and 230 excellent-level smart factories by early 
2025. Among the excellent-level factories, average R&D cycles were 
28.4% shorter, production efficiency was 22.3% higher, defect rates 
were 50.2% lower, and carbon emissions were 20.4% lower. While 
these factories represent selected leaders and outperform the 
national average, they demonstrate China's growing ability to trans-
late advances in equipment, software, and manufacturing processes 
into measurable industrial outcomes.
3. Cross-sector learning. Technologies developed in one large 
Chinese ecosystem can be redeployed into another. The capabilities 
built in EVs (motors, power electronics, batteries, sensing and con-
trol) are now supporting humanoids, autonomous machinery and 
low-altitude aviation. Solar-equipment suppliers have accumulated 
knowledge in thin-film deposition, coating, vacuum systems and pro-
cess control that can be adapted selectively to semiconductor equip-
ment. In Morgan Stanley Research's 2026 China Industrials outlook, 
we estimated automation and industrial-robot localization at 
approximately 40% and 55%, respectively, in 2025, potentially rising 
to 60-70% by 2030. The next localization frontier is therefore 
moving upward from generic hardware into semiconductor equip-
ment, machine tools, high-precision sensors, screws, specialty mate-
rials, software and control systems.
4. Overseas expansion is beginning to provide an external audit of 
domestic capability. China equipment exports grew an estimated 
15-20% annually during 2020-25, while overseas revenue among the 
Chinese industrial stocks under Morgan Stanley coverage increased 
from 8% in 2020 to 11% in 2024.  Companies that progress from fol-
lowing Chinese customers overseas to qualifying with multinational 
clients are moving closer to genuine technology sovereignty and, 
eventually, industrial leadership, in our view.
Exhibit 41: China now combines rising enterprise-led R&D, commercialization infrastructure, localization progress and growing overseas 
validation
Source: NBS, MIIT, Morgan Stanley Research estimates

 BluePaper
38
4. Ecosystem exports and industrial standard-setting 
–  from product exporter to industrial-system orches-
trator
China is already the world’s largest goods exporter. The next level of 
industrial leadership is not simply about exporting more products 
but complete operating ecosystems that combine products, lifecycle 
maintenance, infrastructure, software, technical standards, and 
localized supply chains. The competitive unit changes from the 
product to the system.
We see five stages unfolding in China's industrial globalization. Many 
companies are already at stages 1-4, and we believe more companies 
can achieve stage 5, which represents industrial leadership rather 
than export success, along with their higher penetration in overseas 
local operations. 
• Stage 1: Product exports. Chinese companies sell finished 
equipment overseas, often competing through price and 
delivery speed.
• Stage 2: Following Chinese customers. Suppliers accompany 
Chinese EV, battery, electronics and infrastructure cus-
tomers into overseas markets.
• Stage 3: Local production and service. Companies establish 
local plants, distribution, maintenance, engineering and man-
agement to meet local-content and customer-support 
requirements.
• Stage 4: Penetrating global customers. The supplier moves 
beyond China-linked customers and qualifies with multina-
tional OEMs, utilities and industrial companies.
• Stage 5: Ecosystem and standard control. The company or 
industry influences interfaces, protocols, certification, ser-
vice practices, data formats, infrastructure design, financing 
models.
China is moving up the ladder, from export products to ODI
China’s outward investment is also becoming more manufacturing-
oriented. Overseas direct investment (ODI) grew at a 19% CAGR over 
2019-24, and manufacturing represented 15% of total ODI in 2023. 
China’s manufacturing ODI has increasingly focused on autos, metals 
and transport equipment, reflecting a shift from exporting products 
toward placing production and supply chains closer to final markets. 
Investment has been increasing in ASEAN, Latin America and parts of 
Europe as companies localize production and mitigate trade bar-
riers.This provides the starting point for ecosystem exports: products 
establish the installed base; overseas factories and service networks 
increase customer dependence; interfaces and standards then 
become more influential.
This process is likely to accelerate in a multipolar world. Tariffs and 
local-content requirements make pure export models less sustain-
able, but they also encourage leading Chinese manufacturers to 
develop local operations  to meet local demand. 
Exhibit 42: China’s globalization is shifting from exports toward local production, service networks and ecosystem embedding
Source: Ministry of Commerce, SAFE, CEIC, Morgan Stanley Research


[Page 39]
M
 BluePaper
Morgan Stanley Research
39
China has already demonstrated selected system-export capability. 
CATL is licensing cell-to-pack technology and providing production-
line and manufacturing know-how in Thailand, while Ford’s Michigan 
LFP plant is designed to use CATL battery-cell knowledge and ser-
vices. China has also delivered two overseas Hualong One nuclear 
units in Pakistan, showing that complex regulated systems can be 
exported selectively.
In the next decade, we expect more industries will extend product 
exports to ecosystem exports. EVs and battery systems, solar-plus-
storage, and power equipment and smart grids are the most 
advanced, as China already combines globally competitive products 
with infrastructure, software, integration and lifecycle services. A 
second group including smart factories, ports and logistics automa-
tion, construction equipment and green shipbuilding, has credible 
system-export potential, in our view, as China has strong manufac-
turing scale, yet still needs deeper overseas service networks, soft-
ware capability, certification and lifecycle support. Humanoids, 
eVTOL and autonomous laboratories represent frontier options, 
which are potentially powerful ecosystem and standard-setting plat-
forms, but their commercial economics, safety and international 
acceptance remain unproven. 
Exhibit 43: Potential ecosystem-export opportunities from existing leaders, emerging industries and frontier industries
Source: Morgan Stanley Research
How can China become an ecosystem exporter?
1)  China possesses powerful market share. Standards emerge more 
readily where deployment is large enough to reveal engineering 
problems and amortize infrastructure. China has 27% of global man-
ufacturing value-added, the world’s broadest industrial-system cov-
erage, leading positions in many new industries such as EVs, 
batteries, solar, equipment and electronics, and large domestic infra-
structure networks. China's industrial operation is more on  ecosys-
tems 
(equipment, 
software, 
component, 
energy, 
services, 
infrastructure, training, etc) at a scale, setting the foundations for 
ecosystem exports.
2) Overseas investment is shifting China from exporter to local 
operator. China’s ODI grew at an estimated 19% over 2019–24, while 
manufacturing accounted for 15% of total ODI in 2023. China’s manu-
facturing ODI has also increasingly focused on autos, metals and 
transport equipment, reflecting a shift from exporting products 
toward placing production and supply chains closer to final markets. 
In our view, this provides the starting point for ecosystem exports, 
with the products establish the installed base, while overseas facto-
ries and service networks increase customer dependence, and inter-


[Page 40]
M
 BluePaper
40
faces and standards then become more influential. This process is 
likely to accelerate in a multipolar world. Tariffs and local-content 
requirements make pure export models less sustainable, but they 
also encourage leading Chinese manufacturers to develop local 
operations to meet local demand. 
3) China’s cost position makes ecosystem adoption affordable, 
especially in emerging markets. The recent wide adoption of China's 
LLM is a good example of the importance of cost affordability.  A mul-
tipolar world also increases demand for alternative industrial archi-
tectures. China is particularly well positioned where customers 
prioritize cost, speed and turnkey delivery. In the near future, the 
strongest opportunities are likely in ASEAN, the Middle East, Latin 
America, Africa, and select parts of Europe, rather than uniform 
global adoption.
Having said that, although we are positive on China's export evolu-
tion, the path will be bumpy, especially given geopolitical resistance. 
Certification and trust also take time to build. 
4) Industrial Technology Innovation Leadership –  from fast indus-
trializer to an industrial R&D engine
This is the least mature of the five breakthroughs, but potentially the 
most transformative. 
 China’s established industrial advantage lies in engineering, commer-
cialization, scale-up and cost reduction. In the AI era, the advantage 
will support upstream extension, into discovery by shortening the 
cycle between scientific knowledge, simulation, experimentation, 
pilot production, manufacturing feedback, achieving a closed discov-
ery-to-manufacturing loop, in our view.
Many global industry leaders are mainly strongest at research 
copilot and predictive design, while China’s potential differentiation 
lies in pilot scale optimization and manufacturing feedback, because 
it has a large physical base for experimentation, pilot production and 
commercialization. AI’s industrial value will be narrowing the “lab-
to-fab” gap.
Evolution from adopter to leader
As mentioned above, China's national R&D expenditure rose to 
around 2.8% of GDP (US$550bn annually) in 2025, second only to 
the US in absolute terms. A deepening R&D ecosystem strengthens 
its ability to localize, refine and commercialize next-generation indus-
trial technologies, providing a credible innovation base for long-cycle 
manufacturing upgrading. China is now an increasingly important 
source of industrial innovation, with the world’s largest concentra-
tion of top innovation clusters and a sharp increase in patent filings.

Why does this matter?  Foundational innovation has a much larger 
multiplier
A downstream sector win benefits one value chain. A breakthrough 
in materials, equipment, sensors or industrial software can lift many 
value chains simultaneously. It raises the performance ceiling of what 
Chinese manufacturers can make, while lowering the cost floor of 
producing it. 
If China becomes a global leader in selected foundational layers, it 
can define cost curves, product specifications, process standards and 
export platforms. Profit pools would migrate upstream into mate-
rials, equipment, components and industrial software. China would 
also gain stronger standard-setting power and greater resilience 
against geopolitical restrictions, and a stronger manufacturing fly-
wheel. 
If China becomes a strong follower rather than a global leader in 
other foundational layers, the impact is still significant. It can reduce 
bottleneck risk, lower the cost of industrial upgrading, increase bar-
gaining power in supply chains, shorten engineering cycles and pre-
serve competitiveness in both mid-end and high-end manufacturing.
Broad industry benefits
Industrial intelligence – AI enabler, AI adopter 
Industrial AI beneficiaries split into AI enablers and AI adopters. 
AI enablers are the physical capex layer: Data-center power equip-
ment, grid equipment, liquid cooling, storage and backup power, 
optical interconnect, automation industrial robots, cobots and 
humanoids, machine vision, motion control, CNC machine tools and 
industrial software. 
We separate AI adopters into 1) the product side, where AI will be 
adopted for product upgrades, such as autonomous driving, and 2) 
the factory side, and we think the fastest downstream adopters are 
sectors with abundant standardized process data and clear ROI. 
From this perspective, auto, electronics, batteries and solar, and 
machinery should move first because inspection, scheduling, predic-
tive maintenance and digital twins can be scaled across many similar 
lines.

Morgan Stanley Research
