Resilience –  Energy security, defense, semiconductor, 
emerging frontiers 
Resilience beneficiaries are the sectors where capex is no longer 
purely discretionary. In a more fragmented and increasingly multi-
polar world, supply-chain autonomy and energy security make semi-
conductors, semiconductor equipment and materials, industrial 
software and OT security, machine tool, grid equipment, energy 
storage, defense and aerospace, and advanced materials structurally 
favored. We would also include future-security sectors such as 
embodied AI, quantum technology and 6G, which have already 
moved into the formal policy funding agenda.
Leadership –  setting standards, gaining global 
market share
Leadership and standards beneficiaries are the sectors where China’s 
scale can translate into rule-setting power, not just export volume. 
The clearest existing leaders are EVs and intelligent vehicles, bat-
teries, solar PV, storage and power electronics, and industrial 
robotics. China accounted for almost two-thirds of global EV sales in 
2024, 80% of global battery cell production, more than 80% of 
solar-PV manufacturing across all stages, 95% of PV wafer manufac-
turing capacity, and 54% of global industrial robot installations. 
Policy is becoming more explicit on standards as well –  MIIT’s 2025 
standards work plan highlights smart manufacturing, industrial 
internet, PV, batteries, storage and embodied AI, and targets more 
than 100 international standards led by Chinese entities. We believe 
the sectors with the largest installed bases and export scale are best 
positioned to turn manufacturing leadership into ecosystem and 
standards leadership.

44
Industry 5.0 is set to anchor China’s next industrial capex supercycle, taking cumulative industrial investment to Rmb340trn (US
$50trn) in 2026-35 and creating Rmb80trn (US$12trn) of incremental capex demand versus a non-Industry 5.0 path, even as 
economy-wide investment growth slows with the aging population. The opportunity reflects a decade-long reconfiguration of 
China’s production system: AI should raise returns from upgrading its vast installed industrial base, while policy priorities and a 
stronger global capex cycle sustain the incentive to reinvest. The cycle should start gradually, with capex growth at around 4-5% in 
the next two years as lingering excess capacity, curbs on duplicative buildout and advanced-chip constraints weigh on spending, 
before accelerating to 6-7% from 2028 as capacity is absorbed, semiconductor localization advances, and AI shifts from 
infrastructure buildout to factory-wide deployment, production redesign and commercialization.
Industry 5.0 –  A Long Capex Supercycle
A New Capex Cycle –  Unlocking a US$12trn 
Opportunity by 2035 
Industrial investment has become the main counterweight to 
China’s broader capex slowdown: Since the demographic tide 
turned  from 2015 – tilting the economy towards net consumers over 
income earners – the national saving rate has trended down, pres-
suring investment's share of GDP. Yet industrial capex – including 
investments on factories, manufacturing equipment, industrial-re-
lated software and R&D, and electricity production & grids – has 
bucked this trend, with growth at an 8.8% CAGR in 2021-25, up from 
6.1% in 2016-20, lifting its share of GDP from 15.4% to 17.4%. The 
industrial capex resilience has been powered by Covid-era closed-
loop production, the new-energy boom, and surging AI and frontier-
sector spending, and should be further supported by the rising Asia 
capex supercycle driven by AI and AI-related infrastructure, energy 
transition, defence, and a rise in regional  industrial capex. These have 
cushioned the drag from property (share in GDP  down 4pp to 3.3%) 
and traditional infrastructure (down 1.7pp to 7.6%). 
Industry 5.0 should extend this upcycle as a capex-heavy transfor-
mation, not a light-asset digital upgrade or an ordinary cyclical 
recovery:  It requires simultaneous investment across three layers: 
(1) enabling infrastructure – including AI compute, data centers, 
power systems, grids, storage and cooling; (2) factory upgrading – 
including intelligent equipment, robots, sensors, edge semiconduc-
tors and industrial software; and (3) strategic resilience and leader-
ship – including supply-chain autonomy, critical technologies and 
capacity to sustain global market-share gains. Because each layer 
enables and reinforces the next, the cycle should be broader and 
more persistent than a normal replacement upswing.
We expect Rmb340trn cumulative industrial investment in 
2026-35, with Industry 5.0 creating Rmb80trn of incremental 
capex demand: This represents a major shift in investment composi-
tion, not a reversal of the economy-wide capex growth slowdown – 
we expect total investment/GDP to decline from around 39% today 
to 36.5% by 2035. The key potential drag will likely be  structural 
weakness in  property and traditional infrastructure spending, given 
demographic aging, local-government debt problems, and a still-
large housing inventory overhang. That said, Industry 5.0-induced 
capex should outperform,  as AI diffusion raises demand for both 
intangible and physical investment, while the global capex super-
cycle supports external demand and facilitates overcapacity absorp-
tion. 
We thus expect industrial capex/GDP ratio to rise to ~19.5% by 2035 
from 17.4% in 2025, slightly higher than its previous peak of 19.2% in 
2011. This would bring US$12trn (or Rmb80trn) of additional indus-
trial capex over the next decade vs. a non-Industry 5.0 trajectory, 
where share of industrial capex in GDP could slip to ~14.0% given 
persistent overcapacity, loss of manufacturing competitiveness amid 
an aging population, and weak support from external demand. 
The cycle should start slowly and accelerate as capacity and tech-
nology constraints ease: Despite the rising global capex supercycle 
and increasing policy support, Industry 5.0 will hardly bring a broad-
based manufacturing capex boom from day 1, given still pervasive 
excess capacity and Beijing's efforts to reduce repetitive buildout 
across regions/sectors under the "unified national market" initiative. 
Meanwhile, China’s ample existing power capacity reduces the need 
for a US-style surge in power-infrastructure investment, while data-
center deployment remains constrained by the availability and per-
formance of domestically produced advanced chips. We thus expect 
industrial capex growth to remain slow over the next couple of years, 
at about 4-5% annually  (vs. 5.7% p.a. in 2024-25). With overcapacity 
digestion, easing tech bottlenecks owing to rising semiconductor 
localization,  and a shift of AI deployment towards broader factory 
adoption and then to large-scale commercialization,  industrial capex 
growth could accelerate to 6-7% p.a. from 2028.

Why we believe this will be a stronger and 
longer supercycle
Learning from history: The history of industrial revolutions sug-
gests that capex can remain elevated for extended periods when:
• A new general-purpose technology coincides with large-scale 
physical deployment and complementary system redesign. 
For example, US electrification beginning in the 1880s 
required massive investments in power generation, grid infra-
structure, and factory reorganization.
• An economy undergoes industrial transformation. For 
example, Japan’s shift toward capital-intensive industries 
beginning in the 1960s drove substantial investment in heavy 
industry, high-efficiency equipment upgrades, and automa-
tion technologies.
• Supply chains are reallocated. For example, China’s rapid 
gains in global export market share following its WTO acces-
sion in the 2000s triggered significant investment across 
manufacturing and export-oriented industries.
When multiple factors occur simultaneously and reinforce one 
another, they can create multi-year industrial reinvestment 
cycles. (Details in Appendix III – Long Capex Cycle for Industrial 
Revolutions Historically .)
Industry 5.0 is revolutionary, requiring intensive capex for longer 
This is not a normal cyclical capex upswing driven by inventory 
restocking, but a structurally elevated industrial capex cycle with the 
potential to last close to a decade. AI + autonomy + sovereignty + 
industrial ecosystems are all happening simultaneously, which 


[Page 46]
M
 BluePaper
46
means each round of investment is likely to create the need for the 
next one. AI drives data-center, power, cooling, and network capex; 
autonomy drives robots, sensors, motion control, machine tools, and 
software; sovereignty enables domestic substitution, redundancy, 
and strategic capacity build-out; and dense industrial ecosystems 
accelerate replication across suppliers and downstream users. 
1. AI + physical AI's fast penetration
The capex expansion from industrial intelligence in our view stems 
from three core tailwinds: 1) the AI revolution itself requires heavy-
asset infrastructure investment. 2) AI will contribute to a manufac-
turing upgrade cycle that unlocks new sources of efficiency, 
resilience, quality improvement, and export competitiveness across 
China’s factory base. The penetration of intelligent equipment and AI 
agents will see an exponential acceleration. 3) Chinese capital goods 
companies benefit from global AI construction demand with 
expanding market share. 
Meanwhile, it is also more recurring and networked than past capex 
waves. China has more than 15k 5G+ industrial internet projects cov-
ering all 41 industrial categories, has already built 4k+ 5G factories, 
and is targeting 10k 5G factories by 2027. Combined with the smart-
factory rollout, that means this cycle is not a one-off plant build; it is 
an ongoing upgrade loop across equipment, connectivity, software 
and data.
Industrial Revolution 1.0 was built on coal and steam, 2.0 on elec-
tricity and oil, 3.0 on automation and the grid, and 4.0 on digital sys-
tems layered on electrification, and 5.0 is increasingly defined by AI 
at scale running on electricity at scale. China is uniquely positioned 
because it combines the three capabilities this new era requires in 
one system: a vast power network, the world’s deepest manufac-
turing base, and a dense industrial ecosystem that can turn AI 
demand into physical deployment. In other words, China is not just 
building LLM models; it is building the electricity, grid equipment, 
cooling systems, storage, robots, machine tools, sensors, and down-
stream applications that allow AI to move from software into the real 
economy, and industrial capex reinforce each other in a long-duration 
cycle.
Exhibit 56: AI expands the industrial capex pie across the full value chain 
Source: Morgan Stanley Research 
2. Reshaping the capex landscape –  skewed to equipment  
Equipment capex to surpass construction capex. Industry 5.0 is not 
just enlarging China’s capex pie – it is decisively rotating it toward 
equipment, automation, controls, digital industrial infrastructure, 
and frontier manufacturing. The clearest proof is that China's equip-
ment capex  is materially outperforming headline investment in the 
past few years and has sustained into 2026. 
We estimate equipment capex as a share of nominal GDP will rise to 
approximately 9% by 2035 under the China Industrial 5.0 scenario. 
This implies a 10-year average of roughly 7.5%, above the 2016-25 
average of 6.0% but below the 8.5% average recorded in 2006-15. 
The increase reflects (1) greater investment in intelligent equipment 
to achieve higher levels of factory autonomy, and (2) an accelerated 
shift in manufacturing cost structures from labor expenses (opex) to 
capital investment, driven by an aging population and rising labor 
costs, which improve the return on equipment investment. As a 
result, we expect equipment capex to grow at a CAGR of 8.7% 
during 2026-35, accelerating from 4.6% in 2016-25.
The next capex cycle will be structurally skewed toward high-end 
equipment and frontier technologies. In recent years, China's high-


[Page 47]
M
 BluePaper
Morgan Stanley Research
47
tech manufacturing value-added growth has consistently outpaced 
overall industrial value-added growth. This suggests that the next 
phase of industrial investment is unlikely to resemble a broad-based 
rebound in traditional industries. Instead, investment is likely to con-
centrate in advanced equipment, robotics, industrial AI, electronics, 
and precision components. While narrower than previous industrial 
upcycles, this investment wave should be higher quality, more inno-
vation-intensive, and more supportive of emerging manufacturing 
supply chains.

3. Security priority 
In this cycle security has become a capex accelerator in its own right. In a more fragmented and increasingly multipolar world, capex is no longer 
being determined only by demand growth or near-term ROI; it is also being pulled forward by the need for resilience, sovereignty, self-suffi-
ciency, energy security (green transition after energy shocks). 
Exhibit 60: We expect China's semiconductor self-sufficiency 
ratio to reach 32% by 2028
Source: Gartner, WSTS, Morgan Stanley Research (e) estimates.
Exhibit 61: China’s push toward energy independence is 
becoming increasingly critical in the wake of renewed geopolitical 
disruptions
Source: Morgan Stanley Research


[Page 49]
M

Morgan Stanley Research
49
We place the US$12tn (approximately Rmb80tn) incremental capex TAM under China's Industry 5.0 into three layers: (1) 
Infrastructure, (2) Factory upgrades, and (3) Resilience/leadership new capacity from global market share gain. Industry 5.0 is 
ultimately about embedding intelligence into the physical economy. That said, AI infrastructure and energy are the enabling base, 
factory upgrades is the largest deployment layer, and resilience/leadership is the strategic overlay that makes this cycle longer, 
more policy-anchored, and less like a normal cyclical rebound. From a scale perspective, new capacity and factory upgrades are 
broadly similar, each with capex at ~US$6tn  over 2026-35, followed by  ~US$0.5tn  from infrastructure. 
From a financing perspective, the bank loans will likely remain the largest capital supply source for tangible industrial capex (to 
make up 47% of industrial capex in the  next 10 years, the same as the average mix seen in  the past five years), due to banks' unique 
positioning – wide reach and deep penetration – in industry supply chains.  China's (including HK) capital markets could eventually 
contribute ~15% of industrial capex funding, roughly evenly split across primary the market (PE, VC) and secondary market (IPO, 
follow-on) fund raising. 
Capex: How to Finance and Where to Invest? 
We frame the capex opportunity under Industry 5.0 as a three-layer 
stack: Infrastructure, Factory upgrades, and Resilience/leadership. 
The key investment point is that this is not one single spending 
bucket, but a multi-layer buildout in which each layer reinforces the 
next. 
1. Infrastructure (AI infrastructure + energy): the backbone to 
enable Industry 5.0
Infrastructure provides the compute backbone and electricity 
required to run AI, automation, and digitalized manufacturing at 
scale. This includes hyperscaler spending on GPU/ASIC clusters, AI 
servers, networking, edge compute, and data-center buildout, as well 
as the associated power systems, cooling, grid reinforcement, 
storage, 
transformers, 
switchgears, 
and 
backup 
power. 
Infrastructure is not a standalone tech spend or a background utility 
cost; it is the upstream enabling capex that allows intelligence to 
move into the real economy. 
2. Factory upgrades: the core deployment layer
Factory upgrades is the main deployment layer, where intelligence is 
embedded into the installed manufacturing base through intelligent 
equipment, robotics, sensors, edge semis, and industrial software. 
The investment case is compelling because China already has a very 
large manufacturing base, which means the opportunity is not mainly 
greenfield construction, but the retrofitting and upgrading of 
existing factories into smarter, more flexible, and more resilient sys-
tems. This is also the layer where AI translates most directly into pro-
ductivity gains. 


[Page 50]
M
 BluePaper
50
3. Global market share gain / resilience: the strategic overlay that 
extends the cycle
 Resilience/leadership is the strategic overlay that makes the cycle 
broader and longer than a normal industrial recovery, as localization, 
security, standards, and global market-share ambitions all become 
part of the capex equation. This includes spending on semiconduc-
tors, semiconductor equipment and materials, industrial software, 
critical components, defense-related manufacturing, grids and 
storage security, and frontier sectors where China wants to build 
long-term leadership.

Factory upgrades - A large part of the capex 
Industrial AI fundamentally restructures the manufacturing pro-
cess. Industry 5.0 changes the factory workflow from a local optimi-
zation loop into an end-to-end adaptive operating system. In other 
words, the workflow shifts from “data → dashboard → human deci-
sion” to “multi-source data → AI reasoning → governed execution → 
continuous learning,” making the system more adaptive, predictive 
and autonomous.
• In the Industrial 4.0 model, workflow is still largely linear and 
factory-bound: machine data are collected, displayed on 
dashboards, analyzed, then handed back to engineers or 
operators for decision-making and local process adjustment. 
That architecture improves visibility and efficiency, but 
humans remain the key decision node and the optimization 
target is still mostly the individual process or the single fac-
tory. 
• Industry 5.0 extends the loop across the full value chain. 
Market demand, design data, supplier data, machine data, 
quality data, energy/carbon data, field-use data and even 
recycling data are fed into a digital twin or AI model, which can 
generate governed recommendations or automated adjust-
ments, execute with human supervision, and then learn con-
tinuously from outcomes. 


[Page 51]
M
 BluePaper
Morgan Stanley Research
51
Exhibit 63: Workflow comparison for Industrial 4.0 and 5.0  
Source: Morgan Stanley Research 
The workflow shift will change the factory architecture. In a cur-
rent manufacturing factory, humans are still embedded in both the 
digital loop and the physical loop: MES / ERP / planning systems 
remain heavily dependent on manual decisions and exception han-
dling, while machines and production lines still rely on operators for 
setup, movement, inspection, and repetitive tasks. In an Industry 5.0 
“AI Agent Factory,” that architecture, in our view, will shift to a three-
layer model of Perception, Reasoning, and Action. The perception 
layer captures real-time factory data through sensors, machine 
vision, connectivity, and edge compute; the conception layer trans-
lates that data into decisions through industrial models, planning / 
optimization engines, digital twins, and agent orchestration; and the 
action layer executes through smart equipment, robots, AMRs, and 
eventually humanoids. The key implication is that once factory oper-
ations move toward closed-loop sensing, reasoning, and execu-
tion, both software and hardware need to be upgraded together. 
This shift supports a meaningful step-up in higher equipment com-
plexity and software content per factory, reduces reliance on manual 
labor and drives a broader migration of factory economics from labor 
spend toward technology and automation investment.


[Page 52]
M
 BluePaper
52
Exhibit 64: We expect intelligent equipment and smart robots penetration will further accelerate in an AI agent factory in the Industrial 5.0 
era
Agents to become the orchestration layer: Instead of multiple isolated islands, AI can coordinate layering these systems, translating a design 
change, customer order or plant exception into coordinated action rather than leaving those hand-offs to humans. The orchestration layer 
leverages a toolbox of specialized agents across the value chain. In practice, that means horizontal agents coordinating workflows such as 
production planning, inventory and logistics across the enterprise, while vertical agents operate inside engineering, quality, maintenance and 
factory execution.
Exhibit 65: AI Agent in Horizontal and Vertical Layers
Source: McKinsey & Company, Global Lighthouse Network.

We are at the inflection point, the adoption of AI is accelerating: 
According to the Manufacturing Leadership Council's Industrial AI 
Survey 2026, more than 70% of manufacturers surveyed are cur-
rently using GenAI products (up 24ppt from 2024), as well as strong 
(potential) adoption of Agentic AI. Nearly 90% are planning to 
increase GenAI usage in the next two years. 
Under Industry 5.0 we anticipate US$4.5tn (approximately 
Rmb30tn)  incremental equipment opportunity for factory 
upgrades. Industry 5.0 should see a  rotation of investment towards 
high-end equipment and emerging-frontier capex, as higher factory 
autonomy requires more intelligent equipment. We assume: 1) total 
equipment capex as a percentage of total nominal GDP to continue 
increasing to ~9% in 2035 vs. ~6% in 2025, implying a 10-year average 
of ~7.5% in 2026-35, higher than an average of ~6.0% in 2016-25 
while slightly below 8.5% in 2006-15; this  suggests an equipment 
capex growth acceleration to ~9.0% CAGR  in 2026-35,  vs. +4.6% in 
2016-25 and only ~1.6% without Industry 5.0 in 2026-35. This trans-
lates to ~Rmb45tn equipment capex uplift; 2) equipment capex for 
factory upgrades as a percentage of total equipment capex is ~70% 
based on historical patterns, suggesting a TAM of about Rmb30tn.

Intelligent equipment, robotics and automation value pool will 
grow exponentially. The physical layer of the factory becomes more 
modular, more adaptive, and more AI-native. For equipment, we 
believe the upgrade path is from traditional equipment to intelligent 
equipment with embedded AI functions. In practice, that means more 
cameras, machine vision, force / torque sensors, environmental sen-
sors, controllers, communication modules, and edge semiconduc-
tors embedded into the machine itself, allowing the equipment to 
become more adaptive, self-diagnosing, and responsive to variable 
production conditions. The most direct beneficiaries should there-
fore be robotics and smart equipment as well as automation vendors. 
Robotics will penetrate broadly and be more versatile. We believe 
the robotics penetration will accelerate beyond traditional industrial 
arms toward cobots, AGVs / AMRs, and selected humanoids, which 
are better suited for flexible automation, mixed-model production, 
intralogistics, inspection, and selected assembly workflows. Our 
global robotics TAM model suggests a US$2.5tn (Rmb18tn) TAM 
combined for the China market during 2026-35, with 10-year CAGR 
at 30%. Without Industry 5.0, we anticipate the China TAM will grow 
at 15% CAGR, in-line with the historical robotics market growth trend 
– this implies a ~US$1.5tn (~Rmb10tn) robotics value uplift from 
Industry 5.0.

software was mainly placed above the field-device and control 
layers, including SCADA/HMI, MES/MOM, and enterprise systems, 
and was primarily used for monitoring, reporting, scheduling, and 
supervisory control. In the Industry 5.0 model, software is no longer 
just a dashboard or support tool sitting on top of the factory. It 
becomes the intelligence layer that connects perception, reasoning, 
and execution. As factories move from the old workflow of “data → 
dashboard → operator decision” toward “data → model / agent → 
governed action,” software’s role rises from a supporting function to 
core production infrastructure.
This shift should drive both a larger software TAM and higher soft-
ware value capture. On the TAM side, factory intelligence creates 
new and expanding software categories, including industrial data 
platforms, digital twins, AI training and simulation, AI orchestration, 
production optimization, predictive maintenance, energy manage-
ment, industrial cybersecurity, edge management, virtual PLC / soft 
control, and eventually agent-based factory management. These 
tools are required across the full factory lifecycle: digital twins and 
simulation help design and validate production lines before deploy-
ment; MES/MOM and AI scheduling optimize throughput, inventory, 
labor, and power usage during production; machine vision, predictive 
maintenance, and edge-control software improve yield and uptime at 
the equipment layer; and industrial DataOps platforms connect sen-
sors, PLCs, SCADA, MES, ERP, and cloud-based AI models. 
China's industrial software was a ~Rmb340bn market in 2025, 
according to MIIT, representing ~7% of manufacturing equipment 
capex, vs. ~3% in 2018, as it was often treated as an add-on to hard-
ware. Without the AI-driven factory upgrade, we expect this penetra-
tion rate would slowly increase to ~8% by 2035. However, as AI 
Software becomes more valuable in smart 
factories 
Beyond the equipment upgrade itself, we believe the important 
structural change is that factory architecture is shifting from a hard-
ware-led automation pyramid to a software-defined automation eco-
system in the Industry 5.0 era. In the traditional automation pyramid,

factories, digital twins, cloud-edge collaboration, virtual control, intelligent robots, and autonomous production systems scale over the next 
decade, we estimate industrial software’s share will rise toward ~17% in 2035 – this will create around a Rmb7tn increment industrial software 
market in 2026-35, we believe.

[Page 56]

 BluePaper
56
New capacity for both new and existing 
industries 
AI will further amplify China’s manufacturing competitiveness 
with improved productivity…  China already has strong advantages 
in supply-chain depth, engineering talent, manufacturing scale and 
ability to iterate quickly. With AI, these advantages should become 
more powerful, allowing China to improve productivity and further 
move up the global manufacturing value chain. We believe that the 
share of China's manufacturing sector in its GDP could be maintained 
at the current level of ~25% over the next decade, in-line with 15th 
FYP's guidance to  “keep the share of manufacturing in the national 
economy at an appropriate level”. Besides continued policy support 
for manufacturing upgrades, this would also be backed by two fac-
tors: 
• Industry 5.0 could offset the demographic drag that would 
normally dilute manufacturing. Conventional wisdom 
holds that an aging population lifts the services share, as a 
shrinking workforce pulls resources out of goods production 
and the elderly consume more services. For China, both legs 
bite less. On the supply side, AI-enabled factory retrofits, 
robotics, smart equipment and industrial software substitute 
directly for the missing labor – China already leads Germany 
and Japan on robot density and holds the world's largest 
robot stock. On the demand side, China's manufacturing is 
anchored by rising global market share and a decade-long 
domestic capex supercycle – demand streams that are far 
less exposed to the domestic aging-driven tilt toward ser-
vices.
• Manufacturing should become more value-add intensive, 
not just larger in volume. Faster factory productivity would 
ordinarily compress the nominal manufacturing share, as 
cheaper goods lose weight against costlier services. China 
could escape this trap over the medium term by broadening 
its technology-intensive frontiers – advanced equipment, 
automation, robotics, semiconductor tools and industrial 
software – where unit value-add and pricing power rise fast 
enough to more than offset the deflationary pull. Indeed, 
Korea, Germany and Japan have shown this pattern: econo-
mies that have automated and specialized have kept manu-
facturing share elevated, despite an aging population. 
Without Industry 5.0, we estimate China's manufacturing share in 
GDP would gradually decline in the next decade to 20%. The differ-
ence between these two scenarios implies roughly Rmb70tn of man-
ufacturing value-add uplift over the next decade. 
…together with a higher manufacturing capex intensity.  
Historically, China’s manufacturing capex intensity has continued to 
rise –  manufacturing capex as a share of manufacturing value-add 
increased from 30%+ in the 2000s, to 40%+ in the 2010s, and close 
to 50% into  2020s. This, in our view, also reflects the structural 
upgrade of China’s manufacturing base from labour-intensive pro-
duction toward capital- and technology-intensive production. In the 
Industry 5.0 era, each unit of incremental output will require more 
advanced equipment, automated production lines, robotics, smart 
logistics, precision machinery, testing equipment, power systems, 
digital infrastructure and AI-enabled factory systems, and this should 
structurally lift capex intensity. We assume China’s manufacturing 
capex as a percentage of manufacturing GDP  will continue to rise 
mildly from ~49% in 2025 to ~52% in 2035 with Industry 5.0. This, 
combined with the Rmb70trn Industry 5.0-induced manufac-
turing value-add uplift, means that new capex expansion could 
reach ~US$6tn (or ~Rmb40tn) over the next decade.

How industrial capex is funded in China 
historically
Our financing analysis focuses on tangible industrial capex, which 
accounts for ~85% of total industrial investment and captures the 
project-based spending most directly supported by bank credit and 
other funding sources. Intangible investment is less readily mapped to 
specific funding channels, making tangible capex the more relevant 
and measurable basis for this analysis. 
We estimate external funds consistently accounted 
for 60%-70% of annual industrial capex between 
2020 and 2025. 
Bank loans, in particular mid-to-long term loans lent to industrial sec-
tors, are the single biggest funding source, which represented 50% 
of total funding in 2025. The gross origination each year is based on 
the PBOC disclosed industrial loan balance and assuming an average 
three year tenor. Notably, mid-to-long term industrial loans origina-
tion stayed at around Rmb10tn a year from 2023 to 2025.
The primary and secondary market at its peak in 2021 supplied 13% 
of industrial capex funding, which subsequently moderated to mid-
single digits in 2024 and 2025 following a sharp cool down in fund-
raising in A shares. Looking ahead, as capital intensity rises in the cur-
rent innovation cycle, we see the capital markets' role in funding 
innovation and industrial capex to rebound steadily, especially in 
areas like semis, high-end manufacturing, robotics, etc., which sit at 
the forefront of the industrial upgrade or have been identified by the 
government as chokepoints in technology.
Fiscal funds have made up about 10% of industrial capex funding in 
recent years. This included:
• Fiscal cash subsidies: We estimate the subsidy rate based on 
reported government subsidies divided by gross profit of 
listed firms. Then we apply the ratio to industrial value-add.
• Land subsidies: This is based on an IMF study where it esti-
mates the land subsidies (measured by the discount when 
government grant industrial lands compared to market 
value) are around 0.5% of annual GDP.
• Fiscal-led investments and others: This includes in-budget 
fiscal fund investments as well as major national industrial 
project developments backed by special government bonds 
in recent years.

Internal funds account for 30-40% of industrial 
capex – likely a substantial portion of overall indus-
trial firms' annual profits
In 2025, we estimate industrial firms' own funds contributed to 
Rmb6.7tn of capex. This is likely a substantial share of overall indus-
trial firms' profits in the year: NBS reported above-scale industrial 
profit of Rmb7tn plus profit at smaller scale industrial firms (not in 
official statistics) plus profit at many smaller firms along the supply 
chains, such as wholesale trade. 
Opportunities arise from funding China's 
Industry 5.0 
Driven by China's Industry 5.0, our industrial analysts expect capex to 
rise at 6.9% CAGR from 2025 to 2035, reaching Rmb40.1tn by 2035e. 
This will create strong demand for capital in the next 10 years to be 
served by various financial firms. 


[Page 59]
M
 BluePaper
Morgan Stanley Research
59
In particular, we expect bank loans will remain the largest capital 
supply source for industrial capex, due to banks' unique positioning 
– wide reach and deep penetration – in industry supply chains. We 
forecast bank loans to make up 47% of industrial capex in the next 10 
years, the same as the average mix seen in the past five years. 
This will translate into 6.3% 10 year CAGR for industrial mid-long 
term loans. As overall industrial loans make up slightly over one-fifth 
of total credits, we see the loan demand from Industry 5.0 to support 
overall credit growth to maintain at a level around 4-7% over the next 
10 years.
Note that although we show that the growth rate of industrial mid-
long term loans will pick up linearly in the next 10 years, ending at 
~9% by 2035, this is for illustration purposes only. In fact we believe 
the growth will most likely be uneven and cyclical, which is typical for 
industrial loans, with the average annual growth rate staying at 6-7%. 
An uneven growth curve is also more desirable as it allows periodic 
recalibration of credit allocation and risk digestion.
On the other hand, we expect China's (including HK) capital markets 
could eventually contribute around 15% of industrial capex funding, 
compared to the previous peak of ~13% in 2021. This will be roughly 
evenly split across fund raising in primary markets (PE, VC) and sec-
ondary markets (IPO, follow-on). We expect industrial mix in sec-
ondary market fund raising to temporarily rise to 64.5% by 2028, 
from 47.9% in 2025, before moderating to ~57% of total by 2035. 
This is because we think industrial upgrades should also drive income 
growth which could bring more listing opportunities in the service 
and consumer sectors over time, which typically offer higher ROE 
and are also appreciated by capital markets.

Industrial Margins and ROE Upward Trajectory 
Industrial enterprise margins will be in an 
upward trajectory
We anticipate the industrial profit margin to sequentially improve 
to ~8% in 2035 from ~5% in 2025, even slightly higher than the 
2010-11 peak, on the following key drivers:
1. China's investment rotates from downstream scale indus-
tries into foundational layers – China is progressing in foun-
dational innovation – such as upstream materials, 
semiconductor equipment, precision machinery, industrial 
software, sensors, motion controls and power semiconduc-
tors – which have higher margins than the downstream and 
assembly manufacturers.
2. The overcapacity issue to be resolved gradually amid the 
government's anti-involution initiative – China made 
efforts in key leading industries in last cycle – such as NEVs, 
solar, batteries, wind, etc. Similarly, with previous overca-
pacity cycles of light manufacturing in the 1990s and heavy 
industry in 2010s, margins recovered when the overcapacity 
issue was solved and new industries ramped up. 
3. Moving from domestic competition to global expansion – 
In the globalization phase, leading domestic companies with 
proven cost leadership, manufacturing scale, engineering 
capability and supply-chain control can access larger over-
seas profit pools. 
4. China to gradually lead new 0-1 industries –  In frontier 
areas such as nuclear fusion, commercial space, 6G, 
humanoid robots, quantum technology, next-generation AI 
infrastructure, advanced semiconductors, and new mate-
rials, China has the potential to participate at the beginning 
of the innovation cycle. 


[Page 61]
M
 BluePaper
Morgan Stanley Research
61
China's industrial margin cycle: China’s industrial margin history 
can be viewed as a series of recurring upgrade cycles in which capacity 
expansion initially runs ahead of demand, compressing pricing and 
profitability, before supply-side adjustment and industrial upgrading 
restore returns. 
• 1st upgrade: During the first scale build-out from 1979 to 
1997, rapid industrialization created a large manufacturing 
base, but labor-intensive capacity growth outpaced improve-
ments in productivity and market efficiency, driving margins 
to a trough by the late 1990s. The subsequent restructuring 
and WTO scale-up phase from 1998 to 2007 marked the first 
major recovery: state-owned enterprise reform, the exit of inef-
ficient capacity, stronger utilization, export growth and pro-
ductivity gains allowed margins to rise steadily. 
• 2nd upgrade: The same pattern re-emerged after 2008, when 
investment-led expansion – particularly in heavy industry – 
generated excess capacity and increasingly intense price com-
petition, causing margins to weaken through 2015. 
Supply-side reform from 2016 then supported another 
recovery through de-capacity, industry consolidation, better 
utilization and a gradual shift toward higher-value-add man-
ufacturing.
• 3rd upgrade: The 2020–2025 period represents the latest 
capacity-led phase of this cycle. Large-scale investment in 
electric vehicles, batteries, solar equipment and other 
advanced manufacturing sectors accelerated China’s indus-
trial upgrading and strengthened its global competitiveness, 
but capacity expanded faster than near-term demand in sev-
eral industries. The resulting oversupply and “involution” – 
characterized by aggressive price competition, product 
homogenization and declining returns – contributed to a 
renewed fall in industrial margins. This compression should 
not necessarily be interpreted as a failure of the industrial-up-
grade model; rather, it reflects the familiar transition from 
capacity creation to profit normalization. 
Four key drivers of the upward margin trajectory
1. Industrial structure should become more margin-accretive as 
the investment cycle rotates from downstream scale industries 
into foundational layers. 
China's manufacturing value-added breakdown shows a clear struc-
tural shift over the past three decades that labour-intensive manu-
facturing has declined as a share of total manufacturing value-add, 
while technology-intensive manufacturing has steadily increased and 
now represents more than 30% of the manufacturing mix. China has 
achieved dominance in several technology-intensive sectors such as 
batteries, solar modules and EVs. 
Looking into next decade, we believe China is progressing in founda-
tional innovations, such as upstream materials, semiconductor 
equipment, precision machinery, industrial software, sensors, 
motion controls and power semiconductors. These upstream layers 
are closer to IP, process know-how and bottleneck interfaces; in many 
chains, their gross margins are much higher than downstream 
assembly or commodity manufacturing, as some examples show in 
Exhibit 86 . 
Exhibit 86: In most industries, the mid and upstream margin is higher than the downstream, and there is still plenty of room for more 
localization in mid and upstream manufacturing, to support the margin upward movement, in our view

ization rate, and green pie is upstream localization rate.

Case study
#1. Automobiles 
Downstream auto OEMs bear the full force of model-cycle risk, dealer/channel spending, inventory swings, warranty expense, and 
frequent price cuts in an increasingly crowded NEV market. By contrast, upstream component and system suppliers can sell into 
multiple OEM platforms, reuse IP across customers, and monetize bottleneck capabilities such as glass+, smart cockpit/ADAS 
controllers, lightweight structures, steering systems, thermal management and safety electronics. According to the data from 
selected listed China auto-parts leaders and OEMs, auto parts suppliers have structurally higher profitability (4-6% higher) than 
downstream OEMs. 
The reason behind this is where technology and customer stickiness sit, in our view. A smart window, ADAS domain controller, 
chassis system, battery enclosure or steering module is not a generic input. It is designed into the vehicle architecture, qualified 
over long cycles, and often carries safety, software, materials or process know-how. Once installed across several platforms, the 
supplier can compound scale without absorbing the full OEM price war. The auto chain also links to globalization – parts suppliers 
can follow Chinese OEMs overseas first, then penetrate global customers and add aftermarket, spare-parts, calibration, 
maintenance and local engineering services – more recurring and less price-destructive than vehicle assembly alone.

2. Overcapacity issue to be resolved gradually amid Chinese gov-
ernment's anti-involution initiative
China's anti-involution policy is a significant recalibration of the 
country's economic development model, aiming for a better balance 
between growth, innovation, and sustainability. While some effects 
are already visible, the full impact and success of these policies are 
expected to unfold over 2026 and beyond, with particular attention 
on sector-level reforms and market consolidation, supporting 
blended margin improvement. For example: 
• Autos: Tighter scrutiny of price wars has curtailed OEMs’ 
ability to respond to weaker demand through aggressive dis-
counting or loss-leading strategies. This year, regulators and 
industry bodies have intensified efforts to suppress involu-
tion-style competition, pushing the sector toward more 
orderly, value-based growth. Earlier this year, regulators con-
vened leading NEV OEMs and explicitly called for restraint 
against disorderly price wars. Collectively, these measures 
have constrained pricing flexibility, leaving automakers with 
limited scope to offset subsidy reductions or stimulate 
demand through promotions.


[Page 63]
M
 BluePaper
Morgan Stanley Research
63
• Solar: Chinese officials are now deploying multiple measures 
to dismantle price involution, including setting price floors 
and encouraging industry consolidation. While solar names 
are still struggling with significant loss-making and net debt 
positions, recent initiatives have led to recovery in industry 
ASPs, 2025-26. 
Case study
#1 Steel Industry – Supply-side Reform as a Historical Precedent
China's steel industry provides one of the clearest historical precedents for assessing the potential impact of today's anti-involution 
policies. Following years of aggressive capacity expansion after 2008, industry supply significantly outpaced downstream demand, 
resulting in persistent price competition and a sharp deterioration in profitability. Average gross margins across leading listed steel 
producers declined steadily between 2011 and 2015, with several companies approaching break-even levels despite relatively stable 
shipment volumes.
The launch of China's supply-side structural reform in 2016 marked a turning point. Capacity reduction, environmental inspections 
and the elimination of inefficient production materially tightened industry supply. Unlike previous demand-driven recoveries, the 
improvement in profitability was primarily driven by supply discipline rather than accelerating end-market demand.
Historical gross margin trends illustrate this structural change clearly. As excess capacity exited the market, industry gross margins 
expanded materially over the following three years while price volatility moderated. The recovery was sustained despite only 
modest growth in apparent steel demand, suggesting that improved industry discipline was the key driver of profitability.
We believe this historical experience provides a useful framework for evaluating China's current anti-involution campaign. Although 
industry fundamentals differ across sectors, the steel sector demonstrates that government-led supply rationalisation can lead to 
multi-year margin recovery when excessive competition and inefficient capacity are effectively removed.
• Wind: Our China utility team expects robust demand and 
ongoing margin recovery along the value chain to follow 
years of anti-involution. We expect limited wind turbine ASP 
downside amid the anti-involution effort, and we hold a posi-
tive view on key component names given a clear margin 
expansion runway, improving industry dynamics, and export 
tailwinds in the EU market.

3. Leading Chinese industrial companies are moving from 
domestic competition to global expansion.
In the globalization phase, companies with proven cost leadership, 
manufacturing scale, engineering capability and supply-chain control 
can access larger overseas profit pools, where pricing is often less 
competitive than in China and where customers may value reliability, 
technology, service and integrated solutions. As Chinese leaders 
shift from product exports to localized production, global channels, 
spare parts, maintenance and financing, their revenue mix moves 
closer to system and lifecycle economics. For some industries, 
although more Chinese companies expand overseas over time and 
create some incremental competition, overall margins remain fairly 
good with broader regional market exposure.

Case study
#1 Construction Machinery – Overseas expansion drives margin uplift
The construction machinery case – across Sany, Zoomlion, XCMG, Hangcha and Dingli – clearly shows that overseas markets 
consistently deliver higher gross margins than domestic markets, as Chinese equipment companies transition from price-based 
competition at home to value-based competition abroad, supported by stronger pricing power, service monetization, and less 
fragmented industry structures. Chinese construction machinery market competition has intensified since the mid-2010s with 
rapidly increasing localization rate, characterized by intense competition, price transparency, and frequent capacity cycles, leading 
to relatively compressed gross margins. In contrast, overseas markets tend to exhibit less fragmented competition, stronger pricing 
discipline, and higher value placed on reliability, service, and lifecycle performance. As a result, leading Chinese OEMs have started 
to accelerate overseas business operations and market share gains since 2020. 
This margin differential is not merely cyclical but structural. As Chinese companies move beyond simple equipment exports toward 
localized production + service + ecosystem exports, they capture a larger share of the value chain. Overseas revenue increasingly 
includes higher-margin components such as after-sales services, spare parts, maintenance contracts, and financing solutions. 
Importantly, construction machinery is inherently service-heavy – lifecycle maintenance and utilization optimization can contribute 
a significant portion of total profitability. 
4. Chinese companies are increasingly positioned to lead new 0–1 
industries directly, rather than entering only after global leaders 
have already defined the market.
In frontier areas such as nuclear fusion, commercial space, 6G, 
humanoid robots, quantum technology, next-generation AI infra-
structure, synthetic biology, advanced semiconductors, new mate-
rials and next-generation energy storage, China has the potential to 
participate at the beginning of the innovation cycle. If Chinese compa-
nies can help define products, standards, ecosystems and supply 
chains from day one, they may capture global leadership economics 
earlier and avoid the immediate commoditization pressure seen in

many localized sectors. In our view, the combination of globalization 
of existing competitive industries and 0–1 breakthroughs in future 
industries creates a credible path for Chinese industrial margins to 
improve over the coming decade. 
• The last decade was largely a “localization-led volume 
growth, margin compression” cycle for Chinese industrial 
companies. In many large sectors, the highest-margin 0–1 
innovation phase was initially led by overseas pioneers. 
Chinese companies then entered through import substitu-
tion and localization, scaling rapidly on the back of cost 
advantages, supply-chain depth and execution speed. 


[Page 66]
M
 BluePaper
66
However, as localization deepened, more domestic players 
entered, capacity expanded, product differentiation nar-
rowed and pricing power weakened. This created the familiar 
“involution” pattern: strong volume growth but declining 
industry gross margins. New energy vehicles, photovoltaics, 
lithium batteries, wind power, consumer electronics, home 
appliances, steel, cement, textiles and LED lighting are all 
examples of sectors that have experienced some version of 
this cycle in China. 
• In next decade, Chinese companies are increasingly posi-
tioned to lead new 0–1 industries directly, rather than 
entering only after global leaders have already defined the 
market. China is currently investing in frontier areas such as 
nuclear fusion, commercial space, 6G, humanoid robots, 
quantum technology, next-generation AI infrastructure, syn-
thetic biology, advanced semiconductors, new materials and 
next-generation energy storage, China has the potential to 
participate at the beginning of the innovation cycle. If Chinese 
companies can define products, standards, ecosystems and 
supply chains from day one, they may capture global leader-
ship economics earlier and avoid the immediate commoditi-
zation pressure seen in many localized sectors.

Case study
#1 CATL 
CATL is one of the clearest Chinese examples of moving beyond localization into global category leadership. CATL ranked No.1 
globally in power-battery shipments  for nine consecutive years from 2017 to 2025, with 39.2% global share in 2025; it also ranked 
No.1 in energy-storage battery shipments for five consecutive years from 2021 to 2025, with a 30.4% global share in 2025. This 
leadership matters because battery leaders influence product benchmarks in range, safety, charging speed, lifecycle cost and 
energy-storage reliability.
The financial outcome also supports our thesis that 0–1 innovation and scale leadership can create structurally superior 
profitability. CATL had 23.8% gross margin and 21.9% ROE in 2024, both materially above the peer average. This is notable because 
the battery industry has been through a severe price-war and localization cycle, yet CATL has maintained returns far above most 
battery peers.
CATL’s innovation advantage is multi-layered rather than based on one product cycle. It has core technological advantages across 
battery materials, battery systems and recycling, as well as innovation in material systems, system structure, green manufacturing 
and business models. Its product matrix includes LFP, mid-nickel and high-nickel ternary batteries, sodium-ion batteries, M3P 
batteries and condensed batteries, with performance features such as fast charging, long life, long range, safety and wide-
temperature adaptability. This is the type of technology and system-level innovation that can protect margins even when an 
industry becomes more localized and competitive, in our view.

#2: Fuyao Glass 
Fuyao Glass is a different but equally useful Chinese case. Fuyao is the No.1 global automotive-glass player with about 37% share in 
2025. It is not a pure frontier-technology company like CATL; instead, it is a specialized global manufacturing leader in automotive 
glass, where the moat comes from long OEM qualification cycles, quality consistency, safety standards, global delivery and co-
development with automakers. 
The financial comparison shows the value of that specialization. Fuyao had 35.9% gross margin and 22.4% ROE in 2024, well above 
the peer average in the past few years. This return gap reflects the difference between an engineered, safety-critical, OEM-qualified 
autos component and broader glass peers that often have more commodity, construction or cyclical exposure.
Fuyao’s innovation is mainly product/process innovation and customer-solution innovation. The company benefits from functional 
glass trends such as panoramic canopies, smart dimming glass, antenna-integrated glass, HUD-compatible windshields, acoustic 
and thermal functions, encapsulation and modular assembly. In a mature industry, this kind of innovation can still create “0–1-like” 
economics because it turns a commodity input into a qualified system component embedded in global OEM platforms.

ROE Improvement under an efficiency-driven 
revolution
MSCI China's ROE has tracked each industrial stage closely. During 
Industrial 3.0 (2000s–mid 2010s), ROE rose dramatically, from just 
~4% in 2000 to a peak of 16-17% in 2010-2011. This was the golden 
era of capital-intensive, scale-driven growth, with high-ROE banks 
and energy companies dominating the index. ROE then began to fade 
through the later part of this stage, settling to 13-14% by the mid-
2010s as the capex supercycle matured.
During Industrial 4.0 (mid 2010s-early 2020s), the decline continued 
– gradually at first, hovering around 12-13% through 2017-2019, 
before falling more sharply to 10-11% by the early 2020s. This 
reflected both the natural slowdown of the investment-driven 
growth model and a compositional shift in the index – away from 
high-ROE Financials and toward newer tech and advanced manufac-
turing names still in their heavy investment phase.
Since entering Industry 5.0 (2023-present), there are early signs of 
ROE stabilization. MSCI China's ROE has edged up from a trough of 
~9–10% in mid-2023 to around 11%, modest, but directionally mean-
ingful after a decade of erosion. Several forces are driving this: 
• Strong export competitiveness, is supporting top-line 
growth and asset turnover; 
• The government's "anti-involution" campaign is targeting 
excess capacity and destructive price competition across key 
industries, which should help restore pricing power and mar-
gins over time; 
• Corporate self-help, including cost discipline, share buy-
backs, and higher dividends are improving capital efficiency 
from the bottom up. 
The combination of these forces, exports, supply-side discipline, and 
AI-driven efficiency, could mark a structural ROE re-rating for China 
equities.


[Page 69]
M
 BluePaper
Morgan Stanley Research
69
Looking forward, unlike the prior two cycles, our China strategy team believes this round is more about an efficiency-driven revolution, 
which could help lift both major AI-related sectors and overall index ROE. The team expects Energy (up 6-7%), Materials (up 2-3%), 
Industrials (up 2-3%), and Information Technology (up 8-9%) to all see a round of ROE improvement by 2028, contributing around 2% to MSCI 
China’s overall index ROE.

What Does Industry 5.0 Mean for China's Long-term 
Economic Outlook?  
Industry 5.0 should lift China's potential GDP by ~3.5% by 2035, partially cushioning the demographic drag. We now raise our 
China's potential growth forecasts by 30bp to 4.3% for 2028-30 and 50bp to 3.9% for 2031-35 – enough to meet Beijing's 2020-35 
GDP-doubling target. The near-term GDP impact will likely stay broadly neutral, as excess capacity, tech bottlenecks, and J-curve 
labor adjustments offset early gains, before turning more positive from the late 2020s as deployment broadens. Overcapacity 
should also prove more manageable than in the past five years, supported by the anti-involution policy and a global capex 
supercycle. Yet a decisive reflation remains challenging, given Beijing's continued supply-centric policy framework and calibrated 
rebalancing. We expect the GDP deflator to move only gradually towards 0.5-1.0% lowflation territory from 2028, as housing 
stabilizes, capacity is absorbed and margin gains feed into wages, while renewed deflation remains a near-term downside risk if 
rapid household deleveraging coincides with pro-cyclical fiscal tightening.
Revising up our long-term potential GDP 
forecasts for China
Industry 5.0 could become an important offset to China’s aging 
demographics, lifting the country’s potential GDP level by around 
3.5% by 2035 (vs. a no-Industry 5.0 path). The uplift comes from: 1) 
the Industry 5.0-enabled manufacturing capex supercycle, which is 
likely to be a capex-heavy industrial transformation across AI infra-
structure, power systems, robotics, sensors, industrial software, and 
factory retrofits, outweighing structurally weaker capex in property 
and traditional infrastructure sectors. This should lift total capex 
input's contribution to GDP by 1.5pp cumulatively over the next 
decade; (2) a 3pp cumulative increase in TFP over the next decade, 
with broader deployment of AI across the economy enhancing deci-
sion making, optimizing resource allocation, improving coordination 
across supply chains and business functions, and enabling new pro-
duction and service models. These should far outweigh the AI labor 
replacement effect (~1ppt cumulative drag to 2026-35 GDP growth).
 Industry 5.0’s impact on potential growth is likely to be broadly 
neutral in 2026-27:  As discussed in Industry 5.0 –  A Long Capex 
Supercycle , while we expect a US$12tn (~Rmb80trn) Industry 
5.0-enabled incremental investment over the next decade (vs. a non-
Industry 5.0 path), a broad-based manufacturing capex boom would 
not take place from day 1, given still pervasive excess capacity and 
Beijing's efforts to reduce repetitive buildout across regions/sectors 
under the "unified national market" initiative. The key capex driver in 
the near term would be AI computing, data centers, and power grid 
infrastructure, which would have strong growth (15-20%) yet small 
size (only accounting for ~2% in total investment). In particular, 
China’s ample existing power capacity reduces the need for a 
US-style surge in power-infrastructure investment, while data-
center deployment remains constrained by the availability and per-
formance of domestically produced advanced chips in the near term, 
and its private sector-led growth model means that capex  would 
hinge on chip supply, utilization and monetization visibility,  rather 
than administrative targets.  We thus see a modest increase in the net 
capital input contribution to headline GDP, at 0.1-0.2pp. 
Meanwhile, the early phase of AI and automation adoption could 
create transitional pressure on the labor market, especially in rou-
tine, repetitive, and junior-level tasks, This is consistent with the 
classic "J-curve" pattern of general-purpose technology diffusion, 
where labor displacement and organizational adjustment costs tend 
to materialize ahead of broad-based productivity gains, as enter-
prises must first restructure workflows, reskill workers, and recon-
figure production systems before the full efficiency benefits emerge. 
As a result, modest gains from capital input and early productivity 
improvements are likely to be largely offset by labor-market adjust-
ment costs, leaving the net boost to headline potential growth lim-
ited in 2026‑27.
The contribution should turn more positive from the late 2020s, 
as Industry 5.0 shifts from initial infrastructure build-out to 
broader factory deployment and commercialization. On the capex 
side, capacity utilization ratio shall return to a healthier level with 
structurally stronger exports and more disciplined investment, and 
Industry 5.0 would shift from initial AI infrastructure build-out to 
broader factory deployment and commercialization. A virtuous cycle 


[Page 71]
M
 BluePaper
Morgan Stanley Research
71
would be formed: higher efficiency supports competitiveness and 
profit enhancement, which justifies further equipment investment 
and data accumulation. This would lift total annual capital input con-
tribution to GDP by 0.2-0.3pp over the medium term. At the same 
time, an  AI-induced productivity boost would gradually outweigh its 
drag on labor inputs, as  firms move from substitution to human-AI 
collaboration, with workers redeployed into higher-value roles such 
as system integration, robotics operations, data engineering, and 
equipment maintenance. 
We thus revise up our 2028-30 annual potential GDP forecast by 
30bps to 4.3% CAGR,  and see a 50bps uplift to 2031-35 annual 
potential GDP growth, to 3.9% CAGR. Growth at this level would be 
sufficient to reach Beijing's target to double 2020 GDP level by 2035.

Overcapacity issues should be more 
contained than in 2021-25
China’s 3D challenges (debt, demographics, and deflation) have 
resurfaced since 2022, as the structural housing downturn and local 
government deleveraging met with a policy boost to industrial 
supply, not consumption demand, resulting in excess capacity prob-
lems. To be sure, China’s supply-led model remains prone to overca-
pacity. But we believe the magnitude could be reduced in our base 
case, based on three factors: 
• Structurally stronger external demand: Our Asia econo-
mist  believes that the region is entering its strongest multi-
year industrial cycle since the mid-2000s, driven by the 
interactions between AI and AI-related infrastructure, energy 
transition, defence, and a rise in industrial capex. China should 
be a prime beneficiary: it holds 70-90% of global capacity in 
the "new trio" (EVs, batteries, solar), positioning it to capture 
structurally stronger global energy-transition demand, while 
the AI up-cycle offers a multi-year runway for its exporters of 
hardware, AI-related equipment, robotics, where China keeps 
gaining share. Crucially, the demand  is set to  widen from tech 
to non-tech sectors amid broader industrial upgrading – 
helping  ease China's excess capacity problems in more sec-
tors. 
• Healthier capex demand under Industry 5.0: China's shift 
toward Industry 5.0 tilts investment toward upgrading its 
existing manufacturing base rather than purely greenfield 
expansion, by embedding AI, robotics, sensors, and software 
into installed factories to lift productivity. As this demand is 
largely retrofit- and replacement-driven, it skews toward 
higher value-add per unit rather than low-end volume. This 
would help absorb the supply glut, and sharpen China's 
global competitiveness. 
• Smarter industrial policy:  Since July 2025, the anti-involu-
tion campaign has increasingly targeted disorderly price com-
petition and the flow of investment and credit into sectors 
with overcapacity. The 15th FYP takes another step ahead by 
promoting "a unified national market" to address redundant 
construction and zero‑sum competition among local govern-
ments. Beijing has advocated differentiated development 
across regions and emphasized the need to prevent “money-
burning” investment in developing future industries. 


[Page 72]
M
 BluePaper
72
But policy-driven economic rebalancing 
remains calibrated
Meaningful economic rebalancing reforms remain the desired 
path for reflation: While stronger exports and  better industrial 
policy  could help improve capital allocation and mitigate excess 
capacity problems, we don't believe they can fix the supply-demand 
imbalance rooted in local government incentives (see Box: The Hefei 
Paradox: Why Smart Industrial Policy Can’t Fix Macro Imbalances). 
We have long-argued that (here, here, and here) China's rebalancing 
and reflation require deep structural reforms to cadre evaluation 
(local officials), fiscal structure, and social safety nets, which would 
reallocate fiscal resources from investment to consumption, and 
help unwind the >Rmb30trn cumulative excess savings since 2018: 
Cadre evaluation reform: Shift verifiable performance metrics 
away from output volume toward household income, consump-
tion, and environmental quality.
Fiscal system reform: Reduce reliance on indirect taxes (e.g., 
VAT) that reward scale expansion, and transition toward direct 
taxes linked to efficiency and profitability. Reorient central-local 
transfers from project-based allocation to public service provi-
sion.
Social welfare reform: Strengthen the safety net to lower pre-
cautionary savings (currently ~33% of disposable income). This 
requires reallocating fiscal resources from investment, 
increasing SOE equity transfers, and building a multi-tier social 
security system.
Yet medium-term policy framework has remained supply-centric: 
Beijing's 2026-30 Five-Year Plan provides explicit, quantifiable inno-
vation and productivity targets, yet support to consumption remains 
qualitative and non–binding. In our view, China's resilience through 
successive shocks – the 2025 tariffs, Middle East tensions since 
2Q26, and the ongoing housing downturn – has likely reinforced poli-
cymakers’ confidence in the prevailing supply-centric policy frame-
work to prioritize tech self-sufficiency and energy security.  In con-
trast, policymakers have continued to view consumption as a 
function of industrial upgrading and employment growth, not a 
policy lever, making direct cash transfers to households challenging. 
While Beijing has increasingly recognized the importance of 
strengthening public services and the social safety net to reduce pre-
cautionary savings, implementation will likely remain gradual, given 
concerns on the near-term multiplier effect on consumption and 
medium-term funding sustainability. 
Structurally weaker fiscal revenue also constrains Beijing's band-
width for meaningful demand support: As it is,  government budget 
revenues and land sales revenues combined have declined from pre-
Covid levels of 27-28% of GDP to 19% today, due to a housing down-
turn-induced land sales slump and deflation-driven tax revenue 
contraction. While there remains ample room for central govern-
ment to increase leverage to support the economy (debt/GDP ratio 
remained low in 2025, at 29%), Beijing has been on the cautious side 
given  concerns on moral hazard and public debt sustainability. This 
explains why policymakers have been hesitant on providing more 
substantial counter-cyclical demand support, and pro-cyclical at 
times (such as a stricter tax collection, dialing down consumption 
support) when public debt resolution is prioritized over short-term 
economic growth. 
This means economic rebalancing efforts will likely remain cali-
brated  in the coming year: Such a supply-centric mindset and fiscal 
constraints have reinforced policymakers' preference for investment 
over consumption, as the former is deemed an asset with future 
returns while the latter is often viewed as shifting demand forward 
at the cost of higher debt for future  generations. This means more 
state resources will continue to be allocated towards energy transi-
tion and frontier technologies – Al, semiconductors, quantum com-
puting, and advanced manufacturing – rather than household 
wallets.

We thus expect continued lowflation over 
the next decade
Deflationary pressures to linger in 2026-27 amid a two-speed 
economy: We view this year's inflation uptick as a false dawn driven 
by geopolitically-induced oil shocks, while the underlying supply-de-
mand imbalance remains severe. The economy continues in an 
entrenched K-shape, with improving exports and sluggish domestic 
demand amid the continued property downturn, household delever-
aging, and slow fiscal rollout. Pass-through from export strength to 
domestic demand has been limited, as the initial driver is tech- and 
capital-intensive, and overcapacity lingers. Yet resilient exports have 
eased the perceived urgency for policy support, resulting in de facto 
fiscal tightening – though we expect this drag to ease this Fall with 
further policy fine-tuning facilitating faster budget rollout.  
 We thus see the GDP deflator slipping from 0.8% in 2026 to 0.2%Y 
in 2027 as oil prices normalize, with risk tilt to the downside – 
should rapid household deleveraging coincide with more aggressive 
pro-cyclical fiscal tightening, deflationary pressures may re-emerge. 
A gradual shift into lowflation territory is possible from 2028, but 
decisive reflation remains unlikely: Given that policy efforts in eco-
nomic rebalancing will likely stay calibrated, a decisive reflation 
towards 2-3% will be challenging. A more probable path is a slow, 
shallow, market-driven shift toward 0.5-1.0% p.a. lowflation from 
2028 onwards, driven by three catalysts:
• Stabilization of housing market in tier 1-2 cities from 2H27, 
as supply contraction and demand adjustment run their 
course – ceasing to be a deflationary drag rather than 
becoming a growth driver.
• A return of industrial capacity utilization to healthier 
levels in next 2-3 years, which would be much longer than 
the 2016-17 cycle (when the ratio quickly rebounded from 
~73% in 2H16 to a healthier level of >76.5% in 2Q17  on a syn-
chronized housing and export recovery and supply-side 
reforms), given the absence of meaningful consumption/
housing stimulus and more pervasive overcapacity problems.
• Better industrial profit margin over the medium term: We 
expect margins to climb  toward ~8% by 2035 from ~5% in 
2025, driven by a rotation up the value chain into higher-
margin foundational layers, the globalization of competitive 
Chinese leaders, and an economy-wide productivity lift from 
Industry 5.0 (for more details, please  see Industrial Margins 
and ROE Upward Trajectory ).

Box: The Hefei Paradox: Why Smart Industrial Policy Can’t Fix Macro Imbalances
Hefei – the city that rescued NIO, backed CXMT, and seeded China’s rise in advanced display manufacturing – provides a prime 
example for the school of thought that smarter industrial policies can solve bottlenecks of emerging industries and foster global 
champions. But even this  city has not escaped overcapacity and deflation. We view Hefei's case as a microcosm of China's broader 
challenge: even with an improved industrial policy, it has only improved investment allocation towards more productive sectors, 
but has not fixed the supply-demand imbalance rooted in local government incentives.
What is Hefei's recipe for success? Hefei’s model works better than traditional local industrial policies because the government 
acts less like a blanket subsidy provider and more like an ecosystem planner and VC investor. The approach included:
• Mobilizing fiscal resources as patient capital to anchor lead firms 
• Promoting public-private co-investment 
• Building supplier networks around them 
• Conducting market-based exits 
• Recycling gains into the next strategic sector
This  approach has helped reduce subsidy-induced market distortion and solved funding bottlenecks at the early stage of emerging 
sector development – when private capital is still hesitant. 
The city has cultivated various national/global champions: It supported BOE in launching China's first sixth-generation TFT-LCD 
display-panel line, turned the rescue of NIO into the development of an NEV hub in the city, and backed CXMT as a 'chain leader' to 
develop a DRAM supply chain when private capital was still in doubt. Today, the city is a major production base across NEVs, 
displays, and appliances, and hosts globally competitive players in PV inverters, energy storage, and DRAM. At the macro level, 
Hefei's real GDP size nearly doubled over the past decade, to Rmb1.4trn in 2025, and its national ranking climbed into the top 18 
(from #24 in 2015). 
But Hefei's recipe is hard to replicate: Its success relied on a rare alignment of timing, bold early bets, local industrial fit, and 
favorable demand cycles that only materialized with a lag. If many regions imitate the same surface tools (large upfront 
government seed capital, anchor-firm recruitment, industrial park buildout), and focus on the same frontier sectors, this may result 
in redundant investment nationwide, compounding the overcapacity problem. 
Indeed, even Hefei is not immune to overcapacity and deflation: While Hefei has outperformed in industrial production and 
exports over the past five years, consumer goods sales lagged and price deflation was more severe than the national average – a 
reflection of overcapacity and constrained corporate earnings despite market-share gains. This reflects China's broader industrial 
challenge: globally competitive supply chains coexisting with compressed profitability and persistent deflation. Even with a 
smarter industrial policy, the shift is in what China invests in, not how much it invests – leaving the supply-demand gap unresolved.

77
 While China has been the world’s largest manufacturing powerhouse, we believe its share in global manufacturing GDP could rise 
further from 28% in 2025 to ~30% by 2035 – a ~2ppt gain. Industry 5.0 should favor China over DMs, given its scale, all-around 
manufacturing ecosystem, and deployment speed, to be reinforced by the 15th Five-Year Plan’s focus on AI+, frontier technologies 
and advanced manufacturing. Future share gains should increasingly come from "complex production at scale" – machinery and 
electronics, EVs and batteries, robotics, industrial automation, high-end equipment and advanced materials. Tariffs may reroute 
supply chains,  but cannot quickly replicate China's industrial depth and know-how; China can therefore capture value not only 
through finished goods, but also by supplying the equipment and components powering manufacturing growth elsewhere. 
China's Global Manufacturing Share: From 28% in 2025 to 
30% by 2035
Why China can maintain its manufacturing 
lead during Industry 5.0
China's nominal manufacturing share decline in 2022-25 under-
stated its real competitiveness: In nominal USD terms, China's share 
of global manufacturing GDP fell 2.1pp to 28% in 2022-25, fueling 
investor concerns over its manufacturing competitiveness amid an 
aging population and rising multipolarity. However, this decline was 
largely a function of domestic deflation and RMB depreciation 
against the US dollar. Adjusted for purchasing power parity and infla-
tion, China's share actually rose 1.6pp to 32%. The underlying gains 
are broad-based and structural: China is now the largest exporter in 
>50% of global product categories (based on HS2), with its share of 
global industrial goods exports rising from 5% in 2001 to ~20% by 
2024. While it sustained dominance in traditional sectors, it has 
steadily expanded into semiconductors, high-end equipment, 
machinery, batteries, EVs and robotics – sectors that are driving the 
current global trade upcycle.

78
We believe China will be able to widen its lead in global manufac-
turing GDP by 2ppt over the next decade:  To be sure, China is not 
the only beneficiary of Industry 5.0. AI-enabled manufacturing will 
reduce labor intensity, improve yield and make smaller-scale local 
production more economical, supporting reshoring/near-shoring in 
selective DM sectors such as semiconductors, batteries, defense and 
medical supply chains. However, this does not mean a broad displace-
ment of China. Industry 5.0 rewards economies that can deploy new 
technologies across large factory networks, convert production data 
into process improvement, and iterate quickly through dense sup-
plier ecosystems and coordinated policy support. In each of these 
dimensions, China's starting point is the advantage – the same indus-
trial base that anchored Industry 4.0 is what now supports industrial 
AI scaling.
• Scale-to-data flywheel: China’s large factory base, con-
nected equipment and industrial internet platforms give it 
more real-world production data to train industrial AI 
models, optimize processes and improve quality control. As 
smart-factory adoption expands from pilots to broader clus-
ters, productivity gains can compound across sectors, helping 
to offset the demographic drag.
• Faster execution through ecosystem depth…  Industrial AI 
creates value only when insights can be translated quickly 
into design changes, process tweaks, component substitu-
tion and capacity reallocation. China's comprehensive indus-
trial coverage and dense supplier clusters shorten this 
feedback loop meaningfully. DM peers – even those with 
strong AI stacks – still face longer supplier qualification, 
higher integration costs and slower ramp-up, in part because 
they are simultaneously trying to rebuild the underlying cap-
ital stock that China already has in place.
• ...and with strong policy support: Beijing’s support  is not 
confined to subsidies, but its ability to identify structural 
demand shifts early and mobilize resources to build capacity 
to meet demand, even ahead of time.  As an example, the  case 
of Hefei – the city that rescued NIO, backed CXMT, and 
seeded China’s rise in advanced display manufacturing – 
shows  how efficient, smart industrial policies can remove 
bottlenecks in emerging industries,  and foster global cham-
pions. The 15th FYP’s focus on AI+, advanced manufacturing, 
robotics, high-end equipment and frontier technologies 
should help move Industry 5.0 from isolated breakthroughs 
to scaled manufacturing productivity gains. China is 
deploying industrial policy to upgrade an already comprehen-
sive manufacturing base, versus the  US and Europe, which are 
rebuilding or defending selected strategic sectors. 
• Lower-cost diffusion of automation: The reshoring argu-
ment assumes robots help high-wage economies offset labor 
costs, but China can deploy the same technologies at a larger 
scale with lower integration cost. Its large domestic market 
for robotics, automation equipment and industrial software 
should accelerate cost-down, customization and broader fac-
tory-level diffusion.
