79
Supply chain diversification will not derail 
China's manufacturing share gains
In our view, supply chain diversification will likely reshape  China's 
role from direct exporter to system-level supplier, rather than dis-
place it. Tariffs can redirect final assembly and trade flows, but sup-
plier networks, engineering capabilities and scale economies are 
much slower to replicate. Our senior global economist, Rajeev Sibal, 
estimates that only ~40% of China-to-US exports can be readily sub-
stituted, while ASEAN and other emerging manufacturing hubs 
remain highly reliant on Chinese machinery, components and produc-
tion know-how. The key test of China+1 is whether recipient coun-
tries build local capital stock, supplier depth and domestic value 
added; otherwise, they will likely remain assembly hubs within a 
China-centered production system. As a case in point, while China’s 
share in US imports has declined from 21.6% in 2017 to 8% currently 
due to higher tariffs, its export market share for the world ex-US 
(accounting for 90% of China's exports) has gone up from 12% to 16%. 
Near term, DM reshoring itself is import-intensive, which would 
benefit Chinese and North Asian suppliers: The current trade cycle 
is unusually narrow, concentrated in AI-related capital goods— pre-
cisely the categories where North Asia holds hard-to-replace 
upstream capacity and China dominates midstream components and 
equipment. Building domestic AI and manufacturing capacity there-
fore requires importing the very inputs it is meant to eventually 
replace: stronger US investment lifts imports of chips, servers, trans-
formers and grid equipment before it lifts domestic output. Given the 
long lead times to build fabs, power infrastructure and supplier eco-
systems from scratch, we expect this front-loaded import intensity 
to persist through the current capex cycle – extending, rather than 
eroding, China's manufacturing share gains outside the US.
For more detailed analysis, please see China Trade in a Global Context

[Page 80]
M
 BluePaper
80
Where can we see more market-share gains? 
We  believe China’s manufacturing sector still has meaningful room 
to gain global share because its export competitiveness has become 
broad-based across value-chain layers and product categories. China 
initially surpassed major exporters in labour-intensive products such 
as textiles, furniture and wood products, in the early 2000s, and 
since the 2010s moved into heavier and more complex categories 
including metals, electrical machinery, chemicals, machinery and 
motor vehicles. China’s share has increased not only in consumption 
goods, but also in capital goods, intermediate goods and raw mate-
rials, suggesting China is increasingly embedded in the global indus-
trial supply chain.
China’s ongoing manufacturing upgrade favors  technology-inten-
sive industries.  China's manufacturing value-added breakdown 
shows a clear structural shift over the past three decades that the 
labour-intensive manufacturing has declined as a share of total man-
ufacturing value added, while technology-intensive manufacturing 
has steadily increased and now represents more than 30% of the 
manufacturing mix. 
China's largest export category is Machinery & Electronics with 
>40% in 2025. The composition of China’s exports reveals that the 
country’s export profile has shifted decisively from textiles and light 
manufacturing to more advanced products that reflect deepening 
industrial capacity. Cars, batteries and advanced shipping have 
become essential pillars of China’s exports, alongside smartphones, 
computers and integrated circuits, indicating deeper upstream capa-
bilities, not just assembly scale.
Accordingly, China’s global share in electronics and machinery has 
increased significantly over time, while vehicles have also acceler-
ated notably in recent years. This suggests China’s manufacturing 
competitiveness is moving from “low-cost production at scale” 
toward “complex production at scale.” As China’s manufacturing mix 
becomes more technology-intensive, its future share gain should 
increasingly come from higher-value categories rather than tradi-
tional low-end exports.
China will continue to lead in high-value industries globally.  China 
has achieved dominance in several  technology-intensive sectors such 
as batteries, solar modules, and EV industries.  These examples show 
how  China’s share-gain playbook works: large domestic demand cre-
ates scale, scale supports supply-chain localization and cost reduc-
tion, and cost reduction plus rapid product iteration drives global 
competitiveness. We believe this model can extend into the next gen-
eration of high-end and emerging manufacturing sectors, including 
robotics, electric machinery, CNC machine tools, semiconductor, 
automation, and advanced materials, among others. This breadth 
matters because China’s future share gains are not dependent on one 
single product cycle; they are supported by a diversified manufac-
turing base, and are expanding into the two ends of the smile curve.

83
In this report, our central thesis is that even though the economy will 
likely remain stuck in lowflation territory with calibrated economic 
rebalancing, China will enter the Industry 5.0 era characterized by 
intelligence, resilience and ecosystem leadership, backed by policy 
support and a global industrial capex supercycle. This should bring 
strong incremental investment demand to make up for structurally 
weaker capex in property and traditional infrastructure sectors, 
enhance labor productivity to offset reduced labor input on aging 
demographics, and strengthen China's position across global manu-
facturing value chains.
Yet, structural transitions are rarely linear. The path from investment 
to productivity, profits and income growth often involves periods of 
adjustment, while shifts in domestic policy and the external environ-
ment can materially alter the pace and quality of the cycle. In our 
view, three medium-term risks stand out:
1. Policy: Cyclically, Beijing tends to withdraw policy support 
when external demand strengthens, but this could risk 
turning into a pro-cyclical tightening if it coincides with 
household deleveraging, continued property downturn, and 
still-weak 
exports 
spillover 
to 
domestic 
demand. 
Structurally, the continued supply-centric policy setup will 
likely keep the economy prone to overcapacity, particularly 
in frontier industries where local governments and firms 
race to secure strategic positions. Both factors could keep 
deflationary risks alive.
2. Geopolitics: Our base case is that China continues to gain 
global manufacturing share despite supply-chain diversifica-
tion, shifting from a final-goods exporter to a system-level 
supplier of production abroad. This will depend on stable ties 
with major non-US partners.  Outbound investment by 
Chinese manufacturers is central to this transition: it  helps 
firms navigate trade barriers, secure end-markets, and 
transfer technology and production capabilities to host 
economies – partly easing tensions created by China’s strong 
exports and weak imports. The recent OFDI tightening is thus 
a key risk to watch. While we view this as a transitory tight-
ening aimed at enforcing existing rules and closing regula-
tory loopholes, a prolonged or broadly applied stance could 
disrupt this outbound channel and reignite wider trade fric-
tions.
3. Technology: Tighter external controls and persistent gaps in 
advanced chips, industrial software and high-end equipment 
– compounded by fragmented data and insufficient organi-
zational redesign – could delay scaled deployment and raise 
Risks to Watch  
localization costs. If investment runs ahead of efficiency 
gains, commercialization, returns on capital and China’s man-
ufacturing-share gains would weaken, prolonging industry 
consolidation.
Along these lines, we present two alternative scenarios to our base 
case:
Bull case: Accelerated economic rebalancing, 
healthy reflation
From Industry 5.0 into a broad-based upswing: In this scenario, 
Beijing pairs the supply-side push behind Industry 5.0 with acceler-
ated social-welfare reform and stronger housing-stabilization mea-
sures. Deeper pension, healthcare and hukou reforms, together with 
reduced negative wealth effect from housing, help release part of the 
excess precautionary savings for consumption. Stronger domestic 
demand helps to absorb rising industrial output, reduces price-de-
structive competition, improves corporate earnings, gives firms 
more internal cash flow to fund Industry 5.0, and leads to stronger 
wage growth and thus consumption. This leads to a virtuous cycle, 
accelerating the convergence of the two-speed economy by allowing 
the productivity gains from AI and advanced manufacturing to circu-
late through the wider economy. 
This combination delivers stronger potential growth, healthy 
reflation and a firmer global manufacturing position through 
2035: Stronger domestic demand raises capacity utilization and 
shortens the lag from investment to productivity, while improved 
corporate earnings and cash flow support continued innovation, 
workforce redeployment and organizational upgrading. We there-
fore estimate potential GDP growth at 4.7% in 2026-27, 4.5% in 
2028–30 and 4.2% in 2031–35, reflecting a stronger cumulative 
uplift from AI and Industry 5.0 on TFP and capex input, at ~6pp over 
the next decade, alongside a smaller transitional drag from AI-related 
labor displacement (0.5pp). The GDP deflator rises from lowflation 
in 2026–27 toward 1.5–2% over the medium term, easing debt-defla-
tion pressures and facilitating private-sector balance-sheet repair. A 
more balanced demand mix reduces reliance on external markets to 
absorb domestic capacity, easing  trade frictions. Combined with 
faster industrial-AI breakthroughs supported by stronger corporate 
earnings, this widens China’s advantages in cost, speed, yield and 
flexibility, lifting its share of global manufacturing value-add toward 
33% by 2035.


[Page 84]
M
 BluePaper
84
Bear case: More entrenched deflation amid 
premature policy tightening
Premature fiscal consolidation, undisciplined industrial invest-
ment and weaker external market access reinforce a demand–
deflation–overcapacity loop: Under this scenario, Beijing prioritizes 
debt resolution over short-term economic growth, and aggressively 
tightens fiscal policy  while household deleveraging and the property 
correction remain incomplete, further weakening domestic demand. 
Meanwhile, local governments and firms continue to expand AI infra-
structure, robotics and smart-factory capacity without sufficient 
demand or return discipline, creating 'smart overcapacity' and 
renewed margin pressure. Moreover, prolonged restrictions on man-
ufacturing OFDI slow overseas localization, and leave more output 
reliant on direct exports and transshipment. The resulting trade 
imbalances trigger broader protectionist responses among non-US 
partners, further constraining the external demand needed to absorb 
domestic capacity.
This adverse feedback loop results in weaker potential growth, 
entrenched deflation and a lower global manufacturing share: The 
further deterioration in  supply-demand imbalance and resulting 
deflation depresses corporate earnings, and constrains private-
sector funding for innovation and broader Industry 5.0 diffusion. We 
thus see potential GDP growth falling to 4.4% in 2026-27, 4.0% in 
2028–30 and 3.5% in 2031–35, with the cumulative GDP uplift from 
AI and Industry 5.0 limited to ~2% by 2035, largely offset by the 
AI-induced  labor replacement effect. As oil prices normalize, GDP 
deflator could slip back to negative territory from next year, and stay 
around -0.5% to -1.0% over the medium term, raising real debt bur-
dens and reinforcing private-sector deleveraging. China remains the 
world’s largest manufacturing base, but weaker nominal growth, 
lower returns, tighter market access and partial supply-chain dis-
placement cap its share of global manufacturing value-add at ~27% 
over the next decade.

The global manufacturing map is changing as industrial policy and trade restrictions ramp up: direct US-China trade has declined, 
tariffs and technology controls are intensifying, and final shipment routes are in transition. Even though these forces drive bilateral 
de-linking, our analysis shows that the actual underlying productive capacity shifts are considerably slower to shift. China’s role in 
global manufacturing supply chains remains central, and China continues to not only expand capacity but also rise up the value 
chain. 
China Trade in a Global Context 
Mayank Phadke, Rajeev Sibal
The current trade cycle is unusually narrow and focussed around 
complex goods with concentrated production. AI-related invest-
ment, data-center infrastructure, semiconductors, servers, power 
equipment, electrical equipment, and capital goods are driving a dis-
proportionate share of global trade momentum. Going forward, 
robotics and their parts, and electric vehicles and similar high-tech 
goods tied to AI applications could drive the next wave of trade 
expansion. These considerations favors economies with existing 
manufacturing 
ecosystems 
in 
semiconductors, 
electronics, 
machinery, batteries, and high-complexity industrial goods – China is 
uniquely-well positioned to continue to benefit as the AI trade 
evolves.

China’s advantage is also likely to be reinforced by industrial 
policy and focused capital deployment. China Industrial 5.0 is an 
extension of its manufacturing model: using AI, robotics, industrial 
software, automation, sensors, power systems, advanced materials, 
and precision equipment to upgrade an already deep production 
base. The key point is not simply that China is adding capacity, but 
that it is adding capital stock and productivity in the same sectors 
driving the current trade cycle. In a world where industrial policy is 
increasingly shaping markets, China’s advantage lies in deploying 
policy into its comprehensive manufacturing ecosystem to pivot to 
fast-growing sectors, while other regions try to rebuild their capital 
stock and manufacturing base.

The core distinction in a global trade context is between trade 
rerouting and capacity relocation. Rerouting changes where 
goods are shipped from. Relocation changes where goods are 
made. The evidence so far points more strongly to rerouting, real-
location, and selective de-risking than to broad-based capacity 
relocation. China’s influence is therefore changing form, rather than 
diminishing. Its direct share in US imports has fallen, but China 
remains embedded through intermediate goods, components, 
machinery, capital equipment, and regional supplier networks. As 
final assembly shifts to ASEAN, Mexico, and other economies, China 
remains central to the production system behind those exports. 
China's trade is broadening and exposing it to both faster growing 
economies and faster-growing products. The growing diversification 
of trade destinations has allowed exports to not only be resilient, but 
even be the driver of growth while trade tensions rose.

Trade rerouting drives regional divergence. China and North Asia 
remain central to the manufacturing cycle. ASEAN benefits from 
China+1 diversification but often remains dependent on Chinese 
inputs. Mexico benefits from nearshoring and downstream assembly, 
though policy stability is critical. The US remains the demand center. 
The concept of reshoring will remain a policy focus – yet for now, it 
remains narrow and AI-linked, while broader reshoring and capacity 
relocation are likely to be a very gradual process. Europe faces rising 
competition in advanced manufacturing and competes directly with 
China in key capital goods, while Canada remains strategically impor-
tant upstream but is underexposed to the downstream sectors 
driving current trade growth.
The Trade Cycle in Context Amidst De-
Globalization
Global trade is fragmenting, but not collapsing. Policy has redi-
rected trade flows, particularly between the US and China, but pro-
duction structures remain persistent. Tariffs, technology controls, 
industrial policy, and geopolitical risk have encouraged firms to diver-
sify final assembly and sourcing. However, these forces have changed 
measured trade patterns faster than they have rebuilt productive 
capacity.
The distinction between shifting productive capacity and trade 
patterns is critical. A decline in direct US imports from China does 
not necessarily mean that Chinese production has been displaced. 
Indeed, our analysis suggests US imports from other Asian econo-
mies, such as Vietnam and Indonesia, increased in line with increased 
imports of these countries from China, which could suggest trans-
shipment. At the same time, there has been a steady increase in the 
trade linkages between China and rest of Asia, particularly ASEAN.

The current adjustment is therefore better described as selective 
re-configuration than broad relocation. Trade flows are being reor-
ganized across Asia, Mexico, and other third markets, but production 
ecosystems are not moving at the same speed. Modern supply chains 
are complex, capital-intensive, and geographically concentrated 
because they depend on supplier depth, technical know-how, logis-
tics networks, and scale economies, and shifting these is a gradual, 
expensive, and risky process, making any true changes in productive 
capacity slow. 
Direct import shares are becoming a less complete indicator of 
supply-chain dependence. A better framework looks at China’s 
share of global productive capacity and global manufacturing 
value add, its role in upstream inputs, its supply of capital goods, 
and its position in regional value chains.
The Current Trade Cycle Is Narrow and AI-
Led
The current global trade cycle is not a broad-based goods 
recovery; it is narrow, investment-led, and concentrated in 
AI-related and capital-intensive sectors. AI hardware, semiconduc-
tors, servers, data-center infrastructure, electrical equipment, power 
equipment, machinery, and related capital goods have become the 
strongest part of the trade cycle. Outside these areas, goods demand 
has been softer.

Composition matters because AI-linked supply chains are highly 
import-intensive. US capex can rise sharply without immediately 
generating domestic production. In the early stage of the cycle, 
stronger investment can lift imports of semiconductors, servers, 
electrical equipment, cooling systems, power equipment, and other 
capital goods. The first effect of reshoring or capacity formation can 
therefore be higher imports, not lower imports.
The AI cycle also reinforces the importance of existing production 
ecosystems. Taiwan and Korea remain central in advanced semicon-
ductors, memory, and other upstream inputs. ASEAN and Mexico 
have gained in downstream assembly and integration. China remains 
important across intermediate goods, machinery, electrical equip-
ment, components, and broader manufacturing ecosystems. The 
result is not a clean relocation of supply chains but a more dispersed 
and still deeply interdependent production network.
The trade cycle is therefore reinforcing existing Asian manufac-
turing advantages even as policy pushes firms to reduce direct 
China exposure. Economies with established scale in electronics, 
machinery, semiconductors, batteries, clean energy, electrical equip-
ment, and precision manufacturing are better positioned than econo-
mies that have market access but less relevant industrial capacity.
Tariff policy is another trade-cycle catalyst. The key issue is no 
longer only the level of tariffs, but the durability and legal form of the 
tariff regime. A shift toward more durable statutory authorities 
could reduce some uncertainty, but it would also make trade policy 
a more persistent determinant of sourcing decisions. That matters 
for global trade because firms are unlikely to commit long-duration 
capital unless they believe policy incentives and market access rules 
are stable enough to underwrite investment.


[Page 89]
M
 BluePaper
Morgan Stanley Research
89
Industrial Policy Is Deepening China’s 
Capacity Advantage
Industrial policy is becoming a more important driver of trade out-
comes. Structured Industrial policy across key economies is shaping 
markets, moving away from episodic state support. Governments are 
increasingly using subsidies, localization requirements, procurement 
preferences, import restrictions, export controls, and investment 
restrictions to influence where future productive capacity is built.
China Industrial 5.0 should be understood in this context. It is the 
next stage of China’s manufacturing model: a shift from scale manu-
facturing toward intelligent, resilient, and globally leading industrial 
ecosystems. The objective is to embed AI, robotics, industrial soft-
ware, automation, sensors, power systems, advanced materials, and 
precision equipment into the production base, making the capex 
cycle both capacity-expanding and productivity-enhancing.
China’s starting point is the core advantage. The US, Europe and 
other manufacturing regions are using industrial policy to rebuild 
or defend selected strategic sectors. China is using industrial 
policy to upgrade an already comprehensive manufacturing base. 
Policy-directed finance, local-government coordination, infrastruc-
ture provision, supply-chain localization, procurement, and domestic 
demand can all be deployed into existing industrial clusters.

China’s Role Is Changing Form, Not 
Disappearing
China’s direct trade relationship with the US has weakened, but 
China’s role in global manufacturing has not declined in the same 
way. We think the right framing is bilateral de-linking, not a global 
shift away from China.  China can lose direct US import share while 
maintaining or even expanding its influence in global manufacturing. 
If third-country exports to the US rise alongside those countries’ 
imports from China, the data may show diversification in final ship-
ment terms but gloss over a continued Chinese presence in manufac-
turing production.
China has adapted by diversifying export destinations, deepening 
regional trade links, and upgrading into higher-value manufac-
turing. Its export base is no longer concentrated only in low-end con-
sumer goods. China has gained competitiveness in machinery, 
electrical equipment, transport equipment, EVs, batteries, solar, 
industrial automation, and other advanced manufacturing catego-
ries. These sectors are closely aligned with the current global invest-
ment cycle.
China’s role is also shifting from direct exporter to system-level 
supplier. As final assembly moves to ASEAN, Mexico, and other 
economies, China can still provide intermediate inputs, components, 
machinery, and equipment used in those exports. This makes China’s 
manufacturing influence broader than its direct share of US imports. 
Indeed, analysis of supply chains suggests that well over a third of all 
intermediate inputs for machinery and electrical/electronics manu-
facturing in Asia come from China. This position is likely to be further 
consolidated in coming years, putting China in pole position both 
upstream and downstream for goods that are likely to drive the trade 
cycle. 
Supply-chain reorientation globally is a moving target – bottle-
necks and strategic sectors are changing. Other economies are 
trying to build capacity in strategic sectors, while China is simulta-
neously expanding capital stock, upgrading productivity, and 
localizing bottlenecks in those same sectors.

Understanding the Vectors of Trade: Complexity, Concentration and Geopolitical Distance 
In Supply Chain Strain, our deep dive on Global Supply Chains, we laid out a framework for assessing shifts in supply chains 
based on three factors: complexity, concentration, and geopolitical distance. We find that China is well situated across all 
three axes, and Industry 5.0 will further enhance their position. 
Complexity favors economies that already produce sophisticated goods at scale. China, alongside Korea, and Japan remain 
well positioned on this measure, as importers of low-complexity goods and exporters of high-complexity goods. Their export 
bases are aligned with the sectors driving current demand: electronics, machinery, semiconductors, electrical equipment, batteries, 
and high-end capital goods. Canada underperforms relative to income on this dimension, while Europe’s challenge is maintaining 
competitiveness against China in sectors where it historically had strength.

Concentration matters because the most important supply chains are not easy to relocate. Advanced semiconductors, memory, 
AI hardware, batteries, industrial equipment, and precision machinery are concentrated for a reason. They require specialized skills, 
supplier ecosystems, and large-scale capital investment. The higher the concentration and complexity of a supply chain, the slower 
the relocation process is likely to be.

Geopolitical distance matters because policy is increasingly shaping trade flows. Industrial policy is fragmenting supply chains 
by geography and by product. Tariffs, technology controls, local-content rules, and strategic-sector policies are changing the 
incentives around sourcing and investment. But policy can redirect trade faster than it can recreate production ecosystems. The 
result is a world where trade routes fragment before productive capacity relocates.

China and North Asia
China and North Asia remain the core beneficiaries of the current industrial cycle, given their alignment with the sectors driving trade growth: 
AI hardware, semiconductors, memory, electronics, electrical equipment, machinery, batteries, and clean-energy manufacturing.

93
Taiwan and Korea benefit from hard-to-replace upstream capacity. 
Their roles in advanced semiconductors, memory, and other AI inputs 
are not easily replicated elsewhere. High demand can support a self-
reinforcing capex cycle in which strong profitability drives invest-
ment, investment reinforces capacity, and capacity supports export 
performance.
China benefits through a broader channel. Even where final assembly 
shifts away from China, Chinese inputs and capital goods remain 
embedded in regional supply chains. China also continues to gain 
share in higher-value manufacturing categories, including machinery, 
transport equipment, electrical equipment, EVs, batteries, and indus-
trial automation. This allows China to remain central to global manu-
facturing even as direct US-China trade declines.
ASEAN
ASEAN is both a beneficiary of diversification and a channel through 
which China remains embedded in global production. Vietnam, 
Thailand, Malaysia, Indonesia, and other regional economies have 
gained from China+1 strategies, electronics assembly, trade 
rerouting, and rising demand for intermediate goods.
Exhibit 145:EM Asia has replaced China in US import share, but not 
necessarily in production

Source: USITC, Morgan Stanley Research
The durability of ASEAN’s gains depends on whether export-share 
gains translate into domestic value-added deepening. Final assembly 
can move faster than supplier ecosystems. A country can gain US 
import share while still relying heavily on China for inputs, compo-
nents, machinery, and capital goods.
The key test for ASEAN is therefore local capex, supplier deepening, 
and rising domestic value added. If these follow, ASEAN becomes a 
more durable winner. If not, the region remains a rerouting and 
assembly hub within a still China-centered production ecosystem.
 Mexico
Mexico is a natural beneficiary of supply-chain reorientation because 
of proximity to US demand, integration into North American manu-
facturing, and exposure to downstream assembly. It can gain from US 
efforts to reduce direct China exposure, particularly in autos, elec-
tronics, machinery, and selected industrial goods.
But Mexico’s upside depends on policy stability. USMCA uncertainty 
matters because manufacturing investment decisions require long 
timelines. Firms need confidence in rules of origin, compliance 
requirements, tariff treatment, exemptions, and sector-specific 
policy. The agreement does not need to collapse to affect invest-
ment. Uncertainty alone can keep firms in wait-and-see mode.
Mexico can therefore gain trade share before it gains durable produc-
tive capacity. The stronger case for Mexico requires evidence that 
higher exports are accompanied by capex, supplier deepening, and 
higher local value added.
 United States
The US sits at the center of the cycle as both demand center and 
policy fulcrum. AI capex, data-center investment, and technology 
infrastructure are driving import demand, while tariffs, industrial 
policy, and technology controls are reshaping sourcing incentives.
However, stronger US investment does not immediately mean lower 
import dependence. Many of the goods required for AI infrastructure 
and manufacturing capacity are import-intensive. In the near term, 
the path toward reshoring may run through higher imports of capital 
goods and components.

The US can build capacity in selected strategic sectors over time. But 
the process is likely to be narrow, technical, expensive, and long-
dated. The key test is not whether imports from China fall. It is 
whether US domestic capital stock, manufacturing output, supplier 
ecosystems, and production capability rise in the sectors that matter.
 Europe
Europe remains a high-complexity manufacturing region, but it faces 
a more difficult trade cycle. The strongest areas of global trade 
momentum are AI hardware, electronics, semiconductors, electrical 
equipment, power equipment, and selected capital goods. Europe 
has industrial depth, but it is less directly exposed to the AI hardware 
cycle than North Asia.
Europe also faces increasing competition from China in sectors 
where it historically held stronger positions, including machinery, 
transport equipment, electrical equipment, clean technology, and 
other advanced manufacturing categories. This is different from ear-
lier phases of China’s rise, when competitive pressure was concen-
trated in lower-value consumer goods.

Europe retains industrial capability, but is at risk of losing share in the

95
Our Attractive view on US Industrials is underpinned by our call for 
an extended US capex upcycle. We believe the entanglement of 
structural tech diffusion and rising protectionism will drive 
higher capital intensity into the US (AI, Reshoring, Electrification). 
When combined with improved ROI on efficiency upgrades post 
inflation reset, we see stronger and more secular demand for cap-
ital equipment and think the benefits primarily accrue into the US 
market (Capex Bifurcation).  Given the above, we favor US Capex 
exposure above all else  vs. more concern on the International side  – 
if the US is taking share, someone else is losing (Tale of Two 
Hemispheres). The objective of Trump 2.0 policy and ongoing trade 
negotiations is to drive a "strategic rebalance" of the country's 
US$1.2T trade deficit – this can only be accomplished by companies 
serving the US market via domestic production (both US and Foreign 
corps) or increased US exports to its key trade partners – both 
options require a higher rate of domestic production and invest-
ment, and this is where the opportunity lies. Within short-cycle, we 
see 1H’26 bifurcation between those tied to fixed investment vs. 
those tied to consumer spend. The beneficiaries of the Western 
migration are not the ones selling the products but instead the 
ones constructing and serving the facilities, and that is US 
Industrials – a group that has not seen market growth in decades but 
now sits atop the US policy agenda. 
Times Are Changing
Over the last 45 years,  manufacturing capex dollars have chased low 
cost labor – a motivation that has made it difficult for US to compete 
vs. low cost producing nations, but we believe this is changing. With 
structural Tech diffusion taking hold at an accelerated rate and com-
panies focused on building resiliency following a period of unprece-
Multipolar World – US Industrials  
Brandon Knutson, Toby Okwara & Christine Yao, US Industrials Multi Industry
dented disruption, we believe that capital and proximity to end 
user will increasingly dictate the regional allocation of capex – 
parameters to which the US has no equal. As detailed below, the US 
touches 55% of global investment (25% inward, 30% outward), top 
amongst any nation. In addition, the US accounts for ~30% of Global 
consumption, top amongst any nation (equal to the EU plus China). 
The US remains a significant outlier as the only country / region that 
does not produce the goods it consumes (US accounts for 30% of 
global consumption vs. just 16% of production). Should Humanoids 
allow US manufacturing (Mfg) costs to achieve parity with low cost 
foreign producing nations, we see scope for production to move 
more in line with consumption, as is the case across ROW.  With struc-
tural tech diffusion narrowing the global Mfg cost profile and bene-
fiting those with access to capital (US), it is unclear why the US 
should remain such an outlier. 
The same Tech/AI diffusion that is reshaping software productivity is 
now beginning to move into the physical economy. As one example, 
Project Prometheus (link), the Jeff Bezos-backed AI platform focused 
on engineering and manufacturing applications, supports our view 
that AI is increasingly being directed toward complex physical pro-
duction, not just digital workflows. While still early, we believe the 
strategic implication is significant: AI-enabled engineering, simula-
tion, process design and factory optimization can compress the 
design-build-test cycle for complex manufactured products and 
reduce the historical labor-cost disadvantage of producing in the 
US. In our view, AI can accelerate the transition from a manufacturing 
model defined by labor arbitrage to one increasingly defined by cap-
ital access, automation intensity, proximity to end-market, energy 
availability, IP protection and supply-chain resiliency – all areas 
where the US is structurally advantaged.

the context of 4% Global Mfg growth), it would suggest the cumu-

While the market is highly focused on Data Centers at ~4% of US 
electricity demand, we do not think enough attention is being paid 
to the manufacturing economy which accounts for ~26% of 
domestic electricity consumption. US Mfg electricity consumption 
has declined over the last 25 years as outsourcing took hold and 
domestic production stagnated. We believe this is beginning to 
change - US Mfg capacity is realizing material YoY growth for the first 
time in more than a decade, which will facilitate higher production 
rates and in turn electricity consumption as the new facilities ramp 
up operations (link). Our math suggests US Reshoring can add 
~200 bps of incremental growth to US Mfg production over the 
next two decades. 
We are not trying to say that we will see a mass wave of shuttered 
Asian facilities reopened in the US, but rather trying to determine 
where incremental investment will flow, and we see the scales tilting 
toward the US. As the global economy grows and new technolo-
gies take hold, the world will always be adding Mfg facilities, and 
our aim is to determine in what geography the capacity lands. A 
higher level of production requires a larger fixed asset base to sup-
port production, a driver of incremental capex. In general, the for-
ward capex to production opportunity tracks at a roughly 1:1 
relationship so if production goes higher, so will the capex / fixed 
assets needed to support.  The forward opportunity is compounded 
by the simple fact that the US Mfg Economy has been under-invested 
over the last 25 years, compounding the level of spend needed to 
support ongoing US Reshoring momentum. Prior to China joining the 
WTO, US Mfg Investment grew at a 3.8% CAGR in real terms but 
growth slowed to just 0.7% CAGR over the following 25 years. 
Assuming the 3.8% long-term CAGR was sustained (reasonable in

US Manufacturing Capacity Back on Growth, 
Production Will Follow
The nature of our US Reshoring incomings suggest the thematic is 
really just a function of policy driven headlines rather than actual 
impact on the ground - we very much disagree and want to provide 
some concrete proof (pun intended). US Industrial production 
capacity has shown material growth for the first time in two decades 
as companies reshore production in response to global instability / 
Covid supply chain risk, US tariffs, and Structural Tech diffusion. We 
believe activity should continue to build over time – Reshoring 
momentum begets more momentum as supply chains are estab-
lished and this infrastructure can be shared / leveraged, all in-line 
with our $10T US Reshoring thesis (Deep Dive). If companies are 
expanding capacity in the US, higher production will follow – even 
if end demand disappoints. The relocation of output into the US 
market in response to Trump 2.0 tariffs is the top reason we turned 
more constructive on the US production economy in our 2026 out-
look (Layering in Cyclical Torque) – a call that has only strength-
ened in 1H'26 on volatile geopolitics which both raises the cost of 
International production and drives concern on supply chain reli-
ability (More Durable Than Feared). 


[Page 98]
M
 BluePaper
98
Exhibit 153:US Manufacturing Production Capacity (ex Utility) YoY %: Showing material growth for the first time in two decades as 
Companies reshore production in response to: Global instability / Covid supply chain risk, US tariffs, Structural Tech diffusion
Source: Morgan Stanley Research, Company Reports
The common thread across industries adding capacity is that they fall 
into two buckets: 1) those with a clearly positive demand outlook 
(Semis and Communication Equip, Electrical Equip), and 2) those 
where the US retains a structural cost advantage versus ROW 
(Chemicals and Petroleum/Coal). Capacity declines are centered on 
Consumer facing verticals,  which tells us that not all verticals react 
to changing supply chain costs. We  have long thought that the best 
US Reshoring verticals are simply industries that need to add 
capacity - our call is not that  we are going to see a mass wave of shut-
tered Asian facilities reopened in the US but rather the US is better 
positioned to land the incremental factory relative to any period over 
the last 45 years due to a flywheel of structural tech diffusion and 
rising protectionism (Game of Increments and Prize of Trillions).  As 
the global economy grows and new technologies take hold, the 
world will always be adding Mfg facilities and we think the scales 
are tiling westward. A growing pushback to our US Reshoring thesis 
in recent months is that the capacity adds are simply an extension of 
the Data Center trade (Semiconductor, Utility, Computer Equip, 
Electrical Equip) – while this is true, it speaks to the point that 
capacity follows demand. And what matters for the US Reshoring 
thematic is that these capacity adds are now coming to the US 
market, a marked difference vs. 5-10 years ago. Note, Electrical 
Equipment capacity is currently tracking +4% yoy but saw 20%+ 
declines over the prior two decades after China joined the WTO. That 
reversal is US Reshoring.
The reason US producers have been able to quickly ramp up is 
because the initial wave of spend has been focused on efficiency and 
productivity investment (i.e., Automation), and smaller projects with 
quicker payoffs relative to large capacity expansion (link). But what 
is most important is that the incremental unit of production is 
beginning to shift to the US market in response to changing sup-
ply-chain costs (i.e.,  tariffs) and this is the leading indicator for 
where larger capacity projects will land when it is ultimately 
needed. The Attractive view on US Industrials and the “GDP +3” 
thesis detailed in our Sept 2024 launch (link) is underpinned by our 
call for an extended US capex upcycle.  US Reshoring is always 
viewed through a capex lens but once these facilities are com-
pleted, production will ramp higher – a tailwind for US IP that is 
not dependant on demand. If a company builds a new factory, it will 
be utilized. Best positioned to benefit are companies that sell mainte-
nance, repair, and operations (MRO) into these facilities. US Mfg TAM 
expansion also brings under-appreciated and long-term tailwinds for 
US Power demand  – manufacturing accounts for 26% of US elec-
tricity consumption (vs. MSD for Data Centers) and carries a ~90% 
correlation to manufacturing output which will follow capacity 
higher (link).

US Manufacturing orders have accelerated sharply in 2026, 
building on 2H'25 momentum and driven by changes in US tariff 
policy, pushing incremental production plus investment into the 
domestic market ( Exhibit 160 ) – in line with our 2026 outlook 
which turned constructive on US short cycle (Layering in Cyclical

amidst trough sentiment on US-Iran conflict (link). The momentum 
sets the stage for a return to decade high industrial growth in 
2H'26 – 2027 as the pickup in product levels layers on top of sus-
tained capex strength (link). Amongst all the US Industrial verticals, 
seeing sustained momentum in US Industrial Machinery orders is 
most constructive as this suggests companies are expanding US pro-
duction capacity in response to tariffs, which has changed the global 
supply chain cost calculation – this is positive for all US manufac-
turing verticals because higher production will follow these 
investments in capacity.

102
European Capital Goods companies typically are global compa-
nies, so we would expect their sales and share prices to broadly 
benefit from rising global manufacturing investment. Typically, 
regional revenue exposure tends to be 40% EMEA, 30% North 
America and 30% Emerging markets (15% China). China taking an 
increasing share of global manufacturing is both an opportunity (for 
some) and a risk (for others).
We think the key potential beneficiaries from rising China invest-
ment are: 1) Atlas Copco (OW), 2) Spirax (OW), and 3) Siemens 
(EW). 
European companies are also moving quickly  in the area of 
Physical AI within manufacturing, with key examples being 
Siemens' through its Digital Industries division and software, and 
Kion through its automated forklifts and warehouse automation seg-
ment. Atlas' industrial products (e.g., Compressors and Industrial 
metrology), and Spirax steam and heating systems, are also impor-
tant products in improving manufacturers' efficiency. On the other 
hand, we continue to see ongoing risk on margin pressure from China 
competition for companies like Signify (UW). 
If we just talk about European manufacturing spend (Industrial 
production and Capex), Europe's industrial base faces structural 
challenges, which are heightened in a multipolar world. However, 
this spending on industrial manufacturing is flowing into North 
America and to a lesser extent China, where many of our companies 
still have very strong #1 or #2 market positions in product areas like 
Industrial compressors or Factory Automation hardware. 
 Europe's reliance on foreign energy supply, manufacturing and 
defence has led  to higher energy costs and years of de-industrialisa-
tion, which have created a competitiveness gap vs. the rest of the 
world. Multipolar world dynamics (such as Russian and Middle East 
conflicts, US tariffs and China's critical raw material bans, etc.)  fur-
ther hinder Europe's ability to cope with this gap, especially in the 
absence of a coordinated Industrial Policy. The European automotive 
sector's loss in market shares to Chinese players is a case study for 
how US tariffs and China's shift to higher-value chain products can 
combined affect European industries in the absence of anti-competi-
tion policy.
Multipolar World – Europe Industrials  
Max Yates, European Capital Goods
There are both threats and opportunities for European players in 
the context of Chinese and US industrial ambitions driving a 
decade-long capex upcycle. We see higher competition risks in less 
differentiated European sectors (such as lighting, forklifts, and wind 
turbines), while high-value software/equipment companies with 
existing market share in the US and Chinese markets are set to ben-
efit. Automation players and Industrial AI enablers like Siemens 
stands out if industrial AI becomes embedded in factory automation, 
EDA and digital-twin workflows. Similarly,  European players with 
large revenue exposure to these regions and localized production, 
will benefit from the second derivative effects of increased spending 
on industrial projects.
We are also starting to see some nascent AI adoption from 
European manufacturers with BMW piloting Humanoids and Kion's 
AI enabled warehouse automation at customer QXO. While these 
roll-outs of physical AI may be lagging some of the developments in 
China, we still see adoption taking place. In addition, Siemens as the 
world's largest discrete automation company, remains well posi-
tioned to play a central role in physical AI. Furthermore, Siemens is 
developing an Industrial Foundation Model (IFM) trained on large-
scale industrial and engineering data from Siemens and their cus-
tomers, to enable AI applications across design, manufacturing, and 
operations. The model is designed to understand industrial context, 
including 3D models, technical specifications, and production data, 
delivering domain-specific insights and automation. 
EU Industrial Production Trending Sideways 
European  manufacturing value added as a share of GDP has 
declined from roughly 19-20% in the early 1990s to c.15% in 2024 
( Exhibit 164 ), and Europe’s manufacturing value-add growth has 
lagged the US and the rest of the world over the past five years 
( Exhibit 103 ). This is in large part driven by Europe's focus on high-
value service industries and its outsourcing of cheap manufacturing 
to lower labour cost countries like China. 
EU Industrial Production has also failed to regain sustained 
momentum after the 2-year industrial recession following the out-
break of the Russia-Ukraine conflict in 2022. Exhibit 3 shows that 
although we saw improvements in EU IP in 2025 (from a low base and 


[Page 103]

some pre-buying ahead of US tariffs), European Industrial lead indica-
tors remain mixed in 2026 and will likely continue to move sideways. 
For example, in Germany industrial production remains well below 
pre-Covid levels, and in June  was flat MoM and YoY. However, in 
terms of the manufacturing PMIs in July , there was an interesting 
regional step-up  in Germany (52.2), while the French manufacturing 
PMI continued to hover below 50. In aggregate, we think European 
industrial demand continues to move broadly sideways, however, 
this does not mean we see a clear demand deterioration.

Europe faces Structural Challenges 
Europe's competitiveness gap stems from a multitude of factors 
analyzed in depth in the 2024 Draghi Report (link). The main ones 
include higher energy costs (and associated physical infrastructure 
and permitting constraints), as well as higher and non-homogenous 
taxes and overall regulatory burdens that stymie innovation, growth 
and competitiveness.  
For example, Europe's manufacturing base has been hindered by 
elevated energy costs in comparison to their  US and Chinese peers. 
In 2024 industrial electricity prices in the EU reached €0.199 per 
kWh, compared to €0.082 in China and €0.075 in the US.  This has 
been amplified since the  Russia-Ukraine conflict, exposing 
Europe's, and in particular Germany's, reliance on cheap Russian 
gas which subsequently had to be re-routed, predominantly to the 
US (42% of EU gas imports arrived as LNG in 2023, up from 20% in 
2021). European industries have had to pay higher spot prices due to 
liquefaction and transport costs; moreover, energy prices have been 
further compounded by an under-invested energy grid. The effects 
have therefore been tied to the energy intensity of the business with 
chemicals and paper & packaging businesses suffering far more than 
Capital Goods, as shown in Exhibit 169 . 
Exhibit 168:Estimated final electricity price for large industrial cus-
tomers in energy-intensive industries, 2019-2025. In 2025, EU 
prices were double US  and 40% higher than China.  
Source: IEA, Morgan Stanley Research
Exhibit 169:Total final energy consumption by industrial sector, EU,

The Middle East crisis added additional inflationary pressures on 
raw material prices for European industrial companies. Key infla-
tionary elements are Copper, Plastic and Silver. This has an outsized 
impact on European Electrical equipment company's direct raw 
materials purchasing. Although we see this as a temporary effect, 
these episodes evidence Europe's reliance and risks in the context of 
the new multipolar world order. 
European policymakers recognize these threats, but a strategic 
and coordinated Industrial policy is unlikely. Recent European 
Commission acts like the Net-Zero Industry Act, the European Chips 
Act, the Critical Raw Materials Act and trade defence measures such 
as tariffs on Chinese EV imports, alongside proposals to prioritize 
European suppliers in strategic sectors and accelerate investment in 
AI, clean technology and digital infrastructure are evidence that 
European policy makers are aware of the structural risks to Europe. 
However, fragmented fiscal capacity, slower investment cycles and 
regulatory complexity continue to limit Europe's ability to match the 
scale and speed of China's industrial strategy as well as the US's inno-
vation cycles.

This competitiveness gap has been felt across the European 
Industrial majors. Especially  capital intensive sectors have been 
forced into significant job cuts with Siemens, Thyssenkrupp, BMW  
committing to reducing head count by 8%, 12% and 5% respectively 
(see Exhibit 13). Siemens highlighted the "muted demand primarily in 
the key markets of China and Germany coupled with increased com-
petitive pressures have considerably reduced orders and revenue in 
the industrial automation business". Furthermore, Thyssenkrupp 
argued that the layoffs were needed to "become more competitive 
and secure as many jobs as possible for the future".

Automotive Sector: Case Study for European  OEM Companies losing market share to Chinese players: The European auto 
OEMs have been gradually losing market share, declining in the EU5 from 72% in 2020 to 67% in 2025 and projected to fall to 62% 
by 2027. In particular, this intensified competition has been concentrated in Sports Utility Vehicle (SUVs), Battery Electric Vehicle 
(BEVs), Plug-in Hybrid Vehicle (PHEVs) and entry level vehicles where Chinese OEMs have particular advantages when it comes to 
lithium-ion batteries and scale economics.

This has not been universal with  Chinese OEMs gaining the most market share in the UK, Spain and Norway where Chinese 
OEM market share has risen by 7-10%pp since 2020, whereas in Germany and France, OEM market share has remained stable as 
Exhibits 11 and 12 show below. This has been driven by both the strength of the legacy OEM in the home market (in the case of the 
UK there is no national champion so easier for Chinese OEMs to penetrate) and governmental policy with regards to emission 
taxes and subsidies. 
Companies that have maintained and even grown market share  have had strong brands and premium categorisation whereby 
they compete less with the entry level models that Chinese OEMs look to compete against. This has resulted in OEMs like 
Volkswagen maintaining market share whereas Stellantis stands out as the clear underperformer, with market share falling from 
22% to 16%, representing the largest decline among major European OEMs.

domestic European markets. Overall, we think that it is more likely

forklifts) Chinese players will continue to target overseas mar-

China and US Capex Upcycles  create Risks 
but also Opportunities for Europe 
In the context of Europe's structural challenges, China's Industry 
5.0 creates clear competition risks. We are already seeing an 
increase in Chinese industrial equipment players gaining market 
share in Europe, especially in low differentiation products (such as 
lighting and  forklifts). 
However, Chinese companies have also clearly stated their mid-
term ambition to gain market share in Europe in  automation, 
robotics and construction equipment, areas that lack a European 
competition policy. For example, Industrial Automation  companies 
are expanding capacity in Europe with management targeting 30% of 
their revenue coming from overseas in the mid-long term (vs. 6% cur-
rently). Similarly, Robotics players like Estun are focusing on expan-
sion into Europe, through lower pricing (c.20% lower than European

In addition to this solution, Siemens has also rolled out Agentic 
tools within its purpose-built EDA Software called Solido. Solido 
harnesses Siemens’ EDA AI system to deliver advanced generative 
and Agentic AI capabilities throughout the Solido Custom IC plat-
form to transform next generation design and verification. But this is 
only the beginning for Agentic AI solutions. In a recent white paper, 
Siemens highlighted that "true transformation in EDA demands more 
autonomous AI solutions capable of reasoning, acting, and iterating 
independently". In terms of Agentic AI progress, Siemens estimates 
that fully autonomous AI agents in EDA will evolve progressively in 
waves, each wave building upon prior innovations and demanding 
multi-domain expertise .
Other clear winners from China intelligent manufacturing capex 
will be the enablers of Industrial AI. In Europe, Siemens offers the 
first example of integrated Agentic AI solutions within its Industrial 
Copilot and EDA software solutions. Siemens recently (LINK) inte-
grated Industrial AI agents in the Siemens Industrial Copilot eco-
system. These Agents independently execute complete industrial 
workflows guided by a human. They are designed to integrate with 
third-party AI agents, ensuring interoperability in different industrial 
environments and requirements. By integrating this new technology 
across the entire manufacturing value chain, Siemens predicts this 
will bring up to 50% productivity enhancements for customers. 
Siemens also sees Industrial AI agents as a key solution to labour 
shortages in the manufacturing sector, where Korn Ferry's Future of 
Work report estimates 7+mn shortfall in manufacturing jobs by 
2030.

As the global economy becomes more multipolar, with growth and 
investment spread across a wider range of countries and regions, 
Japan’s manufacturing sector may be well positioned to benefit from 
this shift. Japanese companies have long-standing strengths in areas 
such as advanced manufacturing, automation, materials and preci-
sion engineering, which could become increasingly valuable as com-
panies and governments look to diversify supply chains and build 
production capacity across multiple markets. For Japan, this could 
open up opportunities to tap into new sources of growth while also 
strengthening its role in global supply chains and industrial partner-
ships.
Japanese automation equipment suppliers’ diversified regional 
exposure could position them to capture growth across China, North 
America, Europe, and other parts of Asia, as rising geopolitical ten-
sions drive manufacturers to localize production and strengthen sup-
ply-chain resilience.
Multipolar World – Japan Industrials  
Takeshi Kitaura, Japan Factory Automation, Heavy Industry
Competition in China across industries such as electronics and 
automobiles has increased significantly over the past decade, with 
local manufacturers gaining share as the gap in production efficiency 
and product quality versus Japanese companies has narrowed. 
Japanese products generally continue to have an advantage in quality 
and durability, but are often less competitive on price given the lower 
manufacturing cost base in China.
The trend has been more mixed across automation equipment. 
Japanese suppliers have lost some share in areas such as industrial 
robots and lower-end servo motors, where local products have 
become increasingly competitive. In contrast, Japanese companies 
have maintained stronger positions in areas where equipment per-
formance has a more direct impact on product quality, precision, or 
production efficiency, including CNC systems, high-end motors, 
large PLCs, and pneumatic equipment.

85% investment cost reduction and 80% reduction in time to pro-
duction vs. a newly built factory. This type of approach could 
remain an advantage for Japanese factory automation suppliers as 
manufacturers globally look for ways to reduce energy use, factory 
space and upfront investment.
While Japan may continue to benefit from further growth in China as 
a manufacturing base, a more multipolar world could also create 
opportunities in other regions. Japan remains a major supplier of 
manufacturing equipment and production know-how, and could play 
a role as companies build and localize factories across North America, 
Europe and other parts of Asia.
Japan's automation firm exposure by industry centers around chip 
& electronics and Autos. The  combined exposure reached 50% for 
many companies with key competitiveness remaining high, espe-
cially in high tech semiconductor production, which needs fine con-
trol of equipment to maximize production efficiency and high yield. 
High-end machine tools show continued export strength to China. 
Japan's export of machine tools to China continues to uptrend though 
some shift is likely occurring to local production and local manufac-
turers.  Industry split of orders suggests compared to the past, elec-
tronics related orders have declined while broader general industry 
aimed orders and automotive orders have continued to increase. 
Companies like Fanuc may continue to benefit even as machine tools 
shift to local manufacturers, as CNC (Computer Numerical Control) 
market share looks resilient so far in the local China market, sug-
gesting industrial progress in China has led to continued demand for 
high end CNCs from the global market leader.
Pneumatic equipment is one example where Japanese suppliers con-
tinue to have a strong presence. These products use compressed air 
to control movement and are widely used across factory production 
lines due to their simplicity, reliability, and relatively low cost. SMC 
remains a leading supplier in China and has significant local produc-
tion operations in Beijing and Tianjin.
1Q (April-June) results showed China continuing to drive sales 
growth for Japanese automation equipment companies, with 
China sales growth generally running ahead of overall company 
growth. Continued demand for capital equipment should remain 
supportive for factory automation sales by Japanese corporates.

Japan’s strength in automation equipment continues to come 
from high-quality production, advanced industrial tools including 
WFE, and a strong focus on efficiency, both in operations and energy 
use. Limited land availability and relatively high electricity costs have 
historically been disadvantages for Japan as a manufacturing base, 
but this has also pushed companies to continuously improve produc-
tion equipment and processes.
Daihatsu’s SSC (Simple, Slim, Compact) concept deployed in early 
2010's is a good example. The idea is not only reflected in Japan’s 
small and efficient mini-cars, but also in how Daihatsu produces 
them. By making better use of limited space, simplifying production 
processes and reducing energy use, Daihatsu was able to achieve 
similar production capacity with around half the building space 
and roughly 40% of the equipment investment compared with its 
previous factory.  Recently Mazda has taken a further approach of 
integrating BEV production into its conventional line for ICEs as 
means to avoid building a new plant for BEVs all together leading to

In humanoids, Japan currently trails leading companies in China 
and the US, although activity has started to pick up across both hard-
ware and key components. Japan continues to have strong capabili-
ties in motors, sensors, precision components and factory 
automation, which could become increasingly relevant as humanoid 
applications develop. Geopolitical concerns may also support 
greater domestic development of physical AI, particularly given 
Japan’s need to address labor shortages caused by a declining popula-
tion across both manufacturing and non-manufacturing industries. 
Some Japanese companies announced humanoid plans this year, 
including Toyota, Mitsubishi Electric, Yaskawa, Mitsubishi Motors 
and SoftBank. 
Japan's capital expenditure continues its uptrend, including soft-
ware investments. Japan’s Tankan Survey by Bank of Japan continues 
to show corporates planning to continue capital expenditure growth 
for the manufacturing sector while further growth is expected in 
software investments, which could lead to further digitalization in 
the country.  2026 fixed investment, including land purchasing 
expenses for manufacturers, non-manufacturers, and all-industries, 
is guided at 7.5%, 6.4%, 6.8% YoY, respectively. Software investments 
for the respective domains are also forecast at 21.0%, 9.6% and 13.5% 
YoY in 2026. Machine tool orders have started to recover in the 
domestic market with YoY growth extending to 50% in July orders. 
China made up 24% of total Japan machine tool orders in 2025, 
meaning it remains a key component of machine tool industry orders 
for Japanese equipment makers, which could continue to benefit 
from China's future industrial growth trend. China orders continue to 
grow, helping to reach the ¥2 trillion estimate by Japan Machine Tool 
Builders Association. Looking at Japan's domestic end market trend 
in machine tool orders, automotive industry orders have remained 
suppressed over the past 5 years post the pandemic and await 
recovery, as the industry continues to tackle global and local compe-
tition, and the ever changing dynamics of the industry towards elec-
trification and autonomous driving.
Japan also continues to have a strong position in industrial 
robotics, with companies such as Fanuc and Yaskawa Electric among 
the global leaders.  Japan's industrial robot production remained high 
reaching 38% of  total global robot supply according to International 
Federation of Robotics. This gives Japanese companies a meaningful 
role in the development of physical AI in global manufacturing. By 
working with overseas AI providers while also developing domestic 
capabilities, Japan could continue to provide automation solutions to 
factories globally.

difficult to replicate. Policy frameworks continued to evolve, from 
the National Medium- and Long-term Plan for Science and 
Technology Development (2006–2020) launched in 2006 to Made 
in China 2025 introduced in 2015, providing a coordinated roadmap 
for upgrading China's industrial capabilities and moving the economy 
up the value chain. China was the only country covering all categories 
in the UN Industrial Classification, with the world's deepest and most 
clustered manufacturing base.
Stage 4 (mid 2010s–early 2020s) shifted the focus from scale to 
productivity –  automation, digitalization, and smart manufacturing. 
China is the world's largest industrial robot market, with over 2 mil-
lion industrial robots operating, accounting for 43% of the global 
industrial operational stock in 2024, with wide adoption across 
diverse sectors. In 2025, China's National Development and Reform 
Commission (NDRC) announced a state-backed venture capital guid-
ance fund focused on robotics, AI, and other frontier technologies. 
The fund is expected to mobilize nearly Rmb1 trillion capital from 
local governments and private investors over a 20-year period. 
Factory digitalization accelerated, industrial software adoption 
deepened, domestic equipment and component supply chains 
strengthened materially, and companies moved up the value chain in 
autos, batteries, electronics, and machinery. This is when China's 
industrialization story became less about adding capacity and more 
about improving output per unit of labor and capital. It was not 
merely "more factories", it was a shift toward efficiency, precision, 
and quality, with stronger digital management of production pro-
cesses and a clear policy push for smart manufacturing and advanced 
industry.
Looking forward, Stage 5 (2020s and beyond) is the next frontier. 
Industry 5.0 is not just "more automation." If Industrial 4.0 made the 
factory digitally visible, Industry 5.0 allows it to think, respond, and 
adapt. The core idea is embedding AI directly into industrial opera-
tions –  combining the digital foundation built in Stage 4 with AI, 
robotics, and embodied intelligence to create self-optimizing sys-
tems that learn from every production cycle, rather than following 
pre-programmed instructions. We frame this around three rein-
forcing pillars: Industrial Intelligence (AI + automation + embodied 
intelligence), Industrial Resilience (localization + security + control-
lable supply chains), and Industrial Leadership (global scale + stan-
dards + ecosystem dominance). Over the past decade, China's 
strength came from scale, speed, supply-chain density, and process 
Equity Market Implications 
Laura Wang, Chloe Liu, Vicky Wu
China equity market's evolution through five 
stages of industrial revolution
China's industrial revolution has followed a clear evolutionary path 
since the 1950s, four distinct stages, each building on the foundations 
of the last, progressively moving from basic capacity to scale, from 
scale to efficiency, and from efficiency toward intelligence.
Stage 1 (1950s–1970s) built from scratch. Modeled on the Soviet 
heavy-industry playbook, China established its foundational indus-
trial base –  steel, coal, machinery, chemicals –  through centralized 
Five-Year Plans and state-directed investment. The focus was on self-
reliance and strategic sectors –  energy, basic materials, transporta-
tion, and machinery –  creating a broad if rudimentary national 
manufacturing capability. It was not yet globally competitive by any 
measure, but it created the basic infrastructure and industrial work-
force that everything after would build upon.
Stage 2 (1970s–1990s) introduced reform and opening-up. Chinese 
leader Deng Xiaoping's market reforms, Special Economic Zones, and 
Open Door Policy turned China into an export-oriented manufac-
turing hub. Foreign direct investment surged, labor-intensive indus-
tries like textiles, apparel, and electronics assembly took off, and 
China began plugging into global supply chains — though at this 
point primarily as a low-cost assembly hub, producing to designs and 
specifications set elsewhere. The most important shift was struc-
tural: mass migration of labor from agriculture to manufacturing, the 
emergence of coastal manufacturing hubs like the Pearl River Delta, 
and the creation of a cost-competitive export manufacturing system 
that would become the platform for everything that followed.
Stage 3 (2000s–mid 2010s) saw the capex supercycle that trans-
formed China from an assembly hub into the world's most compre-
hensive industrial ecosystem. WTO accession in 2001 supercharged 
the process. Manufacturing capex grew at a 14% CAGR from 2004 to 
2015, and in 2010 China overtook the US as the world's largest manu-
facturer for the first time in over a century. The key shift during this 
stage was from "cheap manufacturing" to manufacturing depth, 
speed, and ecosystem completeness. Massive investment poured 
into infrastructure and logistics, dense regional manufacturing clus-
ters formed across the Yangtze River Delta, Pearl River Delta, and 
other industrial corridors, and increasingly complete upstream-to-
downstream supply chains made China's industrial base structurally 


[Page 115]
M
 BluePaper
Morgan Stanley Research
115
iteration. Over the next decade, the more important question will be 
whether China can become a global leader, or at least a strong fol-
lower, in the industrial foundations that sit underneath everything: 
advanced manufacturing materials, high-end equipment, sensors, 
precision components, industrial software, and power semiconduc-
tors. 
Exhibit 188:The World and China's industrial revolution path
Source: Maddison Project, Morgan Stanley Research. Note: There is no consensus on the exact year of each industrial evolution, for further detail of each evolution, please see the detail in later section. Industrial 1.0 hap-
pened as early as mid-18th century, the above chart shows 1820-1850 only due to data availability. 
The equity market grew up alongside the industrial base. In 2000, 
there were roughly 1,800 listed companies across A-shares and Hong 
Kong. By May 2026, that number had surpassed 8,200 –  more than 
a fourfold increase. Most of the early growth came during Industry 
3.0, as waves of SOEs and industrial firms went public, pushing the 
total to around 4,600 by the mid-2010s. But the real acceleration 
came during Industry 4.0 –  the launch of the STAR Market in 2019, 
ChiNext's registration-based reform in 2020, and the Beijing Stock 
Exchange in 2021 opened the door for a new generation of high-tech 
and advanced manufacturing companies.
Market cap followed a similar trajectory. Combined A-share and 
Hong Kong market capitalization went from roughly US$1 trillion in 
2000 to over US$23 trillion by May 2026, a more than 20x expansion 
in 25 years. Each industrial stage left its mark: the 2007 bull run took 
market cap past US$5tn for the first time, the capex supercycle of 
Industry 3.0 pushed it past US$10tn, and Industry 4.0 carried it 
beyond US$20tn as tech and advanced manufacturing companies 
gained index weight. But what's changed isn't just size – it's depth. 
The market today is fundamentally broader, with an investable uni-
verse that looks much more like the real industrial economy under-
neath it than it did even a decade ago. As China enters Industry 5.0, 
the equity market is better positioned than ever to capture the next 
wave of value creation – not just in the enablers building the AI infra-
structure, but eventually in the thousands of industrial adopters 
across the economy.

MSCI China's ROE has tracked each industrial stage closely. During 
Industry 3.0 (2000s–mid 2010s), ROE rose dramatically, from just 
4% in 2000 to a peak of 16-17% in 2010-2011. This was the golden era 
of capital-intensive, scale-driven growth, with high-ROE banks and 
energy companies dominating the index. ROE then began to fade 
through the later part of this stage, settling to 13-14% by the mid-
2010s as the capex supercycle matured.
During Industry 4.0 (mid 2010s-early 2020s), the decline continued 
–  more gradually at first, hovering around 12-13% through 2017-2019, 
before falling more sharply to 10-11% by the early 2020s. This 


[Page 117]
M
 BluePaper
Morgan Stanley Research
117
geting excess capacity and destructive price competition across key 
industries, which should help restore pricing power and margins over 
time; and corporate self-help, including cost discipline, share buy-
backs, and higher dividends are improving capital efficiency from the 
bottom up. The combination of these forces, exports, supply-side dis-
cipline, and AI-driven efficiency, could mark a structural ROE re-
rating for China equities.
Looking forward, unlike the prior two cycles, we believe this round is 
more about an efficiency-driven revolution, which could help lift 
both major AI-related sectors and overall index ROE. We expect 
Energy (up 6-7%), Materials (up 2-3%), Industrials (up 2-3%), and 
Information Technology (up 8-9%) to all see ROE improvement by 
2028, contributing around 2% to MSCI China’s overall index ROE.

A-shares the early play on AI enablers; H-
shares the next leg as adopters scale up 
The A-share and Hong Kong market offer very different exposure to 
the AI investment cycle. A-shares are heavily skewed toward AI 
enablers – among  MS-covered HK/China stocks, there are 31 enabler 
names with a combined market cap of over US$1tn, reflecting 
A-shares' deep bench in semiconductors, optical components, indus-
trial automation, and AI infrastructure. In contrast, Hong Kong offers 
concentrated exposure to AI adopters. Morgan Stanley covers 88 
adopter names in the Hong Kong market, representing a combined 
US$1.35tn in market capitalization. These companies are primarily 
internet platforms, consumer technology leaders, and enterprise 
software providers that stand to gain as AI becomes increasingly 
embedded across products, services, and operating processes.
This distinction matters for how the investment opportunity plays 
out over time. In the early phase of Industry 5.0, capital flows toward 
the companies building the picks and shovels –  chips, servers, optical 
modules, and robots –  and that is overwhelmingly an A-share story. 
But as AI adoption broadens across industries –  improving unit eco-
nomics, automating workflows, enhancing customer engagement – 
the value creation shifts toward the adopters, and that is where Hong 
Kong carries more weight. So we see A-shares as the early play on 
AI enablers and infrastructure; H-shares are the next leg as adop-
tion scales up.

On sector allocation, we strongly prefer upstream, asset-intensive 
sectors that are less exposed to AI-driven disruption and more likely 
to benefit from the Industry 5.0 capex cycle, particularly as energy 
sufficiency and supply-chain resilience get reprioritized globally. This 
includes selected Materials, Industrials, and Energy names, where 
rising global market share and improving pricing power offer struc-
tural tailwinds. We also favor the tech localization theme, which con-
tinues to benefit semiconductors IT, driven by both policy support 
and real demand pull from AI infrastructure buildout. 
Given A-shares' higher concentration of high-end upstream manufac-
turing, hard-core tech companies and AI enablers, we prefer A-shares 
over Hong Kong in the near term. The opportunity in Hong Kong 
broadens as AI adoption scales, but that is the next leg.

AI reshaping China EV's competitiveness 
From exports to global collaboration: We expect China's EV whole-
sale volume to grow 11% YoY to 17.2mn units in 2026. While China 
domestic EV demand could decline 5% YoY, we expect EV exports to 
nearly double to 4.9mn units in 2026, as geopolitical energy shocks 
have created a powerful external release valve. We estimate China 
EV brands will have 30% [=16% / (1-46%)] market share outside of 
China by 2030, much higher than the 6% [=5% / (1-22%)]  share of 
China ICE brands outside of China in 2023. 
Autos  
Tim Hsiao, Shelley Wang, Peggy Wang, Joey Xu
In the medium to long term, we believe multilateral collaborations 
(e.g., strategic partnerships, JVs) between global OEMs and Chinese 
EV players could create a global win-win scenario. Through potential 
partnerships in areas like manufacturing, component sourcing, and 
smart EV technology, global and Chinese auto makers could more 
effectively tackle the thorny issues of EV affordability, geopolitical 
uncertainty, and capital sustainability, in our view.
