Morgan Stanley Research
121
AI in EV manufacturing: AI can be used in China’s EV manufacturing 
as a factory-wide operating layer across design, batteries, assembly, 
quality, logistics, and software feedback. As BYD's 2025 Annual 
Report states, "AI empowerment has transcended the single domain 
of intelligent driving, permeating the entire value chain from R&D 
and manufacturing to supply chains, services, and energy manage-
ment. It is now the critical determinant of an automaker’s future com-
petitiveness, efficiency ceilings, and value creation capabilities." 
Chinese EV plants are increasingly becoming "AI car factories" that 
combine robotics, computer vision, digital twins, and real-time opti-
mization. 
• BYD (as an example of traditional EV makers): According to 
EV Magazine, BYD's Xi'an facility operates with approxi-
mately 97% autonomy, employing AI-powered robotics, 
automated guided vehicles (AGVs), and intelligent ware-
housing systems. BYD has also developed "digital twins" of its 
battery manufacturing environments, allowing engineers to 
simulate and optimize production without physical testing, 
leading to a reported 40% reduction in battery defects and a 
20% improvement in average battery lifespan. According to 
UBTech, BYD has deployed UBTech Robotics' Walker S1 
industrial humanoid robot in its manufacturing facilities. The 
Walker S1 is capable of performing tasks such as screw 
assembly, parts sorting, and quality inspection, and can work 
alongside unmanned logistics vehicles, paving the way for 
large-scale, uncrewed operations. 
Exhibit 199:BYD deploys UBTech humanoid robots in its plant
Source: UBTech
• Li Auto (as an example of EV start-ups): It  operates two 
green, intelligent manufacturing bases in Changzhou and 
Beijing, practicing an "AI-driven manufacturing philosophy" 
that leverages algorithms, data, and proprietary AI technolo-
gies. It deploys proprietary systems including the Lianshan 
quality monitoring and early warning platform, and the 
Li-MOS manufacturing management system to drive intelli-
gent efficiency gains and closed-loop quality control. Li 
Auto's Beijing factory reportedly generates about 2 TB of 
data per day. According to management, through AI-enabled 
inspection, modular workstations, and robotics-centered 
process planning, Li Auto has  significantly improved produc-
tion efficiency, manufacturing consistency, and maintenance 
stability.


[Page 122]
M
 BluePaper
122
AI in vehicle R&D: AI is accelerating product development cycles 
across Chinese EV makers. AI-powered tools facilitate generative 
design and digital twin prototyping, allowing for faster experimenta-
tion with materials and technologies, and optimizing aerodynamics 
and safety. Meanwhile, we notice that most Chinese OEMs have 
guided for double-digit YoY R&D spending growth in 2026, driven by 
increased investment in AI, computing power, and autonomous 
driving. While this could risk operating de-leverage near-term if R&D 
growth outpaces vehicle sales volume, we also see the opportunity 
that  it will ultimately convert into meaningful non-vehicle revenue 
streams (e.g., physical AI) and stronger autonomous driving (AD) 
based sales over the long run.
From driving cars to flying cars –  eVTOL
eVTOL demand to take off: China has emerged as the world's domi-
nant force in the eVTOL sector, with AI playing a central and multifac-
eted role across the entire value chain, from autonomous flight 
systems and intelligent manufacturing to digital airspace manage-
ment and mission-critical applications. According to China's Civil 
Aviation Administration (CAAC), the low-altitude economy market is 
projected to reach Rmb3.5tn by 2035, up from Rmb1.5tn in 2025.
AI in eVTOL manufacturing: Similar to EV, AI is also integrated into 
eVTOL manufacturing processes in China. eVTOL manufacturers are 
adopting intelligent manufacturing centers with auto-grade pulse 
assembly lines, featuring micron-level machining accuracy and milli-
meter-level assembly standards. AI is also being leveraged in the 
design and engineering phase to optimize aerodynamics, battery 
management, and energy consumption, with generative AI used for 
simulation-driven design to accelerate development cycles.
AI in air traffic management: We believe in the future, AI can also be 
applied in the low-altitude unmanned traffic management, to ana-
lyze traffic density, optimize flight corridors, and orchestrate simul-
taneous takeoffs and landings, integrating weather forecasts, 
infrastructure constraints, and regulatory inputs for safe and effi-
cient operations.

Market-share gains for Chinese battery producers. We believe

Global innovation leadership and global footprint. Chinese battery 
makers, represented by CATL, have been leading in battery tech-
nology innovation. Battery pack energy density has increased by 
roughly 20% every two years along with new product launches. 
Apart from lithium-ion batteries, CATL is developing sodium-ion bat-

technology.

plants in different regions. We now see that all major battery makers 
in China have plans to build or have built factories in overseas mar-

automation-led and intelligence-led, combined with equipment com-

1. Factory upgrades

equipment, Chinese players have an edge in  higher-power, higher-
precision use cases, supported by better control systems, automa-
tion integration and improving reliability. This product upgrade 
means Chinese equipment can address a broader set of overseas 
demand, including customers that require customized configura-
tions, faster delivery and more application-specific engineering sup-
port.
2) Overseas expansion of Chinese downstream customers. In a 
more multipolar global manufacturing environment, many Chinese 
companies in batteries, EVs, solar, electronics, chemicals, machinery 
and industrial components are adopting a "technology export + local-
ized production + integrated services" model. They building new 
capacity overseas to serve local markets, reduce trade friction and 
improve supply-chain resilience. When these Chinese manufacturers 
build plants abroad, they often bring along familiar Chinese equip-
ment suppliers because those suppliers already understand their 
process requirements, factory standards, automation systems and 
cost targets. This creates a natural export channel for Chinese equip-
ment makers: follow domestic customers overseas first, then build 
local references, service networks and credibility with non-Chinese 
customers.
2. Equipment companies' overseas growth potential
China's machinery exports stayed resilient in 2020-25, growing  at a 
12% CAGR, and further accelerated to 18% y-y in 4M26. Key general 
equipment, such as forklifts, robots, PIMMs, machining centres, and 
laser machine tools, have seen a 16-40% CAGR over 1996-2024, and 
now account for 10-44% of global exports. We expect  the penetra-
tion to continue  its steep upward trajectory supported by technology 
improvement, cost advantages, and superior services. 
1) The competitive edge of Chinese equipment makers is shifting 
from pure cost advantage to product capability, differentiation 
and customization. In many categories, Chinese suppliers are no 
longer simply exporting “cheaper equipment”; they are exporting 
solutions that are better adapted to new operating requirements. For 
example, Chinese players have a dominant position in new energy 
equipment (e.g., solar, wind power, energy storage); in construction 
machinery and heavy trucks, electrification is becoming an important 
differentiator, especially in use cases such as mining, ports, logistics 
parks and urban construction, where customers value lower energy 
cost, lower maintenance cost and easier fleet management. In laser

Benefit from downstream 'going global' initiative in 
multipolar world 
1) China's outward direct investment (ODI) has experienced sus-
tainable growth in recent years. According to the Ministry of 
Commerce, China's ODI grew at a 7% CAGR in 2020-2024, sug-
gesting a proactive shift by Chinese companies towards 'going global' 
as they are actively seeking growth opportunities against the back-
drop of a domestic economic growth slowdown, increasing global 
economic volatility, and rising trade tensions and protectionism. In 
particular, manufacturing ODI saw steady growth – at a CAGR of 13% 
over 2020-2024 and accounting for 19% of total ODI in 2024, driven 
by autos, electronics, batteries, critical metals, etc. 
• By region, China's ODI has seen rapid growth in ASEAN, 
driven by the near-shoring supply chain strategy. ODI in 
Europe remains resilient overall. ODI in the US has shown a 
weak recovery, while ODI in Mexico is growing rapidly, 
implying China is actively seeking alternative pathways to 
mitigate US-China trade uncertainty.
• By industry: With >60% of total manufacturing ODI in the 
last three years, autos have held a dominant position, particu-
larly in Europe to mitigate trade barriers and AD/CVD investi-
gations by strengthening local production. The second-
largest manufacturing industry is metal products, driven by 
China's investment in mining resource countries such as 
Indonesia and South Africa. The third-largest manufacturing 
industry is transportation, led by infrastructure investment, 
e.g., in Belt and Road countries.

2) Major downstream overseas capacity expansion is accelerating. 
In particular, we are seeing global capacity expansion by Chinese 
companies in industries such as automotive, lithium batteries and 
electronics amid geopolitical tensions.
• Autos: As domestic competition has become more fierce, 
Chinese OEMs like BYD, Chery and GAC are actively seeking 
global opportunities. They are rapidly expanding their global 
manufacturing footprint to overcome trade barriers and 
establish a stronger international presence. Their capacity 
expansion plan has reached 530k units and 460k units in 
2025 and 2026, respectively. 
• Batteries: Chinese battery manufacturers are aggressively 
expanding their production capacity globally to maintain 
market dominance and secure access to key markets. Battery 
makers have announced plans to expand capacity by 
>300GWh in the next 5 years,  with the majority in Europe. 
• Electronics: Facing mounting and ongoing tariff uncertain-
ties, supply chains have been accelerating non-China produc-
tion capacity. For example, whereas Apple products were 
fully made in China before 2020, Apple and its suppliers cur-
rently have 10% of iPhone capacity in India and 70% of Apple 
Watch capacity in Vietnam. Our Tech Hardware Team's 
supply chain checks show that Hon Hai, as the major iPhone 
assembly partner, has a long-term target to cover at least 
25% of iPhone shipments in India. Other Chinese 3C players 
(such as Xiaomi, Oppo, Vivo) are also expanding their global 
footprints.

Stock Implications
We prefer: 1) companies with high/increasing overseas exposure with 
global recognition, competitive  product lineups, and  expanding 
global market share potential. We like Weichai Power for its large-
bore engine global market share gain, especially in the US market, 
and  transition into a broader power and energy equipment platform. 
We like Sany Heavy Industry for its leading position in the construc-
tion machinery market with comprehensive product matrix and 
broad  sales network. We like Sinotruk for its HDT global market 
share gain and electric HDT expansion; and 2) companies benefitting 
from AI upgrades and ongoing equipment/factory intelligence. We 
like Han's Laser for its edge in PCB drilling equipment and ultrafast 
laser equipment, and broader laser penetration in precise processing. 
We like Bochu for its dominant position in laser cutting machine 
motion control systems, and its expansion  into other industrial appli-
cations for advanced manufacturing.

Non-linear globalization trajectory underpinned by industrial innovation and  resilience 
The healthcare sector is both an enabler and a key beneficiary of China's Industry 5.0. In medical devices,  we see an opportunity 
for China players to gain global market share in a non-linear trajectory. While the Industry 3.0/4.0 rounds of globalization were 
mainly driven by cost leadership and a focus on low/mid-end markets, we are entering a new globalization era underpinned more 
by technology breakthroughs and supply chain resilience, and tapping into major western markets. In particular, we see early 
evidence of China's competitiveness in high-end medical imaging, treatment equipment, surgical robotics, and high-value medical 
consumables. Accordingly, we expect overseas revenue to make up 50%+ of total revenue for many China medtech companies in 
the next 3-5 years, if not earlier. Key stock calls include United Imaging, Mindray, Edge Medical, MicroPort and Angelalign. 
In drug development and manufacturing, China CDMO and API companies are already playing the role of global manufacturing 
powerhouse. For major CDMO and API names,  overseas revenue contribution has well surpassed that of China already. Similar to 
medical devices, the competitiveness of Chinese companies'  is no longer defined solely by cost advantages, but  value generated 
through efficiency and innovation. This trend is apparent in particular in the CDMO space where leading players are supporting the 
entire drug development cycle from discovery to commercial-scale manufacturing for innovative drugs developed by MNCs. We 
expect by 2030, over 30%  of US FDA approved, on-patent novel drugs will be manufactured in China. On the generic side, China 
already supplies 30% of the world's APIs by volume. Key stock calls include  WuXi AppTec, Huahai and King-friend.
Healthcare
Alexis Yan, Laurence Tam, Clinton Ng, Marco Wong
Medical devices
From net exporter to high-end challenger: China is moving up the 
value chain from manufacturing hub to global innovation competitor. 
China has evolved into one of the world's largest medical device man-
ufacturing and export bases, with overall sector exports now 
exceeding imports and the country generating a net trade surplus in 
medical devices. However, this aggregate picture masks a more 
nuanced reality: historically, the global medical device industry has 
been dominated by US and European players, and China continues to 
run trade deficits in several high-end medical technology segments, 
including high-end imaging systems, advanced IVD platforms, sur-
gical equipment and certain implantable devices.

High-end segments narrowing the gap: These gaps have narrowed 
meaningfully over the past decade as Chinese manufacturers have 
expanded beyond low-value consumables into increasingly sophisti-
cated product categories. China's medical device market has grown 
to become the world's second-largest, exceeding Rmb1.3tn in 2024, 
while exports have continued to gain global market share. Notably, 
Chinese companies have established competitive positions in 
high-end imaging equipment, such as CT, MRI and ultrasound, as well 
as IVD platforms, surgical robotics and clear aligners, supported by 
continuous investment in R&D, engineering talent and manufac-
turing scale. Leading domestic players such as United Imaging, 
Mindray, New Industries, MicroPort MedBot and Angelalign are 
increasingly competing against established multinational incum-
bents both domestically and overseas, reflecting China's growing 
role in global medical technology innovation.
ment, IVD platforms, surgical robotics and clear aligners. At the same 
time, AI is becoming increasingly embedded across the medical 
device value chain, from product design and image reconstruction to 
clinical decision support, robotic-assisted surgery and smart diagnos-
tics (see our China Healthcare: CMEF 2026 –  Highlights and 
Takeaways [14 Apr 2026]). Leveraging large domestic datasets, verti-
cally integrated manufacturing capabilities and growing engineering 
expertise, Chinese device companies are increasingly positioned to 
capture a larger share of global high-end medical technology mar-
kets over the coming decade.
API 
China’s API competitiveness is evolving from scale efficiencies to 
innovation-driven value creation: China has long been a corner-
stone of the global API industry, in particular the commoditized APIs 
such as antibiotics, vitamins and heparins.  The country accounted  for 
30% of global API production volume (note), supported by its cost 
advantages,  comprehensive supply chain and historical outsourcing 
of API production by foreign drug makers to lower-income countries. 
We expect market share to increase in the  next phase of develop-
ment, driven by vertical integration into higher-value segments, 
including specialty APIs, formulations, CDMO services and innova-
tive  drug development. These businesses require deeper R&D capa-
bilities across drug discovery, complex chemical synthesis, and 
process development.  Sector-level data suggests this transition is 
underway, with formulation revenue mix rising from 26.5% in 2023 
to 29.3% in 2025 (note), while  capex in 2025 was broadly flat vs. 
2024 after two years of contraction post-Covid, suggesting peers are 
moving away from lower-value API manufacturing. Meanwhile, R&D 
spending has increased over the past five years, underscoring con-
tinued investment in manufacturing technology and product 
upgrading. Looking forward,  commoditized APIs should remain the 
core base, while leading players will move up the value chain and cap-
ture incremental global share in higher-value segments.

Industry 5.0 and localized supply chains as the next catalyst: 
Industry 5.0 is likely to accelerate this transition through the integra-
tion of AI, advanced automation and increasingly localized supply 
chains. Over the past decade, MNC leaders such as GE HealthCare 
(GEHC.O), Philips (PHIA.O) and Siemens Healthineers (SHL.O) have 
expanded manufacturing footprints and supplier ecosystems in 
China under "China-for-China" strategies, fostering the development 
of a comprehensive domestic supply chain spanning precision com-
ponents, imaging detectors, sensors, automation systems and 
advanced materials. As more core technologies and components 
become locally produced, Chinese manufacturers are increasingly 
internalizing critical parts and production processes, improving 
quality control, shortening development cycles and enhancing cost 
competitiveness.
AI adoption to reinforce global competitiveness: This strength-
ening domestic ecosystem has helped narrow historical technology 
gaps and supported export growth in higher-end segments where 
China has traditionally relied on imports, including imaging equip-

CDMO  
China has become a core engine for global innovative drug devel-
opment: Amid rising global R&D spending and increasing complexity 
in drug development, we see the outsourcing market expanding at a 
faster pace, with China among the largest beneficiaries.  MNCs/
biopharmas are more inclined in selecting  Chinese CDMOs such as 
the WuXi family, Asymchem and Pharmaron as preferred partners, 
supported by their proven execution track record, expertise in novel 
modalities, cost efficiency, quality and  capacity. In 2025, these 
players generated at least two-thirds of their revenue from overseas 
markets.  This trend is particularly evident in innovative small mole-
cules, where drug design and manufacturing have become increas-
ingly complex over the past five years, reflected in heavier molecular 
weights, larger compounds and longer synthesis routes (note). Over 
the past seven years, WuXi AppTec achieved an average win rate of 
~30% in small-molecule commercial manufacturing contracts for 
recently approved drugs, underscoring the rising relevance of 
Chinese CDMOs in global innovative competition. We expect  over 
30%  of US FDA approved, on-patent novel drugs will be manufac-
tured in China by 2030.
Capacity and supply chain diversification matter in the next phase 
of development: When drug projects progress into late-stage and 
commercial manufacturing, capacity needs can rise exponentially 
(from µg to kg), making scalable and GMP compliant supply a key 
criterion for MNC outsourcing decisions. Currently, we see particular 
tightness in global small molecule capacity, while demand for bio-
logics continues to ramp. On a sector level, capex spending has begun 
to recover in 2025 after the post-Covid pullback, with leading 
Chinese CDMOs guiding for faster capex deployment in 2026. This is 
a sign of stronger MNC outsourcing propensity and staying ahead of 
the next wave of new drug launches and potential blockbusters. 
Meanwhile, AI has been an enabler across the drug development 
cycle and  manufacturing process. For example, WuXi Apptec is lever-
aging AI  on top of its automated HTS, virtual screening, CADD and 
DMPK platforms to accelerate hit identification, lead optimization, 
and early screening of weaker candidates. On manufacturing, it is 
using both AI and automation to improve route design, process con-
trol, impurity prediction, deviation detection and batch consistency, 
supporting higher manufacturing quality and faster CMC execution 
(note). Lastly, a majority of capex is now going to overseas markets 
as customer preferences shift towards a more regionalized model 
amid geopolitical instability.

Home Appliances  
Lillian Lou, Hildy Ling, Carlos Liu
Leadership in cost and production efficiency: Scale manufacturing, 
product reliability and cost efficiency are key elements in home appli-
ance companies' core competencies. China's home appliance manu-
facturers have demonstrated their strong cost control and operating 
efficiency advantages vs. global peers in terms of margin perfor-
mance. We believe continued AI development and application across 
the industry could lead to further productivity gains and eventually 
support capital returns. We see three areas where AI can drive effi-
ciency improvements: 
• Manufacturing: Adoption of robotics and AI automation 
manufacturing processes will shorten lead times and 
improve quality consistency, leading to: 1) lower unit labor 
costs; 2) lower defect rates; and 3) higher throughput. Midea 
targets 0.3ppt savings from its manufacturing expenses ratio 
in 2026 by leveraging its new tech adopted in the manufac-
turing process.  
• Supply chain: AI- and data-driven supply chain management 
with more accurate and real-time forecasting and procure-

Overseas expansion  – from cost-led exporters to tech-led global 
operators: In the past, cost advantage and operating efficiency were 
major factors that contributed to home appliance exports from

(read more details in Midea's 2026 Investor Day).

Local-for-local: Instead of exporting Chinese-made products, Midea 
and Haier are shifting to a local-for-local strategy, where products are 
designed, manufactured, and sold within the same regional market. 
For example: Haier's GE Appliances in the US designs refrigerators 
specifically for American kitchens rather than importing Chinese 
models. Midea's factories in India manufacture air conditioners 
designed for India's climate and price points.
Haier has adopted a  “global 3-in-1" (R&D + manufacturing + mar-
keting) integrated localization model with distributed local centers 
across regions, and owns over 60 overseas factories by 2025. For 
Midea, it has built “2+4+N” globalized R&D networks, namely two 
Global Innovation Centers in Shanghai and Foshan, four key global 
R&D centers in the US, Germany, Italy, and Japan, along with other 
overseas local R&D centers. Midea held 29 overseas R&D centers and 
43 overseas production centers as of 2025.

For the quick evolving  RVCs category, we believe global expansion 
has been, and will remain, the key growth driver for Chinese cleaning-
appliance companies. We expect the global ex-China RVC market to 
grow at a 20% CAGR over 2025-28, outpacing China’s 10% given  low 
ownership rates. Outside of North America and Western Europe, 
household ownership of RVCs is still estimated at below 2.5% in 
2025. The ex-China RVC market is estimated at US$8.9bn in 2025, 
equivalent to 3x the size of China. By 2028, we expect it to reach 
US$15.6bn, or 4x China’s market size. Chinese companies are leading 
the RVC market because they have turned the category from a 
basic vacuuming device into a smart home-service robot. Product 
innovation has accelerated from suction power and LiDAR naviga-
tion to AI obstacle avoidance, robotic arms, edge mopping, hot-water 
mop washing, self-cleaning docks and multi-surface automation.

Stock implications: We prefer Midea (OW, 000333.SZ/0300.HK) 
the  most as the key beneficiary of AI breakthroughs and one of the 
pioneers in terms of China home appliance manufacturers expanding 
into the global market, supported by its appliance scale, industrial 
automation, smart-home ecosystem development and manufac-
turing AI capabilities. Overseas markets are Midea's strategic long-
term growth driver for its home appliance segment, with the majority 
of capex in the home appliance segment being allocated to overseas 
capacity, we estimate. Its trading multiple of  12.9x 2027e P/E is lower 
than its average  of 13.6x  since 2017 (A share), and also lower than the 
global home appliances peer average of 14x, which looks attractive 
given an 8% 2025-28 earnings CAGR  and 5-6%  dividend yield during 
2027-28.
We also like Roborock (OW, 688169.SH), the leading smart cleaning 
appliances company in China and globally, with 30% market share in 
the global RVC market in 2025. Roborock's R&D and focus on tech-
product innovation should ensure it remains a leading RVC brand 
globally. It was the first company to launch an RVC with a mechanical 
arm to the mass market, showcasing its R&D and manufacturing 
capability. Its strong brand power supports expansion in its product 
categories and ecosystem building. We view the current valuation of 
17x 2026 P/E and 12.5x 2027E P/E, and an EPS CAGR of 43% over 
2025-27, as compelling.

 Key enabler and beneficiary of Industry 5.0
China's financial system has undergone continued reform, reshaping 
the system to provide sustained support for China's unique decen-
tralized industrial supply chains. Long investment horizons, more vis-
ible cycles, the wide divergence between company and sector ROEs, 
and the large numbers of SMEs needed to support industrial supply 
chains  that cover all industrial categories are key challenges in 
funding their sustainable development. Through balancing the 
financing need between the service  and industrial sectors, deflating 
the housing bubble, establishing a proper mix of government sup-
portive funds, private PE/PCs, an IPO market tilted towards hard tech 
firms and a more sustainable banking system, China's financial 
system has resolved many of the challenges of recent years, and is 
ready to support continued industrial upgrades and expansion for 
years to come, in our view. In addition, we think enhanced financing 
models for infrastructure investments will continue to drive a posi-
tive loop between infra investments and industrial upgrades. 
At the same time, we believe Industry 5.0 and ongoing industrial 
upgrades and investments will provide healthy and sustainable 
credit demand for the financial system, and support financial asset 
yield and healthy returns for financial firms in China. We expect the 
additional industrial investments under Industry 5.0 to support an 
additional 1ppt of loan growth in China over the next 10 years, under-
lining our view that China's financial system has returned to a positive 
development loop, which could support an 80bps rebound in 
average financial asset yields in China over the next three years, vs. 
our current estimate of 50bps.  
Financials 
Richard Xu, CFA, Chiyao Huang, Beryl Yang
China's financial system is a key enabler of 
industrial upgrades
1. China has built a strong mix of various funding 
channels for sustained industrial upgrades through 
proper financial regulation and innovation in recent 
years
China has developed a comprehensive funding ecosystem for innova-
tion that spans bank credit, capital markets, PE/VC, and government 
funding. Each plays a distinct role:
Banks are better positioned to service credit demand along the 
supply chain and scale up verified technologies. High-end manufac-
turing could be increasingly chased by PE given potentially large 
upside, and registration-based IPO could also make it easier for 
market leaders to tap into the secondary equity market. Even so, the 
majority of industrial firms – many of which are mid-size or small – 
along various supply chains may still not appear interesting enough 
to the general equity market. It could therefore make it more costly 
for investment firms to identify opportunities – whereas banks, 
thanks to their wide natural reach to companies via bank accounts 
and settlements, as well as sufficient manpower, are better posi-
tioned to source such credit demand. 
In addition, the setup of AIC (Asset Investment Company) and 
improved cooperation among commercial banking, investment 
banking and asset management units may allow certain leading 
banks to gain market share in the PE/VC market, given their more 
comprehensive knowledge of specific supply chains. 
Capital market reform channels equity capital to hard tech. We 
note the trend of increased financing by manufacturing companies in 
the A-share market in the past three years, up from 45% of total 
equity market financing (IPOs and follow-on) in 2018 to 64% in 2021, 
in-line with the general and gradual shift to the manufacturing sector. 
The launch of a high tech board (STAR Market) and registration-
based IPOs have been key to channeling capital to high tech, particu-
larly hard tech sectors.

2. Funding the capillaries –  millions of SMEs on dif-
ferent supply chains
China has unique competitive advantages in its decentralized indus-
trial supply chains that cover all industrial sectors. This allows it to 
achieve quick product innovation and upgrades, more efficiency 
gains, and lower costs. We believe China's competitive advantages in 
industrial sectors have been further strengthened in recent years, 
aided by a shift in policy and resources to this area. 
better than capital markets owing to their day-to-day interactions 
with SMEs. As a result, banks provide credit to 30mn of the 120mn 
SMEs in China, with a total balance of over Rmb25tn for SME and 
small business loans.
Emerging technologies – such as blockchain, Internet of Things (IoT), 
and optical character recognition – have in recent years been helping 
financial institutions to improve efficiency and extend supply chain 
finance, likely leading to faster growth in such credit. Combined with 
many government-led public data-sharing programs, this should 
improve efficiency in serving industrial- and trade-related credit 
demand.

3. Long-term dedicated support of infrastructure 
buildout with improved project selection and 
financing models should create a positive loop 
between infra investments and industrial upgrades
Infrastructure investment has made up 30-40% of total fixed asset 
investment in China in recent years, amounting to over Rmb20tn per 
annum, as China is upgrading infrastructure such as telecom and 
high-speed railways in preparation for the next phase of growth. 
This has not only provided a cushion for job creation, household 
income and financial asset growth, but also supported revenue and 
EBIT generation in key industrial sectors, contributing to ongoing 
industrial upgrades. China's electricity production has expanded rap-
idly, surpassing the US notably in recent years, reaching close to 
10,000bn KWh – a critical enabler for energy-intensive manufac-
turing and emerging technology sectors.

Policymakers have also shifted infra investment to modern infra proj-
ects, such as data centers and smart cities, which could create a posi-
tive loop for industrial sectors – creating demand for high-end 
manufacturing products rather than just cement and steel. 
The new regime of infrastructure project selection could help 
improve credit allocation efficiency. First, our analysis shows that 
policies introduced to tighten up control over low-quality LGFV debt 
and low-quality projects financed by special local government bonds 
have not only led to slower growth in LGFV debt, but also resulted 
in debt allocation shifting to more productive projects with better 
cash flows. This has already resulted in a notable reduction in infra-
structure projects' reliance on credits. Second, the shift from  funding 
infrastructure via higher cost, less transparent LGFV debt to official 
local and central government debt has also led to a more efficient 
centralized project selection process that has eliminated many low-
return and no-return projects previously selected by city and county 
governments, which should improve credit allocation efficiency com-
pared to the previous regime where weaker regions also took on 
large LGFV debt. 
Our analysis shows that the net interest burden should remain man-
ageable despite rising debt to GDP, given the large asset base owned 
by the various levels of government and stabilization or even 
rebound in infrastructure investment ROA. We expect the govern-
ment's net interest burden to rise modestly in our base case of a 
gradual shift of fiscal resources from infrastructure to consumption, 
to 2.54% by 2030E from 2.09% in 2024. In addition, the government 
could choose to gradually privatize and securitize parts of govern-
ment SOE assets to cover some debt repayments or interest pay-
ments, if needed, which would also keep the net interest burden in 
check.

Coordinated policy support is essential for industrial upgrades
1. Policy has directed financial resources to focus back to industrial from service sectors in 2021
We believe policymakers are trying to maintain a more stable GDP contribution from the industrial sector following a decline from 32% in 2012 
to 26% in 2020, because the industrial sector can support quicker productivity gains at this stage of China's economic development. We have 
seen a coordinated policy direction to shift resources back to manufacturing sectors to preserve and improve China's highly competitive decen-
tralized industrial supply chain that is composed of millions of SMEs.

What measures have been taken, and what has been achieved? 
Policymakers have launched a series of initiatives to help channel 
more financial support into the industrial sector and supply chain, 
including: 
• the launch of a high tech board and registration-based IPO to 
channel capital to high tech sectors; 
• pushing banks to shift lending priority to industrial firms and 
SMEs; 
• heavy IT investment by banks in manufacturing and supply-
chain financing; 
• a comprehensive database of companies launched by local 
government to help bridge the information gap between 
companies and financial firms; 
• ongoing improvement in domestic and global payment pro-
cessing efficiency;
• containment of local SOE and property loans to reduce com-
petition with industrial loans; and 
• containing financial activities that are purely for trading/spec-
ulation (or financial self-circulation).
Fiscal and tax policies have also favored industrial firms in recent 
years:
• Almost the entire incremental fiscal support in 2022 has 
been allocated to local governments for VAT cuts, which will 
benefit the industrial sector more.
• Changes in the land sales revenue collection process essen-
tially should encourage local government to focus on devel-
opment of the industrial sector vs. property.
• Further increases in the tax exemption threshold for busi-
nesses also favor business formation.
Some of these policies are also designed to address the secular 
decline in the industrial workforce since 2014. The total industrial 
sector at its peak employed >100mn people in 2014, after which the 
workforce started to shrink about 4% per year, falling to 74mn in 
2021.

The effect is clear: Total Social Financing to industrial and hard tech 
rebounded to 16% in 2021 from 4% in 2017, and we expect it to reach

growth during 2015 and 2017, but rebounded strongly to 20% in both 
2020 and 2021, helped by more policy guidance, and started to out-
pace overall RMB loan growth from 2020.  This has supported a 
rebound in manufacturing FAI since 2020.

PE/VC investment has pivoted decisively to manufacturing and hard 
tech. PE/VC investment in manufacturing increased from 33% in 2017 
to 63% in 2021 by amount. The most invested areas by PE/VC in 2021 
were biotech/health care (18%), semiconductors (17%), and IT hard-
ware (16%), whereas in 2017, telecoms (16%), Internet (14%), and 
finance (9%) were more favored.
2. Central and local governments are playing a key 
role in nurturing innovation
These efforts include:
• Specialized industrial parks: A series of initiatives by various 
local governments to improve supply chain efficiency via 
more carefully planned and specialized industrial parks.
• Industrial internet, automation, and R&D investment: 
Investments in and promotion of industrial internet, automa-
tion, and R&D.
• Infrastructure shift to data centers and smart cities: A shift in 
infrastructure investment to more data centers and smart 
cities, which would create demand for high-end manufac-
turing products rather than just cement and steel.
• Setting up national and local level investment funds to invest 
in innovative companies at different stages.
• Education reform: Education reform to balance future talent 
supply for both the industrial and service sectors.
Notably,  SOE capital investment platforms – essentially PE/VC funds 
– were formed to meet the financing needs of industrial projects. 
These platforms were a direct extension of local government policy, 
designed to fill the equity financing gap for industrial and hard-tech 
companies that were too small or too early-stage for traditional bank 
credit, and private P/E and VC funds. 
While market-oriented PE/VC activities were cooling down in 2023 
and 2024 due to a sharp fall in IPOs, government-led funds  partially 
filled the gap, given they are  somewhat different than purely market-
oriented funds. The former's objectives usually go beyond purely 
financial returns, which could also include the need for investees to 
set up local production or headquarters, which could generate tax 
revenue as another form of payback.


[Page 147]
M
 BluePaper
Morgan Stanley Research
147
Example: Beijing E-Town Capital
Beijing E-Town Capital was incorporated in February 2009 as a state-owned investment and financing platform to support the 
Beijing Economic Technology Development Zone. The company mainly invests in new-generation IT, biotech and health, EV, 
robotics, and smart manufacturing via private equity funds. By the end of 2023, the company's total assets exceeded Rmb116bn, 
with 199 investment projects and total investment exceeding Rmb89.5bn. Notable investments included BOE's A-share private 
placement, the Singularity Power fund (Rmb4bn+) investing in the integrated circuit industry, and the acquisition of Nexteer from 
General Motors for US$480mn – the biggest overseas acquisition for China's auto parts industry at that time.
Example: Government investment in the creation of China's two biggest memory makers
YMTC and CXMT represent two of China's most consequential semiconductor investments, both founded in 2016 with massive 
state backing at a time when China had virtually zero domestic capability in memory chips. Government funds entered at inception, 
with the National IC Industry Investment Fund Phase I co-founding YMTC alongside Tsinghua Unigroup and Hubei provincial capital, 
while Hefei municipal government funded three-quarters of CXMT's initial investment alongside GigaDevice founder Zhu Yiming.
 National IC Industry Investment Fund Phase II subsequently invested a combined Rmb44.5bn across YMTC entities and Rmb32bn 
across CXMT entities through multiple rounds from 2020 to 2024, joined by an expanding roster of strategic investors including 
Alibaba, Tencent, Xiaomi, and all five major state bank AICs. 
After nearly a decade of cumulative losses totaling tens of billions of RMB, both companies have now reached profitability 
inflection points amid the AI-driven storage supercycle: CXMT posted Rmb24.8bn in 1Q26 net profit alone, while YMTC's 1Q26 
revenue exceeded Rmb20bn with global NAND share surpassing 10%. Today, YMTC is the world's #4–5 NAND flash producer  and 
CXMT is the #4 DRAM maker. This effectively validates the "patient capital" model of Chinese state-led industrial policy, which 
fundamentally reshaped the global memory chip landscape that was once exclusively dominated by Korean and US players.
3. Interest rates have remained reasonable to enable 
the financial system to digest risk through the cycle 
and continue to supply credit to industrial sectors
In recent years, Chinese policymakers have shifted from using 
window guidance to push down lending rates, toward maintaining 
lending rates at a reasonable level. Notably, since 2H25, regulators 
have effectively established a floor for mortgage rates and consumer 
lending rates at around 3%. In 1H26, they extended this approach to 
corporate loans, setting a minimum lending rate no lower than the 
after-tax yield of comparable government bonds.
This shift is important because the credit costs associated with sup-
porting industrial supply chains can be significant. Unlike infrastruc-
ture lending, which has maintained relatively stable credit quality 
despite persistent market concerns, industrial and trade finance 
lending have experienced notable NPL cycles. The weighted average 
manufacturing NPL ratio among banks under our coverage increased 
from 2.0% in 2012 to 6.1% in 2018, with cumulative NPL formation 
equivalent to 24% of loans between 2016 and 2021. As of 1Q26, we 
estimate that roughly 7% of outstanding industrial loans remain high 
risk.

We expect through-the-cycle NPL formation of manufacturing credit 
to range between 1.9% and 3.3% annually, according to the analysis 
above. Considering 50% final loss content (the portion of NPLs that 
can't be recovered eventually), this results in around 1% to 1.7% of 
credit cost inherited in loan pricing. Overall, we think manufacturing-
related loan rates will probably need to stay no lower than the range 
between 4.1% and 4.8% to make them economically sustainable for 
banks through the cycle.
Exhibit 259:We estimate manufacturing loan yields need to stay no lower than between 4.1% and 4.8% to make them economically sustain-
able for banks
Source: PBOC, CEIC, Company data, Morgan Stanley Research
Since 2025, the decline of ordinary corporate loan yield has notably 
slowed and stabilized at 3.5-3.6%, where manufacturing loan yield 
could be a bit higher. In the presence of rate floors, we think loan 
pricing will be more rational, offering potential for loan yield to 
increase based on different risk profiles. This should help banks to 
rebuild their provision buffer, which they have been using in the past 
several years to digest property-related risks.

How was industrial capex funded in China 
historically?
We estimate external funds have consistently 
accounted for 60-70% of annual industrial capex 
between 2020 and 2025
Bank loans, in particular mid-to-long term loans lent to industrial sec-
tors, are the single biggest funding source, representing 50% of total 
funding in 2025. The gross origination each year is based on PBOC's 
disclosed industrial loan balance and assumes an average 3-year 
tenor.  Notably, mid-to-long term industrial loans origination stayed 
at around Rmb10tn a year from 2023 to 2025.
The primary and secondary market at their peak in 2021 supplied 13% 
of industrial capex funding, which subsequently moderated to mid-
single digit levels in 2024 and 2025 following a sharp cool down in 
fundraising in A shares. Looking ahead, as capital intensity rises in the 
current innovation cycle, we see the capital market's role in funding 
innovation and industrial capex to rebound steadily, especially in 
areas like semi, high end manufacturing, robotics, etc., which sit at the 
forefront of industrial upgrades or have been identified by the gov-
ernment as technology chokepoints.
Fiscal funds made up about 10% industrial capex funding in recent 
years. These include:
• Fiscal cash subsidies: we estimate a subsidy rate based on 
reported government subsidies divided by gross profit of 
listed firms. Then we apply the ratio to industrial value add.
• Land subsidies: this is based on an IMF study where it esti-
mates the land subsidies  (measured by the discount when the 
government grants industrial land compared to market 
value) to be around 0.5% of annual GDP.
• Fiscal-led investments and others: this includes in-budget 
fiscal fund investments as well as major national industrial 
project developments backed by special government bonds 
in recent years.

Mix of fiscal funds in industrial capex, 2025

Source: CEIC, WIND, IMF, PE Data, Company data, Morgan Stanley Research
Internal funds account for 30-40% of industrial 
capex –  likely  a substantial portion of overall indus-
trial firms' annual profits
In 2025, we estimate industrial firms' own funds contributed to 
Rmb6.7tn of capex. This is likely a substantial share of overall indus-
trial firms' profits in the year: NBS reported above-scale industrial 
profits of Rmb7tn not including profits at smaller scale  firms (which 
are not in official statistics) or profits at many smaller firms along 
supply chains, such as wholesale trade, etc. 
Opportunities arise from funding China's 
Industry 5.0 
Driven by China's Industry 5.0, our industrial analysts expect capex to 
rise at a 6.9% CAGR from 2025 to 2035, reaching Rmb40.1tn by 2035. 
This will create strong demand for capital in the next 10 years to be 
served by various financial firms.
